# The maximum displacement of parking functions and the fringe subtree sizes of a uniform random forest, with three theorems on a unimodality conjecture

**Abstract.** Celano, Elder, Hadaway, Harris, Martin, Priestley and Udell conjectured that for every $n$ the numbers $b_{n,\ell}$ of parking functions of length $n$ whose maximum displacement is exactly $\ell$ form a unimodal sequence in $\ell$. We prove three statements that hold for all $n$: $b_{n,k}>b_{n,k-1}$ whenever $n\ge21k^2$; $b_{n,k}<b_{n,k-1}$ whenever $k\ge4(n-k+1)^{3/2}$; and if $b_{n,k}\le b_{n,k-1}$ at a position with $n-k\ge2$ and $k\ge(n-k)^{3/2}$, then $b_{n,k'}<b_{n,k'-1}$ for every $k'$ with $k<k'\le n-1$. Together with the exact sequences for $n\le300$, which are unimodal, the third statement shows that the conjecture follows from the single inequality $b_{n,k}\ge b_{n,k-1}$ for $n\ge301$ and $k^2<(n-k)^3$. This inequality is sufficient for the conjecture and is not claimed to be equivalent to it; it remains a conjecture, and the first statement proves it for $n\ge21k^2$. The proofs start from one exact formula: the displacements of a uniform random parking function are, as a multiset, uniform marks on the fringe subtree sizes of a uniform random rooted labeled forest, independent given the forest (a vertex whose fringe subtree has $t$ vertices carries a mark that is uniform on $\{0,\dots,t-1\}$), so that the probability that all displacements are smaller than $k$ is the expectation of $\prod_v\min(1,k/t_v)$, an exponential moment of an additive functional of a conditioned Poisson Galton–Watson tree. No new object is introduced (Section 1.3 names the source of each): the model is the coalescent of blocks of hashing with linear probing studied by Chassaing and Louchard, the forest that records which block each car closed is the decreasing tree of Guedes de Oliveira and Las Vergnas, and for each statement the paper says, from the adjacent literature read in full text, whether the statement is known, a special case is known, a different statement is known, or nothing was found. Two auxiliary theorems are stated in the form of the theorems of Berestycki and Pitman on Gibbs fragmentations: the weights on fringe subtree sizes that are compatible with the deletion of uniform edges are exactly the constant weight and the weight proportional to $1/s$; and the compositions formed by independent Borel variables conditioned on their sum can be refined one cut at a time by an explicit rule, and the rule passes through the corresponding product laws for the weights of the Borel law only, up to factors that do not change the conditioned laws. By exact computation, the sequences are not log-concave for $80\le n\le300$, and the analogous sequences for parking with spare spots fail to be unimodal, in a computation up to $600$ cars, for each number of spare spots from $4$ to $11$ and for none from $0$ to $3$; what separates the original model from these is stated as an open problem with the data.



## 1. Introduction

### 1.1 The conjecture

Let $n\ge 1$. There are $n$ parking spots in a row, numbered $1,\dots,n$, and $n$ cars, numbered $1,\dots,n$, which arrive in the order of their numbers. A preference list is a sequence $\alpha=(\alpha_1,\dots,\alpha_n)$ of integers between $1$ and $n$. Car $i$ drives to spot $\alpha_i$; it parks there if the spot is free, and otherwise it parks in the first free spot to the right of $\alpha_i$; if there is none, it fails. A preference list on which every car parks is a *parking function* of length $n$, and $\mathrm{PF}_n$ denotes the set of parking functions of length $n$. If car $i$ parks in spot $s_i$, its *displacement* is $d_i=s_i-\alpha_i\ge 0$, and the *maximum displacement* of $\alpha$ is $M(\alpha)=\max_i d_i$. The displacements depend on the order of arrival: the lists $(1,1,2)$ and $(1,2,1)$ have the same entries and maximum displacements $1$ and $2$.

For integers $n\ge1$ and $\ell\ge0$ put
$$
A_{n,\ell}=\#\{\alpha\in\mathrm{PF}_n: M(\alpha)\le \ell\},\qquad b_{n,\ell}=\#\{\alpha\in\mathrm{PF}_n: M(\alpha)=\ell\},
$$
with the conventions $A_{n,-1}=0$ and $A_{0,\ell}=1$, so that $b_{n,\ell}=A_{n,\ell}-A_{n,\ell-1}$. The maximum displacement is at most $n-1$; hence $A_{n,\ell}=|\mathrm{PF}_n|$ for $\ell\ge n-1$ and $b_{n,\ell}=0$ for $\ell\ge n$. The sequence $b_{n,0},b_{n,1},\dots,b_{n,n-1}$ is called the *row* of $n$.

> **Conjecture P** [CEHHMPU, Conjecture 6.5]. For every integer $n\ge1$ there is an integer $c\in\{0,\dots,n-1\}$ such that $b_{n,k}\le b_{n,k+1}$ for $0\le k<c$ and $b_{n,k}\ge b_{n,k+1}$ for $c\le k<n-1$.

The peak need not be a single term, and $c$ may depend on $n$. The statement is about all $n$; a result for particular $n$ or for particular kinds of lists does not settle it. The rows for $n\le 6$ are

| $n$ | $b_{n,0},\dots,b_{n,n-1}$ | sum |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | 1 | 1 |
| 2 | 2, 1 | 3 |
| 3 | 6, 7, 3 | 16 |
| 4 | 24, 51, 34, 16 | 125 |
| 5 | 120, 421, 377, 253, 125 | 1296 |
| 6 | 720, 3963, 4594, 3688, 2546, 1296 | 16807 |

(computed by running the parking process on all $n^n$ lists; Appendix B, item C1). The two ends of every row are $b_{n,0}=n!$, since $M=0$ exactly when the list is a permutation, and $b_{n,n-1}=n^{n-2}$ (Corollary 2.7), and the sum of the row is $|\mathrm{PF}_n|=(n+1)^{n-1}$ (Theorem 2.1).

For $1\le k\le n-1$ the *step* at the position $(n,k)$ is $d_{n,k}=b_{n,k}-b_{n,k-1}$.

In terms of the steps, a row is unimodal exactly when it contains no decrease followed later by an increase:

$$
\text{row } n \text{ is unimodal}\iff \text{there are no } 1\le i<j\le n-1 \text{ with } d_{n,i}<0<d_{n,j}. \tag{1.1}
$$

### 1.2 What is known about the rows

The conjecture is stated in [CEHHMPU, Conjecture 6.5], where the parking functions with $M\le\ell$ are called $\ell$-interval parking functions. That paper gives the two ends of each row, identifies the numbers $Z_n(2)$ of parking functions with all displacements at most $1$ with the Fubini numbers [CEHHMPU, Example 2.8], and states the inequalities $b_{n,0}\le b_{n,1}\le b_{n,2}$ and $b_{n,n-2}\ge b_{n,n-1}$ as consequences of its enumerative results [CEHHMPU, Section 6, before Conjecture 6.5]; of these three inequalities, $b_{n,0}\le b_{n,1}$ holds from $n=3$ on and fails for $n=2$, $b_{n,1}\le b_{n,2}$ holds from $n=6$ on and fails for $n=3,4,5$, and $b_{n,n-2}\ge b_{n,n-1}$ holds from $n=2$ on [proved, using the computed rows: Remark 5.5]. Two exact descriptions of the cumulative counts $Z_n(k)$, the numbers of parking functions with $M<k$, are in the literature: a sum over the permutations that record which car ends in which spot [CEHHMPU, Section 4.1, (6)–(8)] (Theorem 2.3 below), and a recursion in $n$ [AF, Theorem 3.7] (Proposition 2.11 below).

On the probabilistic side the maximum displacement is the subject of a theorem of Petersson for hashing with linear probing: $(n+1-M_n)/(n+1)^{2/3}$ converges in distribution to an explicit limit [Pet, Theorem 5.1] (Theorem 2.16 below). So all but an arbitrarily small fraction of the sum of a row lies at a distance of order $n^{2/3}$ from its right end [cited: this is what the convergence says]. The theorem gives the scale and the limiting distribution function of the row; it does not compare two neighbouring terms, and a sequence of distributions can converge to a unimodal limit without being unimodal [proved by an example: the laws on $\{1,\dots,n\}$ with masses proportional to $2+(-1)^j$ are not unimodal for $n\ge4$, and after division by $n$ they converge to the uniform law on $[0,1]$, whose density is constant].

For a neighbouring statistic the standard route to unimodality works: the coefficients of the generating polynomial of the total displacement of parking functions are log-concave [Pap, Theorem 3.1]. For the maximum displacement it does not: the rows are not log-concave for $80\le n\le300$ (Section 5.7).

Apart from the paper that states the conjecture, no article or preprint with a proof of the conjecture, or with a result on the monotonicity of the rows, on the position of their largest term, or on a local form of the limit law, was found. What was done to look for one is the following [cited: the record, with the queries, is Section 5 of the file `literature-adjacent.md`]. The other sources named in the bracket of Theorem 5.3 were read in full and contain no such result. Among them are the three preprints found that cite [CEHHMPU], namely [BEHLMMO], [CTT] and [FHMS], each of which cites it once and for another purpose, and a preprint on a subfamily, the $\ell$-interval Fubini rankings, which counts them for each $\ell$ and does not consider their differences in $\ell$ [ABHJM, Theorem 4.2 and Corollary 4.1]. In addition a search was made on 19 November 2026: six queries on arXiv, the lists of the works that cite [CEHHMPU], [Pet] and [AF], and keyword queries in zbMATH, OpenAlex and Crossref. About 210 records were examined, by their titles and, for the newest, by their abstracts; this part of the search rests on titles and abstracts only. Two keyword queries could not be run, and MathSciNet, Web of Science, Scopus and Google Scholar were not searched. A search of this kind shows where the statements were looked for; it does not show that they are new.

### 1.3 The results of this paper

Three theorems on the sign of the step are proved for all $n$.

- *Increase far on the left* (Theorem 5.3). If $n\ge21k^2$, then $b_{n,k}>b_{n,k-1}$.
- *Decrease far on the right* (Theorem 5.4). If $1\le k<n$ and $k\ge4(n-k+1)^{3/2}$, then $b_{n,k}<b_{n,k-1}$.
- *A decrease continues* (Theorem 5.9). If $n-k\ge2$ and $k\ge(n-k)^{3/2}$, and $b_{n,k}\le b_{n,k-1}$, then $b_{n,k'}<b_{n,k'-1}$ for every $k<k'\le n-1$.

The third theorem turns the conjecture into a statement about one side of the curve $k=(n-k)^{3/2}$, on which the deficit $n-k$ equals $k^{2/3}$, the scale of the limit law: if a row has no negative step at the positions with $k<(n-k)^{3/2}$, it is unimodal (Corollary 5.10). The rows with $n\le300$ have been computed exactly and are unimodal [computational evidence; Appendix B, item C5]. Hence the conjecture follows from the inequality $b_{n,k}\ge b_{n,k-1}$ for all $n\ge301$ and all $k$ with $k^2<(n-k)^3$, which is called Target T below and is itself a conjecture (Conjecture 5.11); the first theorem proves it for $n\ge21k^2$. There is a second route to the conjecture, which does not pass through Target T: if an explicit cross product, formed from the four terms into which two neighbouring steps are decomposed (Proposition 5.8), is positive at all positions of the first layer, $k+2\le n\le2k-1$ (Conjecture 5.12), and the rows with $n\ge15$ are non-decreasing on their left halves, then all rows are unimodal (Corollary 5.13, with the computed rows for $n\le14$, some of which do decrease on their left halves). What carries the status proved are the three theorems and the two implications, Corollaries 5.10 and 5.13, with the reduction to Target T. The hypotheses of the implications do not: Target T and the positivity of the cross product are conjectures, and that the rows with $n\ge15$ are non-decreasing on their left halves is not proved either. The proofs of the three theorems are in Appendix A, and the third uses two finite exact computations, which are named with it.

The paper also records what a proof cannot be [counterexamples, each an exact computation; Section 5.7]: the rows are not log-concave for $80\le n\le300$; a single outcome of the parking process can have a sequence that is not unimodal; and two neighbouring models that share the representation described below have rows that are not unimodal, the forest with binary offspring and the parking process with $r-1$ spare spots, the latter for each of $r=5,\dots,12$ in a computation with at most $600$ cars and for none of $r=1,\dots,4$ in that range. What separates the original model from these is not known, and is stated as an open problem with the data (Problem 6.3).

*The representation, and the four settings in which the paper works.* All proofs start from one exact formula. Let $F$ be a uniform random rooted labeled forest on $n$ vertices and $t_v$ the number of vertices of the fringe subtree of $v$, that is, of $v$ together with its descendants. Then
$$
\Pr(M<k)=\frac{Z_n(k)}{(n+1)^{n-1}}=\mathbb E\prod_{v\in F}\min\Bigl(1,\frac k{t_v}\Bigr),
$$
and more precisely the displacements of a uniform random parking function are, as a multiset, uniform marks on the fringe subtree sizes of $F$ that are independent given $F$ (Theorem 2.6 and Corollary 2.7). The paper introduces no new object: the process, its blocks, the forest and the random tree that enter this formula are all in the literature, and the four items below name the source of each [cited]. The paper is organised by the four standard settings to which its statements belong.

1. *Parking as linear probing, and the coalescent of blocks* (Section 2). On a circle the cars form blocks which merge as cars arrive; this is the model of Chassaing and Louchard [CL]. The block sizes, read from a uniformly chosen empty spot, are independent Borel variables conditioned on their sum [J01, Lemma 4.1] and form the discrete additive coalescent [CL, Sections 1.4 and 8]. The record of which block each car closed is a forest, the decreasing tree of Guedes de Oliveira and Las Vergnas [GL]; Section 2 proves that its shape has the law of the shape of a uniform forest (Theorem 2.6), which connects the block model with the bijections between parking functions and forests of [Sh] and [GL].
2. *Fringe subtrees and additive functionals of conditioned Galton–Watson trees* (Section 3). The uniform forest is a conditioned Poisson Galton–Watson tree without its root (Theorem 2.8 and the paragraph after it), and $\log\prod_v\min(1,k/t_v)$ is an additive functional with a toll that vanishes on fringe subtrees with at most $k$ vertices (Section 3.1). Section 3 counts configurations of fringe subtrees of prescribed sizes exactly (the pattern formula, Theorem 3.2), expands the threshold functional over the vertices above the threshold (Theorem 3.5), and treats the largest fringe subtrees (Propositions 3.7 and 3.8).
3. *Boltzmann models and Gibbs consistency* (Section 4). The Borel masses are the size law of the Boltzmann model of rooted labeled trees at its critical parameter, and the block sizes, in uniformly random order, have the law of the block sizes of a Gibbs partition (Section 4.1). Section 4 proves two statements in the form of the theorems of Berestycki and Pitman on Gibbs fragmentations [BP]: the weights on fringe subtree sizes that are compatible with the deletion of uniform edges are exactly the constant weight and the weight $c/s$ (Theorem 4.2), and the conditioned Borel compositions can be refined one cut at a time by a rule that passes through the product laws for Cayley's coefficients and, up to a factor $c\,\vartheta^t$ that does not change these laws, for no other weights (Theorem 4.3).
4. *The second difference* (Section 5). The step is the second difference of $k\mapsto Z_n(k)$ (Section 1.5). It is written as a positive quantity that counts pairs of vertices above the threshold minus a positive quantity that counts the vertices at the threshold (Proposition 5.1). The first two theorems compare these two quantities. The third compares, at positions where the vertices above the threshold lie on one path, the corresponding pairs of quantities of two neighbouring steps (Proposition 5.8). The language is that of log-concavity, total positivity and, for a fixed threshold, of the dominant pole of a meromorphic generating function.

*What the adjacent literature contains.* The sources of the four settings were read in full text for the statements of this paper, and the brackets of the statements give the result one by one. In summary [cited; each item is detailed at the statement named, and Appendix C is the complete table]:

- **Known.** The law of the block sizes and the additive rule by which blocks merge (Theorems 2.9 and 2.10); the forest of an outcome, with the fringe subtree of a car equal to the block it closed (Proposition 2.4(a) to (c)); the expected number of fringe subtrees of a given size, which is known for every critical offspring law with finite variance (Corollary 3.3(a)); the law of the number of parts of a composition with Cayley weights (Lemma 4.4); the log-convexity of Cayley's coefficients, in its weak form (Lemma 5.6); and, for a fixed threshold, the asymptotic form of the counts, which is an instance of the supercritical sequence schema (Proposition 5.19(b)). Two tools of Section 5.3 are known as well: the implication from the log-convexity of the weights to the lattice condition of the FKG inequality for the laws on cut sets [Lac, Proposition 3.2], which is one half of Proposition 5.7, and the criterion for total positivity of order $2$ [BrT, Proposition 2.5.1].
- **A special case is known.** The pattern formula, for one fringe subtree and for two (Theorem 3.2; exact formulas of another kind, for several fringe subtrees of prescribed shapes or of one common size in a plane tree with prescribed degrees, are [BHJ, Lemma 3.2] and [BHJ2, (5.4)]); the law of the depth of a vertex in a forest, for one tree (Proposition 3.4); the weighted count for the family with spare spots, for the weight of the total displacement (Proposition 2.13).
- **A related statement is known, and differs.** The identity (2.3), which the literature has with the vertex inversion numbers of a labeled forest in place of uniform marks [Sh, Theorem 4]; the description of the displacements as independent uniform marks, which the literature has for the sizes of the blocks in their order of arrival and not for the fringe subtree sizes of a forest [MV, Section 4.1] (Theorem 2.6(b)); Theorem 4.2, whose counterparts characterise weights on the numbers of children and not on fringe subtree sizes [Pa, Lemma 1], [BP, Corollary 3], while for each of its two weights the property itself is in the literature, for the second weight in the form given the sizes of the pieces [P99, Theorem 4], [BB, Proposition 1]; Theorem 4.3, whose counterpart concerns partitions of a set and not compositions with cuts at fixed positions [BP, Theorem 2]; the finite statements on the largest fringe subtrees, whose counterparts are limit theorems (Propositions 3.7 and 3.8); the law of the displacement of a given car, for which the literature has the law of the size of the block that receives it [MV, (4.38)] and the ingredients with two neighbouring results [J05] (Proposition 2.15); the equations for the generating function with a fixed threshold, which the literature has for the weight of the total displacement [FPV], [Kn98] (Proposition 5.16); and the reading of the blocks at earlier times from a tree, which Chassaing and Louchard have for a different tree (Proposition 2.4(d)).
- **Not found in the sources read in full**, which each bracket names. The identification of the sizes of the blocks closed by the cars with the fringe subtree sizes of a uniform random forest, and with it the formula for $\Pr(M<k)$ (Theorem 2.6(b) and (c), Corollary 2.7); the expansion of the threshold functional over the vertices above the threshold (Theorem 3.5); the rule of Theorem 4.3 on a segment, and its converse; and the three theorems on the conjecture with the formulas for the step on which they rest (Sections 5.1 to 5.6). Of the formula for $\Pr(M<k)$ the case $k=1$ is in the literature, as the expectation of the inverse product of the fringe subtree sizes [MM98, Section 5]. The joint law of Lemma 2.5 was not found either; the nearest statement, a theorem of Björner and Wachs on the labelings of a fixed forest, gives the law of the sum of the variables of the lemma and not their joint law [BW, Theorem 1.3].

*Why the classical objects appear.* Cayley's counts of trees and forests, the Borel and Borel–Tanner distributions, the tree function with Lagrange inversion, the additive coalescent, a birthday-type law for the depth of a vertex, and the exponents $3/2$ and $2/3$ occur throughout this paper. Two facts about this are proved. The formula above makes the probability that the maximum displacement is below a threshold the expectation of a functional of the fringe subtree sizes of a conditioned Galton–Watson forest with Poisson offspring [proved: Corollary 2.7 with Theorem 2.8]. And the cumulative counts depend on the fringe subtrees below the threshold, which carry no factor, only through Cayley's coefficients [proved: Theorem 3.5]. That these two facts account for each object of the list is an observation on the proofs of this paper and is not claimed as a theorem; each object enters at the statement of Sections 2 to 4 that is named for it there.

### 1.4 Statuses, literature labels and computations

Every claim of this paper carries one of six statuses, written in brackets at its head or at the place where it is made.

- **proved**: every step is in the text or in a cited source with its theorem number. Where a proof uses a finite exact computation, the bracket says so and names the script.
- **proof sketch**: the argument is given with the missing steps named. One passage of the present version carries this status: the computation of an order of magnitude after Proposition 3.8, whose result is proved in full as Proposition 3.7.
- **computational evidence**: verified by exact computation in a stated finite range, with the script named. In the few places where the computation is in floating point, the bracket says so.
- **counterexample**: an exact instance; the text says what it refutes and what it does not.
- **conjecture**: believed, with the grounds stated.
- **open**: a question, or a statement for which no grounds for believing it are offered.

A bracket that begins with the word *cited* marks a sentence that is not a claim of this paper: the sentence reports what a source states, or what the reading of the sources found, and the bracket names the place in the source or the file in which the reading is recorded. A theorem that is quoted from a source and used without a proof carries the bracket ‘proved; cited’, which means that it is proved in the source named.

A statement that is proved here may be known. Where a statement or its nearest relative was located in the literature, the bracket gives the source with its theorem, equation or page number. For the statements whose standing was in doubt, the adjacent literature was read in full text, source by source, and the bracket carries one of four labels.

- **known**: the statement is in the source named, verbatim or as an immediate special case of a theorem there under that theorem's own hypotheses.
- **special case known**: the source named states a special case; the general statement is proved here.
- **differs**: the source named states a related statement that neither contains this one nor is contained in it; the difference is stated.
- **not found in** [the sources listed]: the sources listed were read in full, or, for a book, in full in the chapters named in the bibliography, and do not contain the statement.

A label says where a statement stands in the sources that were read; it is not a claim that the statement is new, and no such claim is made anywhere in this paper. The sources were read against a fixed list of the statements of this paper; a few auxiliary statements that were not on that list carry the remark that they were not looked for, in place of a label or beside it. A source that could be read only in part is named as such in the bibliography, and a label that would need the unread part says "not verified against" that source. The following restrictions bear on the labels of this paper [cited; the bibliography and `literature-adjacent.md` give the pages]. Knuth's volume [Kn3], to which several sources trace the forest of an outcome and the hook length formula for forests, was not obtained, nor was the article of Konheim and Weiss [KW]. One further article could not be obtained although it was sought: that of Meir and Moon [MM], to which [BB] attributes the splitting property of random recursive trees. The table of statements with their statuses and labels is Appendix C; the file `literature-adjacent.md` that accompanies the paper gives, for each label, the passages on which it rests.

Computations were done in exact integer or rational arithmetic unless a place says otherwise. Appendix B lists the scripts, what each checks, the range, and the result.

### 1.5 Notation and standard facts

| symbol | meaning |
|---|---|
| $n$, $N=n+1$ | the number of cars; the number of spots on the circle of Section 2.1 |
| $p_n=(n+1)^{n-1}$ | the number of parking functions of length $n$; $p_0=1$ |
| $k=\ell+1$ | the *threshold*: "all displacements are smaller than $k$" means $M\le k-1$ |
| $Z_n(k)=A_{n,k-1}$ | the number of parking functions of length $n$ with $M<k$, for integers $k\ge1$; Section 3.1 extends $Z_n(y)$ to real $y>0$; $Z_0=1$, and $Z_n(0)=0$ for $n\ge1$ |
| $d_{n,k}=b_{n,k}-b_{n,k-1}$ | the *step* at position $(n,k)$, $1\le k\le n-1$; $d_{n,k}=Z_n(k+1)-2Z_n(k)+Z_n(k-1)$ |
| $r=n-k$ | the deficit of the position $(n,k)$; it equals $1$ at the last index $k=n-1$ of the row |
| $a_j=j^{j-1}/j!$ | Cayley's coefficient: the number of rooted labeled trees on $j$ vertices divided by $j!$ |
| $\mathrm{Bor}(j)=e^{-j}a_j$ | the mass of the Borel distribution with parameter $1$ at $j\ge1$ |
| $\mathrm{Ren}(j)=j\,\mathrm{Bor}(j)$ | $j^je^{-j}/j!$ for $j\ge 1$; $\mathrm{Ren}(0)=1$ |
| $T(z)=\sum_{j\ge1}a_jz^j$ | the tree function; $T=ze^{T}$ |
| $t_v$ | the size of the subtree of a vertex $v$ of a rooted forest: the number of vertices in $\{v\}$ together with all descendants of $v$ |
| $[x^m]f$ | the coefficient of $x^m$ in a power series $f$ |
| $(x)_j$ | the falling factorial $x(x-1)\cdots(x-j+1)$; $(x)_0=1$ |

The letter $r$ denotes the deficit $n-k$ in Section 5 and Appendix A, and the number of empty spots in the passages on parking with spare spots (Proposition 2.13 and Section 6.3), where this is said again. The letter $q$ has no fixed meaning: according to the place it is a number of trees, of blocks or of parts, a bound on the deficit $n-M$ (Section 3.4), or the variable of a $q$-analogue (Section 2.5). Letters introduced inside a proof or an appendix are local to it.

A *rooted labeled forest on $[n]$* is a graph on the vertex set $[n]=\{1,\dots,n\}$ each of whose components is a tree with a distinguished root; the children of a vertex are not ordered. There are $(n+1)^{n-1}$ of them, and $j^{j-1}$ rooted labeled trees on $j$ vertices (Cayley's formula; [FS, Section II.5.1, (46)–(47)]). It is often convenient to join all roots of a forest on $[n]$ to an additional vertex $0$, the *virtual root*; this turns the forest into a rooted tree on $\{0,1,\dots,n\}$ with root $0$, and every such tree arises once.

**Lemma 1.1** (normalisation) [proved; known: (a) [FS, (46)–(47), p. 127]; (b) Lagrange inversion in the form [FS, Theorem A.2, (14)], the count of forests [FS, Note II.18, p. 128] and [P99, (5)], where it is credited to Riordan; (c) [FS, Proposition IV.5 with p. 279, and Figure VI.9]; (d) [FS, Section II.5.3, p. 132]; the short proof is kept because the proofs of Theorem 2.8 and Lemma 3.6 refer to its steps]. For all integers $m\ge1$ and $q\ge1$:

(a) The number of rooted labeled trees on $m$ vertices is $m^{m-1}=m!\,e^{m}\,\mathrm{Bor}(m)$; the number of such trees with one marked vertex is $m^{m}=m!\,e^{m}\,\mathrm{Ren}(m)$; the number of unrooted labeled trees is $m^{m-2}=m!\,e^{m}\,\mathrm{Bor}(m)/m$; the number of rooted labeled forests on $m$ vertices is $p_m=(m+1)^{m-1}=m!\,e^{m+1}\,\mathrm{Bor}(m+1)$.

(b) $[z^m]\,T(z)^q=\dfrac qm\,\dfrac{m^{m-q}}{(m-q)!}$ for $q\le m$ (Lagrange inversion applied to $T=ze^T$). Equivalently, the number of rooted labeled forests with exactly $q$ trees on $m$ vertices is $\binom{m-1}{q-1}m^{m-q}$, and the sum of $q$ independent variables with law $\mathrm{Bor}$ has the Borel–Tanner law
$$
\mathrm{Bor}^{*q}(m)=e^{-m}[z^m]T(z)^q=\frac qm\,\frac{e^{-m}m^{m-q}}{(m-q)!}.
$$

(c) $\sum_{m\ge1}\mathrm{Bor}(m)=T(1/e)=1$.

(d) $\sum_{m\ge1}\dfrac{a_m}{m}z^m=T(z)-\dfrac{T(z)^2}{2}$; the coefficient $a_m/m=m^{m-2}/m!$ is the number of unrooted labeled trees on $m$ vertices divided by $m!$.

*Proof.* (a) is Cayley's formula together with the definitions of $\mathrm{Bor}$ and $\mathrm{Ren}$; a rooted forest on $[m]$ is a tree on $\{0,\dots,m\}$ rooted at $0$, and there are $(m+1)^{m}/(m+1)$ of those. (b) An ordered $q$-tuple of rooted trees on disjoint label sets with union $[m]$ is counted by $m!\,[z^m]T^q$, and the coefficient is given by the Lagrange inversion formula $[z^m]H(T)=\frac1m[u^{m-1}]H'(u)e^{mu}$ with $H(u)=u^q$; dividing this number by $q!$ gives the number of forests, and multiplying the coefficient $[z^m]T^q$ by $e^{-m}$ gives the convolution, since $\mathrm{Bor}(j)=e^{-j}[z^j]T$. (c) The series $T$ has non-negative coefficients and converges for $0\le x<1/e$, since $a_{m+1}/a_m=(1+1/m)^{m-1}\to e$; there $T$ is continuous and increasing, $T(0)=0$, and $T(x)e^{-T(x)}=x$. The function $u\mapsto ue^{-u}$ is an increasing bijection from $[0,1]$ onto $[0,1/e]$ and takes the value $1/e$ only at $u=1$; so $T(x)$ cannot reach $1$ for $x<1/e$, $T$ is the inverse of this bijection on $[0,1/e)$, $T(x)\to1$ as $x\to1/e$, and $T(1/e)=1$ by monotone convergence. In the language of Theorem 2.8 this says that the critical Galton–Watson tree is finite with probability $1$. (d) Differentiating $T=ze^T$ gives $T'=T/z+TT'$, that is, $T'(1-T)=T/z$; so the derivative of $T-T^2/2$ is $T/z=\sum a_mz^{m-1}$, and both sides vanish at $0$. $\square$


## 2. The model: parking as linear probing, and the coalescent of blocks

The model of this paper is hashing with linear probing, in the form in which Chassaing and Louchard study it under the name of parking [CL]: cars arrive one after another at the places of a circle, each at a uniformly chosen place, and each takes the first free place at or after the one at which it arrives. The parking functions of Section 1.1 are the case of $n$ cars and $n+1$ places, after a rotation (Theorem 2.1). At any moment the places are divided into blocks, each a run of occupied places together with the empty place that follows it, and the arrival of a car merges two neighbouring blocks. Three facts about this process are known and are the starting point of the paper: a car lands in a block with probability proportional to the size of the block, and its displacement is uniformly distributed on it [Pet, Section 2]; at each time the block sizes are independent Borel variables conditioned on their sum [J05, p. 183]; and as time runs they form the additive coalescent [CL, Sections 1.4 and 8]. The section recalls them with proofs (Sections 2.1 and 2.4), because the sources state them in the running text and in other conventions, and it adds the record of which block each car closed.

That record is a forest on the cars (Section 2.2). It is the decreasing tree that Guedes de Oliveira and Las Vergnas attach to the outcome of a parking function [GL]. The theorem of the section says that the shape of this forest, with the order of arrival forgotten, has the law of the shape of a uniform random rooted labeled forest, and that the displacements are independent uniform marks on its fringe subtree sizes (Theorem 2.6); the uniform forest is in turn a conditioned Poisson Galton–Watson tree without its root (Section 2.3). No new object is introduced: the model is that of [CL], the tree is that of [GL], and what Section 2.2 contributes is the link between the two, with its standing in the literature stated at Proposition 2.4 and at Theorem 2.6. Sections 2.5 and 2.6 collect the consequences for other statistics of the displacements, for parking with spare spots and for the displacement of one particular car, and quote the limit law of the maximum displacement.

### 2.1 The parking process on a circle

Place $N=n+1$ spots on a circle, numbered $1,\dots,N$ clockwise, with spot $1$ following spot $N$. A preference list is now an element $\alpha$ of $\{1,\dots,N\}^n$; car $i$ parks in the first free spot met when going clockwise from $\alpha_i$, starting with $\alpha_i$ itself. No car can fail, and exactly one spot stays empty. The displacement of a car on the circle is the number of clockwise steps from its preferred spot to the spot it takes.

**Theorem 2.1** (Konheim and Weiss; the argument is Pollak's) [proved; known: the argument is Pollak's and is given in [FR, Section 2, p. 13]; in the present form, with uniform preferences on a circle of $n+1$ places, it is in [CM, pp. 11–13] and [Kn98, pp. 561–562]; the count is usually attributed to [KW] (not read)]. For every $\alpha\in\{1,\dots,N\}^n$, exactly one of the $N$ lists obtained from $\alpha$ by adding a constant to all entries modulo $N$ leaves spot $N$ empty. A list leaves spot $N$ empty if and only if it is a parking function of length $n$, and then every car takes the same spot, with the same displacement, as on the row. Hence $|\mathrm{PF}_n|=(n+1)^{n-1}$, and if the preferences of the $n$ cars are independent and uniform on the circle, then, after the rotation that moves the empty spot to $N$, the list is uniformly distributed on $\mathrm{PF}_n$.

*Proof.* Adding $1$ to every preference rotates the whole process by one spot, so the empty spot runs through all $N$ spots as the constant varies; this gives the first assertion. If spot $N$ stays empty, no car preferred it (the car would have taken it) and no car passed it (a car passes only occupied spots), so all preferences lie in $[n]$ and the process never leaves the row $1,\dots,n$: the list is a parking function and the two processes coincide. Conversely a parking function fills $1,\dots,n$ on the row and therefore leaves $N$ empty on the circle. The $N^n$ lists fall into classes of $N$ rotations, each containing one parking function, which gives the count and the statement about the uniform law. $\square$

The rotation changes neither the displacements nor anything else that is defined by the relative positions of cars and empty spots. Statements about such quantities can therefore be made and proved on the circle with independent uniform preferences, and they hold for uniform parking functions.

At a moment when some cars have parked, call a *block* a maximal run of consecutive occupied spots together with the empty spot that follows it clockwise; a single empty spot preceded by an empty spot is a block of size $1$. The blocks partition the circle, their sizes add up to $N$, and their number is the number of empty spots.

**Lemma 2.2** (one step) [proved; known: both assertions are in [Pet, Section 2, pp. 457–458], in the running text; the second is also [J05, (4), p. 181], and, for the last car, [FPV, p. 495] and [Kn98, p. 563]]. Suppose the blocks are $B_1,\dots,B_q$ with $q\ge2$, and a car arrives whose preference is uniform on the $N$ spots. Then its preferred spot lies in $B_i$ with probability $|B_i|/N$; in that case the car parks in the empty spot at the end of $B_i$, its displacement is uniformly distributed on $\{0,1,\dots,|B_i|-1\}$, and after it has parked $B_i$ and the block that follows $B_i$ clockwise form one block.

*Proof.* A car whose preference is a spot of $B_i$ moves clockwise through occupied spots until it reaches the empty spot of $B_i$; the $|B_i|$ spots of the block correspond to the displacements $|B_i|-1,\dots,1,0$. Once that spot is filled, the run of $B_i$ continues into the run of the next block. $\square$

We say that this car *closes* the block $B_i$: it fills the empty spot that ends it. So the parking process is a process of merging blocks: it starts from $N$ blocks of size $1$ and ends, after $n$ cars, with one block of size $N$. The law of a car's displacement is decided at the moment of its arrival by the block it lands in, and by nothing else: given that block, the displacement is uniform on it.

### 2.2 Outcomes, closed blocks and the history forest

We now record which blocks were merged by which cars. Let $\alpha\in\mathrm{PF}_n$. The *outcome* of $\alpha$ is the permutation $\sigma=(\sigma_1,\dots,\sigma_n)$ in which $\sigma_i$ is the number of the car parked in spot $i$. For a permutation $\sigma$ and a spot $i$ define the *capacity*
$$
h_i=h_i(\sigma)=i-\max\bigl(\{j<i:\ \sigma_j>\sigma_i\}\cup\{0\}\bigr).
$$

**Theorem 2.3** (the parking functions with a given outcome) [proved; known: with the threshold, [CEHHMPU, Section 4.1, (6)–(8)]; the description of the lists with a given outcome and the product without threshold, [GL, Lemma 2.5]; the case without threshold is attributed in [CEHHMPU] to [KW] (not read)]. Let $\sigma$ be a permutation of $[n]$. In every parking function with outcome $\sigma$, when the car $\sigma_i$ arrives, the spots $i-h_i+1,\dots,i-1$ are occupied and the spot $i-h_i$ (if $i-h_i\ge1$) is empty; thus $h_i$ is the size of the block which that car closes. The parking functions with outcome $\sigma$ are exactly the lists in which, for every $i$, the car $\sigma_i$ prefers one of the spots $i-h_i+1,\dots,i$. Consequently they correspond bijectively to the vectors of displacements $(\delta_1,\dots,\delta_n)$ with $0\le\delta_i\le h_i-1$, where $\delta_i$ is the displacement of the car parked in spot $i$, and for every $k\ge1$
$$
Z_n(k)=\sum_{\sigma\in S_n}\ \prod_{i=1}^n\min(h_i(\sigma),k). \tag{2.1}
$$

*Proof.* Fix $\sigma$ and a spot $i$, and put $z=i-h_i$. The cars that have arrived before $\sigma_i$ are those with smaller numbers, so at the arrival of $\sigma_i$ the occupied spots are $\{j:\sigma_j<\sigma_i\}$. By the definition of $h_i$ the spots $z+1,\dots,i-1$ hold cars with smaller numbers and are occupied, while $z$, if it is a spot, holds a car with a larger number and is empty; spot $i$ is empty. If $\alpha$ has outcome $\sigma$, the car $\sigma_i$ cannot have preferred a spot $\le z$, since it would have stopped at $z$ or earlier, nor a spot $>i$; so its preference is in $\{z+1,\dots,i\}$. Conversely, choose for each $i$ a preference in that range and let the cars arrive; by induction on the number of the car, when $\sigma_i$ arrives the cars with smaller numbers stand where $\sigma$ puts them, so the spots from its preference up to $i-1$ are occupied, spot $i$ is empty, and the car parks in $i$. The choices for different spots do not constrain each other. Finally $M<k$ means $\delta_i\le k-1$ for all $i$, which leaves $\min(h_i,k)$ choices at spot $i$. $\square$

The capacities of a permutation are the sizes of the blocks closed during the process, and these blocks are nested or disjoint. The forest that records the nesting is the history of the process.

**Definition** (history forest). For a permutation $\sigma$ of $[n]$, the *history forest* $\Psi(\sigma)$ is the rooted forest on the set of spots $[n]$ in which the parent of spot $i$ is the smallest $j>i$ with $\sigma_j>\sigma_i$, and $i$ is a root if there is no such $j$. Each spot $i$ is labeled by its car $\sigma_i$.

The parent of $i$, when $i$ has one, is the first spot to the right of $i$ that is empty when the car $\sigma_i$ arrives, that is, the empty spot at the end of the block which this car has just enlarged; the car that later fills this spot closes a block containing $i$. A spot $i$ is a root when every spot to its right is already occupied at that moment; on the circle the empty spot at the end of its block is then the spot $N$, which corresponds to the virtual root.

**Proposition 2.4** (the history forest) [proved; parts (a) to (c) known: [GL, Lemma 3.2, and Lemma 3.3 with its proof]; the same forest, indexed by the cars, is the auxiliary forest of [KY, Section 2.4], which that paper refers to a construction of Knuth [Kn3]; part (d): differs: [CL, Section 8] obtains the forests of the earlier times by erasing, in the reverse order of arrival, the edges of a different tree, built by a breadth-first correspondence (Section 2.4); for the present forest, (d) was not found in [GL], [Sh], [KY], [CM], [WY], [FPV], [Pet], [J01], [J05], [Kn98], [CL], [MV], which were read in full; not verified against [Kn3]]. Let $\sigma$ be a permutation of $[n]$.

(a) The subtree of spot $i$ in $\Psi(\sigma)$ consists of the spots $i-h_i+1,\dots,i$. In particular $t_i=h_i$: the size of the subtree of a car is the size of the block the car closed.

(b) Every spot carries a larger label than each of its children: a car arrives after all the other cars in its subtree. Call a forest on the label set $[n]$ with this property *decreasing*.

(c) The map $\sigma\mapsto\Psi(\sigma)$, with the spots replaced by their labels, is a bijection from the permutations of $[n]$ onto the decreasing forests on $[n]$.

(d) Join the roots of $\Psi(\sigma)$ to a virtual root placed at the spot $N=n+1$ of the circle. For $0\le t\le n$, delete the edges that join the spots of the cars $t+1,\dots,n$ to their parents. The trees of the resulting forest are the blocks of the parking process after $t$ cars: each tree consists of an empty spot, which is its root, and of the run of occupied spots that precedes it.

*Proof.* (a) Write $W_i=\{i-h_i+1,\dots,i\}$. If $j\in W_i$ and $j\ne i$, then $\sigma_j<\sigma_i$, and the first spot to the right of $j$ with a larger label is at most $i$; so the parent of $j$ lies in $W_i$, and following parents from $j$ one stays in $W_i$ and moves to the right until one reaches $i$. Hence $W_i$ is contained in the subtree of $i$. Conversely, if $i$ is the parent of $j$, then all spots strictly between $j$ and $i$ carry labels smaller than $\sigma_j<\sigma_i$, so $j\in W_i$ and moreover $W_j\subseteq W_i$, because a spot to the left of $j$ that lies in $W_j$ carries a label smaller than $\sigma_j$. By induction along the path, every descendant of $i$ lies in $W_i$.

(b) holds by the definition of the parent.

(c) Let $i_1<\dots<i_m$ be the children of a spot $j$, or the roots. Then $\sigma_{i_1}>\dots>\sigma_{i_m}$, since if $\sigma_{i_a}<\sigma_{i_b}$ for some $a<b$, the parent of $i_a$ would be a spot at most $i_b$, and $i_a$ would be neither a child of $j$ nor a root. By (a) the subtrees occupy consecutive intervals, each ending at its root. Thus $\sigma$ is recovered from the labeled forest: order the roots, and the children of every vertex, by decreasing label from left to right, and place the vertices on the spots $1,\dots,n$ in postorder (every subtree before its root). Conversely, starting from any decreasing forest, this procedure produces a permutation $\sigma$, and $\Psi(\sigma)$ is the given forest: in postorder, the vertices that come after a vertex $v$ and before its parent belong to the subtrees of later siblings of $v$, whose labels are smaller than the label of $v$, so the first larger label to the right of $v$ is that of its parent, and a root has no larger label to its right.

(d) After $t$ cars the occupied spots are those with $\sigma_i\le t$. Let $e$ be an empty spot (a spot with $\sigma_e>t$, or the spot $N$) and let $i$ be a spot of the run of occupied spots that precedes it. The parent of $i$ is the first spot to the right of $i$ whose car is later than $\sigma_i$; it lies in the run or is $e$, because $e$ carries a later car than every spot of the run (or is the virtual root). Following parents from $i$ one therefore reaches $e$ without leaving the run. Since the edges above the empty spots are deleted and no others, the tree of $e$ in the resulting forest is $e$ together with its run. $\square$

Part (d) is the link between the forest and the process in time: removing the cars in the reverse order of their arrival takes the history forest apart into the blocks of the earlier moments. The edges are removed here in an order determined by the arrivals, from the top of the forest downwards, and not in uniform random order.

The decreasing forests are a small part of all labeled forests, and a uniform parking function does not choose its outcome uniformly: by Theorem 2.3 the outcome $\sigma$ has probability $\prod_i h_i(\sigma)/p_n$. The next lemma is the classical count of decreasing labelings, and the theorem after it shows that the two effects cancel exactly.

**Lemma 2.5** (hook length formula for forests) [proved; the last assertion is the hook length formula for forests, known: [GS, Lemma 2.1], credited there to [Kn3, Exercise 20, p. 70] (not read), and [BW, p. 167], which quotes it from the same page of [Kn3]; the same lemma of [GS] gives the mutual independence of the events $\iota_v=0$; the law of the sum of the numbers $\iota_v$, which follows from the joint law, is known: [BW, Theorem 1.3]; the joint law: not found in [Sh], [GL], [WY], [KY], [CM], [J05], [Kn98], [MV], [MM98], [BW], which were read in full (in [BW] the proof of that theorem for this sum is an induction with the two steps of the proof of this lemma [BW, pp. 180–181], and the article states the result for the sum only); not verified against [GS] outside its second and tenth sections, which were read and do not contain it]. Fix a rooted forest with $n$ vertices and label its vertices by a uniformly random bijection onto $[n]$. For a vertex $v$ let $\iota_v$ be the number of vertices in the subtree of $v$, other than $v$, whose label is larger than the label of $v$. Then the variables $\iota_v$ are independent, and $\iota_v$ is uniformly distributed on $\{0,\dots,t_v-1\}$. In particular the probability that the labeling is decreasing, that is, that $\iota_v=0$ for every $v$, is $\prod_v 1/t_v$.

*Proof.* Consider first a tree with $t$ vertices and root $x$. The label of the root is uniformly distributed among the $t$ labels, so $\iota_x$ is uniform on $\{0,\dots,t-1\}$. Given the label of the root and the sets of labels that fall into the subtrees of the children of the root, the labelings of these subtrees are independent and uniform, and for a vertex $v$ of one of these subtrees $\iota_v$ depends only on the relative order of the labels inside that subtree. By induction on the number of vertices, the variables $\iota_v$ of each of these subtrees are independent and uniform on their ranges, and their joint law does not depend on the conditioning; hence all the variables $\iota_v$ of the tree are independent. For a forest, the labelings of the components are independent and uniform given their label sets, and the same argument applies. $\square$

**Theorem 2.6** (the law of the history forest) [proved; (2.2) and (a): differs: [Sh, Theorem 4] gives the left side of (2.3) as the sum over the labeled forests of the product of $w$ at the vertex inversion numbers, and (2.3) follows from it by Lemma 2.5; the bijections of [Sh, Lemma 2 and Theorem 3], [GL, Theorem 3.4 and Remark 3.5] and [KY, Section 2.4] carry the displacement of a car to the number of inversions at a vertex; (b) and (c): differs: [MV, Section 4.1 with Note 1, p. 38] states that, given the sizes of the components that receive the cars, the displacements are independent and uniform; the identification of these sizes with the fringe subtree sizes of a uniform forest was not found in [MV], [Sh], [GL], [KY], [CM], [WY], [FPV], [Kn98], [J01], [J05], [Pet], [CEHHMPU], [CL], which were read in full (in [CL] the displacement of a car does not occur); not verified against [Kn3]]. Let $f$ be a function on the positive integers with values in a commutative ring containing the rational numbers. Then
$$
\sum_{\sigma\in S_n}\ \prod_{i=1}^n f(h_i(\sigma))\;=\;\sum_{F}\ \prod_{v\in F}\frac{f(t_v)}{t_v}, \tag{2.2}
$$
where $F$ runs through the rooted labeled forests on $[n]$. Consequently:

(a) For every function $w$ on the non-negative integers with values in such a ring,
$$
\sum_{\alpha\in\mathrm{PF}_n}\ \prod_{i=1}^n w(d_i)\;=\;\sum_{F}\ \prod_{v\in F}\ \frac1{t_v}\sum_{d=0}^{t_v-1}w(d). \tag{2.3}
$$

(b) Let $\alpha$ be a uniform random parking function of length $n$. Let $F$ be a uniform random rooted labeled forest on $[n]$ and, given $F$, let $(D_v)_{v\in F}$ be independent with $D_v$ uniform on $\{0,\dots,t_v-1\}$. Then the multiset of pairs (size of the block closed by a car, displacement of that car) of $\alpha$ has the same law as the multiset $\{(t_v,D_v):v\in F\}$.

(c) For every $k\ge1$ and every shape $S$ of a rooted forest with $n$ vertices, the number of parking functions with $M<k$ whose history forest has the shape $S$ is $\frac{n!}{|\mathrm{Aut}(S)|}\prod_{v\in S}\min(1,k/t_v)$, that is, the number of labeled forests of shape $S$ times the probability, given a forest of that shape, that all the marks $D_v$ of (b) are smaller than $k$. In particular the shape of the history forest of a uniform parking function has the law of the shape of a uniform random rooted labeled forest.

*Proof.* By Proposition 2.4 the left side of (2.2) equals $\sum_\Delta\prod_{v}f(t_v)$ over the decreasing forests $\Delta$ on $[n]$. Group the labeled forests by their shape, that is, by the isomorphism class of the underlying unlabeled rooted forest. The product $\prod_v f(t_v)$ depends only on the shape. A shape $S$ with automorphism group of order $|\mathrm{Aut}(S)|$ carries $n!/|\mathrm{Aut}(S)|$ labeled forests, and by Lemma 2.5 the fraction $\prod_v 1/t_v$ of them is decreasing, because the property of being decreasing is invariant under automorphisms. Hence both sides of (2.2) equal $\sum_S \frac{n!}{|\mathrm{Aut}(S)|}\prod_{v\in S}\frac{f(t_v)}{t_v}$.

(a) By Theorem 2.3, the sum over the parking functions with outcome $\sigma$ of $\prod_i w(d_i)$ is $\prod_i\sum_{d<h_i}w(d)$. Summing over $\sigma$ and applying (2.2) with $f(t)=\sum_{d<t}w(d)$ gives (2.3).

(b) Apply the same argument with $f(t)=\sum_{d<t}x_{t,d}$, where the $x_{t,d}$ are independent indeterminates. The left side becomes the generating polynomial of the multiset of pairs over all parking functions, and the right side is $p_n$ times the expectation of $\prod_v x_{t_v,D_v}$. A random finite multiset is determined in law by this expectation.

(c) This is what the proof of (2.2) gives shape by shape, with $f(t)=\min(t,k)$: the parking functions with outcome $\sigma$ and $M<k$ number $\prod_i\min(h_i,k)$ by Theorem 2.3, and the decreasing forests of shape $S$ number $\frac{n!}{|\mathrm{Aut}(S)|}\prod_v1/t_v$. $\square$

Thus the shape of the history of the parking process, which is the history with the order of arrival forgotten, is distributed as the shape of a uniform random rooted labeled forest; and this forest, together with independent uniform marks on its subtree sizes, gives the law of the displacements as a multiset. Two cautions belong here. The labels of the uniform forest are not the numbers of the cars; the history forest itself is decreasing and is far from uniform among labeled forests. And the statement concerns the multiset of displacements: which car has which displacement is not recorded by the uniform forest.

**Corollary 2.7** (the maximum displacement) [proved; special case known: for $k=1$ the right side of (2.4), $\mathbb E\prod_v1/t_v=n!/p_n$, is in [MM98, Section 5] in the form $n!/n^n$ for the tree with its root (Section 3.1), and the left side is the proportion of permutations; for $k\ge2$ not found in [CEHHMPU], [Pet], [MV], [Sh], [GL], [KY], [CM], [WY], [FPV], [Kn98], [J01], [J05], [CL], which were read in full: none of them states (2.4) or describes the law of $M$ as that of $\max_vD_v$; the nearest statement is the fragmentation of an almost full table in [Pet, p. 458]; the value $b_{n,n-1}=n^{n-2}$ is known: [CEHHMPU, Example 2.8]]. With $F$ and $(D_v)$ as in Theorem 2.6(b), $M(\alpha)$ has the law of $\max_v D_v$, and for every integer $k\ge1$
$$
\frac{Z_n(k)}{p_n}=\Pr(M<k)=\mathbb E\prod_{v\in F}\min\Bigl(1,\frac{k}{t_v}\Bigr). \tag{2.4}
$$
Equivalently $D_v=\lfloor U_vt_v\rfloor$ with independent uniform variables $U_v$ on $[0,1)$. In particular $b_{n,n-1}=n^{n-2}$: the maximum equals $n-1$ exactly when the forest is a single tree, which happens for $n^{n-1}$ of the $p_n$ forests, and the mark of its root equals $n-1$, which has probability $1/n$.

*Proof.* By Theorem 2.6(b) the multiset of the displacements of $\alpha$ has the law of the multiset $\{D_v\}$, so $M(\alpha)$ has the law of $\max_vD_v$. The first equality of (2.4) is the definition of $Z_n(k)$, with $|\mathrm{PF}_n|=p_n$ (Theorem 2.1). Given $F$, the events $D_v<k$ are independent and have the probabilities $\min(1,k/t_v)$, since $D_v$ is uniform on $\{0,\dots,t_v-1\}$; taking the expectation over $F$ gives the second equality. The variable $\lfloor U_vt_v\rfloor$ is uniform on $\{0,\dots,t_v-1\}$, so the marks can be constructed in this way. For the value of $b_{n,n-1}$, a mark is at most $t_v-1$, and $t_v=n$ holds only at the root of a forest that is a single tree; the count is made in the statement. $\square$

In the language of the process, (2.4) says that the probability of the event $M<k$ is the expectation of the product, over the cars, of the probability $\min(1,k/h)$ that a car landing in a block of size $h$ has displacement smaller than $k$ (Lemma 2.2). The threshold $k$ acts on each car separately, and only through the size of the block the car closes.

*The forest and the displacements in the literature* [cited; the labels are those in the brackets of Proposition 2.4, Lemma 2.5, Theorem 2.6 and Corollary 2.7]. The tree is known, and so is its relation to the displacements at the level of bijections. Guedes de Oliveira and Las Vergnas attach to the outcome of a parking function the tree in which each car is joined to the next greater car on its right, and prove that the number of values available to the displacement of a car is the number of its descendants plus one [GL, Lemma 2.5 and Lemma 3.3]; they call this the crux of their construction and say that it is implicit in Shin's paper [GL, p. 5]. Kenyon and Yin use the same tree, with the rule that the displacement of a car plus one is the rank of a label within the subtree of its vertex, and refer it to a construction of Knuth [KY, Section 2.4]. Shin's bijection between rooted labeled forests and parking functions carries the number of inversions at each vertex to the displacement of the corresponding car, so that the displacements of the parking functions and the vertex inversion numbers of the labeled forests have the same joint generating function [Sh, Theorem 4]; for the totals this is the theorem of Kreweras (Section 2.5), and Knuth refers a bijection with this property to an exercise of his book [Kn98, p. 567; Kn3, not read]. On the side of the process, Petersson describes the fragmentation of an almost full table in which the displacement of each removed item is uniform on its left block [Pet, p. 458], and Flajolet, Poblete and Viola decompose a table at its last key, whose displacement takes every value between $0$ and the number of keys on its left [FPV, p. 495]; neither builds a tree from this. The statement nearest to Theorem 2.6(b) is in a recent preprint of Marckert and Varin on parking on a circle with general rules of displacement: the total displacement is the sum of the variables $\lfloor U_ib_i\rfloor$, where the $U_i$ are independent and uniform on $[0,1]$ and $b_i$ is the size of the component that receives the $i$-th car, and given the sizes of the receiving components the costs of the cars are independent [MV, p. 4, and Section 4.1 with its Note 1, p. 38]. That is the conditional law of the displacements given the sizes of the blocks in their order of arrival; the preprint builds no tree or forest from the process, and it does not treat the maximum displacement.

Three things were not found in the sources of this paragraph that were read in full, which are [GL], [KY], [Sh], [Pet], [FPV], [Kn98] and [MV]; the brackets of the four statements name the complete lists of the sources read for each. None of them relates the tree to the blocks of the process at intermediate times, as Proposition 2.4(d) does; the tree of Chassaing and Louchard from which the block sizes at earlier times are recovered by erasing edges is a different tree (Section 2.4). None of those that treat labeled forests, [GL], [KY] and [Sh], states the law of the vertex inversion numbers of a uniformly labeled forest, Lemma 2.5, which is what turns Shin's identity into the sum (2.3) over forests of a product of functions of the fringe subtree sizes; the bracket of the lemma names the further sources read for it. One of them, the article of Björner and Wachs on the labelings of a fixed forest, has the law of the sum of these numbers and not their joint law [BW, Theorem 1.3]; Section 2.5 says what it contains. And none identifies the sizes of the blocks closed by the cars with the fringe subtree sizes of a uniform random forest, which is the content of Theorem 2.6(b) and (c) beyond the conditional law just quoted, or states the formula (2.4) for the maximum.

*Weights on the fringe subtree sizes.* For a function $g$ on the positive integers, with values in a commutative ring containing the rational numbers, and a rooted forest $F$ put
$$
\Phi_g(F)=\prod_{v\in F}g(t_v),\qquad Z_n[g]=\sum_{F}\Phi_g(F),
$$
the sum being over the rooted labeled forests on $[n]$, with $Z_0[g]=1$. Thus $Z_n[g]/p_n$ is the expectation of $\Phi_g$ over the uniform forest, and by (2.2) with $f(t)=t\,g(t)$
$$
Z_n[g]=\sum_{\sigma\in S_n}\prod_{i=1}^n h_i(\sigma)\,g(h_i(\sigma)). \tag{2.5}
$$
By Theorem 2.6(a), every sum over the parking functions of a product $\prod_iw(d_i)$ of one function of the displacements is of this form, with $g(t)=\frac1t\sum_{d<t}w(d)$ [proved: this is (2.3)]. The maximum displacement is the case $w=\mathbf 1_{\{d<k\}}$, for which $g(t)=\min(1,k/t)$ and $Z_n[g]=Z_n(k)$; Section 2.5 gives other cases. In the combinatorial literature the number $t_v$ is called the hook length of $v$, and sums over families of trees of products of a function of the hook lengths are the subject of hook length formulas [CGG], [KP], [JY]; Section 3.1 gives the probabilistic reading of $\log\Phi_g$ as an additive functional.

### 2.3 The uniform forest as a conditioned Galton–Watson forest

**Theorem 2.8** (Poisson Galton–Watson trees) [proved; known: [J12, Example 10.2, p. 131, with (10.10)]; for mean $1$ a remark of Aldous [Ald, p. 242]; for every mean in $(0,1]$, [P99, Section 4.2], where it is called an observation of Aldous; also [CM, pp. 5–6]]. Let $0<\lambda\le1$ and let $\mathcal T$ be the family tree of a Galton–Watson process started from one individual in which every individual has a Poisson($\lambda$) number of children; for these $\lambda$ the tree is finite with probability $1$, as the proof shows. Forget the order of the children and label the vertices by a uniformly random bijection onto $\{1,\dots,|\mathcal T|\}$. Then every rooted labeled tree $\tau$ with $m$ vertices has probability
$$
\Pr(\mathcal T=\tau)=\frac{e^{-\lambda m}\lambda^{m-1}}{m!}. \tag{2.6}
$$
Consequently the size of $\mathcal T$ has the Borel distribution, $\Pr(|\mathcal T|=m)=e^{-\lambda m}(\lambda m)^{m-1}/m!$, and conditionally on $|\mathcal T|=m$ the tree is uniform on the $m^{m-1}$ rooted labeled trees with $m$ vertices, for every such $\lambda$. For $\lambda=1$ the size has the law $\mathrm{Bor}$ of Section 1.5.

*Proof.* An ordered rooted tree in which the vertex $v$ has $c_v$ children has probability $\prod_v e^{-\lambda}\lambda^{c_v}/c_v!=e^{-\lambda m}\lambda^{m-1}\prod_v 1/c_v!$, since $\sum_vc_v=m-1$. A rooted labeled tree $\tau$ without order arises from $\prod_vc_v!$ ordered labeled trees, each of which is obtained from one ordered tree and one of the $m!$ labelings. This gives (2.6); the right side depends on $\tau$ only through $m$, and there are $m^{m-1}$ trees of size $m$. Summing (2.6) over all trees gives the probability that the tree is finite, $\sum_{m\ge1}m^{m-1}e^{-\lambda m}\lambda^{m-1}/m!=T(\lambda e^{-\lambda})/\lambda$, and this equals $1$ for $0<\lambda\le1$, because by the proof of Lemma 1.1(c) the function $T$ is on $[0,1/e]$ the inverse of $u\mapsto ue^{-u}$ on $[0,1]$. $\square$

Two consequences of (2.6) are recorded in the next two paragraphs [proved].

Formula (2.6) shows why the mean disappears: the law of $\mathcal T$ is an exponential family in which the size is the sufficient statistic, so conditioning on the size removes the parameter. The value $\lambda=1$ is distinguished only as the critical one: for $\lambda<1$ the size has the masses $\lambda^{-1}(\lambda e^{1-\lambda})^m\,e^{-m}a_m$, which carry the exponentially decaying factor $(\lambda e^{1-\lambda})^m$ in addition to $e^{-m}a_m$; at $\lambda=1$ this factor is absent, the masses are $e^{-m}a_m$, and their sum is $T(1/e)=1$. The restriction to $\lambda\le1$ serves only to make the tree finite with probability $1$: the proof of (2.6) uses no bound on $\lambda$, so for $\lambda>1$ the finite trees have the same probabilities and, given its size, the tree is again uniform.

Combining Theorem 2.8 with the remark on the virtual root in Section 1.5: take a Poisson($\lambda$) Galton–Watson tree conditioned to have $n+1$ vertices, label it uniformly by $\{0,\dots,n\}$, condition on the root having label $0$ (an event independent of the shape) and remove the root. The result is the uniform random rooted labeled forest on $[n]$, the object of Theorem 2.6. The number of its components is the number of children of the root and is not fixed.

### 2.4 The blocks at intermediate times, their coalescent, and the last car

The history can be stopped after $t$ cars. On the circle there are then $q=N-t$ blocks.

**Theorem 2.9** (the blocks at an intermediate time) [proved; known: for the blocks of linear probing the law is [J01, Lemma 4.1], quoted in [J05, Lemma 3.1] and in [J12, Example 12.6, p. 146] and stated without proof in [Pet, p. 459]; the count of the lists that leave a given set occupied is [KY, Proposition 2.5 in the numbering of the arXiv version] and the case of unit masses of [MV, Theorem 2.6]; the identification with the tree sizes of a uniform forest is [CL, Section 5.1]; Pavlov's representation of those tree sizes is [P99, Proposition 7 and Section 4.1]]. Let $n$ cars with independent uniform preferences park on the circle of $N=n+1$ spots, and consider the moment after the first $t$ cars, $0\le t\le n$; put $q=N-t$. Choose one of the $q$ empty spots uniformly at random and read the sizes of the blocks clockwise, starting after the chosen spot. The resulting composition $(s_1,\dots,s_q)$ of $N$ has probability proportional to $\prod_{i}a_{s_i}$. Equivalently, it has the law of $q$ independent Borel variables conditioned on their sum being $N$, and the law of the sizes of the trees, in uniform random order, of a uniform random rooted labeled forest with $q$ trees on $N$ vertices. Moreover, a given set $O$ of $t$ spots is the set of occupied spots with probability $t!\,N^{-t}\prod_ia_{s_i}$, where the $s_i$ are the sizes of its blocks; in particular all configurations with the same multiset of block sizes are equally likely.

*Proof.* Let $O$ be a set of $t$ spots whose complement consists of $q$ empty spots, and let the block ending at the $i$-th empty spot contain $c_i=s_i-1$ occupied spots. A list of $t$ preferences leaves exactly $O$ occupied if and only if, for each $i$, exactly $c_i$ cars prefer the occupied spots of the $i$-th block and these cars, in their order of arrival, form a parking function on those $c_i$ consecutive spots: cars cannot pass an empty spot, and a car preferring an empty spot of the final configuration would have taken it. The number of such lists is $\frac{t!}{c_1!\cdots c_q!}\prod_i(c_i+1)^{c_i-1}$, and $(c+1)^{c-1}/c!=s^{s-2}/(s-1)!=a_s$ for $s=c+1$. So the probability of $O$ is $t!\,N^{-t}\prod_ia_{s_i}$, which depends on $O$ only through the block sizes; the first assertion follows because a pair (configuration, chosen empty spot) is the same as a pair (position of the chosen spot, composition). Since $\mathrm{Bor}(s)=e^{-s}a_s$ and the sizes add up to $N$, the weight $\prod_ia_{s_i}$ is proportional to $\prod_i\mathrm{Bor}(s_i)$. Finally the number of pairs consisting of a rooted labeled forest with $q$ trees on $N$ vertices and an ordering of its trees in which the sizes are $s_1,\dots,s_q$ is $N!\prod_ia_{s_i}$, and every forest has $q!$ orderings; so the sizes of the trees of a uniform forest, taken in uniform random order, have the same law. $\square$

The representation of the tree sizes of a uniform forest by conditioned independent variables is due to Pavlov; see [P99, Proposition 7 and Section 4.1]. Theorem 2.9 shows that it holds for the blocks of the parking process at every time, by a direct count.

The evolution in time is also classical. By Lemma 2.2 the block in which a car lands is chosen with probability proportional to its size, and it merges with its clockwise neighbour. By Theorem 2.9 all configurations with the same multiset of block sizes are equally likely, so at a single time the neighbour is a uniformly chosen other block; Theorem 2.10 below says, and its proof shows, that this remains true when the earlier sizes are known as well. Chassaing and Louchard observed this merging rule and its coincidence with the coalescent of Aldous and Pitman [CL, Sections 1.4 and 8].

**Theorem 2.10** (additive coalescent) [proved; known: the merging rule is [CL, Section 1.4, (1.5)] and the identification with the discrete additive coalescent is [CL, Section 8], both in the running text there; for the model of aggregating servers on a cycle, of which the present process is the case of equal rates, the additive rule for the sizes is [Ber, Proposition 2(ii)]; the coalescent of forests is [AP, Lemma 1] and [P99, Theorem 5 with (10)]; the statement of [CL] is recalled in [MV, Remark 1.13], where the law of the process of the multiset of sizes is shown to be the same for a class of rules of displacement [MV, Theorem 2.7]]. The multiset of block sizes of the parking process on the circle, as a function of the number of cars, is a Markov chain. When there are $q\ge2$ blocks, with $m_x$ blocks of size $x$, the next car merges a block of size $x$ with a block of size $y\ne x$ with probability $(x+y)\,m_xm_y/(N(q-1))$, and two blocks of size $x$ with probability $x\,m_x(m_x-1)/(N(q-1))$. Equivalently, if the blocks are numbered in uniformly random order and have sizes $s_1,\dots,s_q$, the blocks $i$ and $j$ are merged with probability $(s_i+s_j)/(N(q-1))$. This is the discrete-time additive coalescent: the same chain describes the sizes of the trees when the edges of a uniform random labeled tree on $N$ vertices are deleted in uniform random order and time is reversed [AP, Lemma 1].

*Proof.* Let $\mathfrak M_t$ be the multiset of block sizes after $t$ cars and $O_t$ the set of occupied spots. We show by induction on $t$ that, conditionally on $\mathfrak M_0,\dots,\mathfrak M_t$, the set $O_t$ is uniformly distributed over the sets of $t$ spots whose block sizes form the multiset $\mathfrak M_t$. This holds for $t=0$. Suppose it holds for $t$, and let $O'$ be a set of $t+1$ spots with multiset $\mathfrak M'$, obtained from $\mathfrak M_t$ by replacing two sizes $x,y$ by $x+y$. If emptying a spot $z$ of $O'$ divides a block of size $b$ into blocks of sizes $a$ and $b-a$ and leaves a set with multiset $\mathfrak M_t$, then the multisets $\{x+y,a,b-a\}$ and $\{b,x,y\}$ coincide, and comparing their largest elements gives $b=x+y$ and $\{a,b-a\}=\{x,y\}$. So the sets $O$ with multiset $\mathfrak M_t$ from which one car can produce $O'$ are obtained by emptying a spot $z$ of $O'$ that divides a block of size $x+y$ of $O'$ into a block of size $x$ ending at $z$ followed by a block of size $y$, or the same with $x$ and $y$ exchanged; by Lemma 2.2 the car fills $z$ with probability $x/N$, respectively $y/N$. Hence the probability of the event $\{O_{t+1}=O'\}$ together with the given history is the constant probability of each $O$ times $\frac{x+y}N$ times the number of blocks of size $x+y$ of $O'$ (times $\frac12$ if $x=y$), which depends on $O'$ only through $\mathfrak M'$. This proves the claim for $t+1$. Given the history, $O_t$ is thus uniform over the configurations with multiset $\mathfrak M_t$. By the correspondence between configurations with a marked empty spot and pairs (position, composition) used in the proof of Theorem 2.9, the sequence of block sizes read from a uniformly chosen empty spot is then uniform over the arrangements of $\mathfrak M_t$; so, for blocks numbered in uniformly random order, the block that follows block $i$ clockwise is each of the other $q-1$ blocks with probability $1/(q-1)$. By Lemma 2.2 the next car lands in block $i$ with probability $s_i/N$ and merges it with the block that follows it, which gives the probability $(s_i+s_j)/(N(q-1))$ for the pair $\{i,j\}$; summing over the pairs of blocks with given sizes gives the first form. These probabilities depend on the history only through $\mathfrak M_t$. $\square$

For $N=7$ the transition probabilities and the Markov property were also checked by enumerating all histories (Appendix B, item C3).

Chassaing and Louchard also describe a tree, built from a breadth-first correspondence between parking functions and trees [CL, Section 5.1], from which forests with the tree sizes of the blocks at all earlier times are obtained by erasing the edges above the cars in the reverse order of their arrival [CL, Section 8]. That tree is not the history forest [proved: by the example that follows]. For the parking function $(1,1)$ the breadth-first correspondence makes both cars children of the root, because the cars that prefer the first place are the children of the root [CM, Proposition 4.1 and the description before it], whereas in the history forest the first car is a child of the second, whose subtree is the block of size $2$ that it closed. What the two trees have in common is the property of Proposition 2.4(d) at the level of the sizes.

The identification with the deletion of edges places one further quantity, which is not used later [proved, with the identification of the two chains that Theorem 2.10 cites]. For an integer $k\ge1$ let $\tau$ be the largest number of cars after which every block of the parking process on the circle still has at most $k$ spots. Then $n-\tau$ has the law of the number of edges that must be deleted, in uniform random order, from a uniform random labeled tree on $N$ vertices until every component has at most $k$ vertices: after $e$ deletions there are $e+1$ components, after $t$ cars there are $N-t$ blocks, the two chains of multisets of sizes have the same law under $e=n-t$, and the property is monotone in time. The law of $\tau$ is not derived in this paper.

The decomposition of the process at its last step gives a recursion in $n$ for the totals $Z_n[g]$.

**Proposition 2.11** (last-car recursion and exponential formula) [proved; known: the recursion (2.7) for the threshold weight is [AF, Theorem 3.7], and for the weight $[t]_q/t$ of the total displacement [FPV, p. 495] and [Kn98, (2.1)]; the equation (2.8) for an arbitrary weight is [CGG, Theorems 4.1 and 4.4]]. For every weight $g$ and every $n\ge1$,
$$
Z_n[g]=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\binom{n-1}{j}\,(j+1)\,g(j+1)\,Z_j[g]\,Z_{n-1-j}[g]. \tag{2.7}
$$
Equivalently the exponential generating function $F_g(x)=\sum_{n\ge0}Z_n[g]x^n/n!$ satisfies
$$
F_g'(x)=F_g(x)\sum_{t\ge1}t\,g(t)\,Z_{t-1}[g]\frac{x^{t-1}}{(t-1)!},\qquad\text{that is,}\qquad F_g=\exp\Bigl(\sum_{t\ge1}t\,g(t)\,Z_{t-1}[g]\frac{x^{t}}{t!}\Bigr). \tag{2.8}
$$

*Proof.* Use (2.5). In a permutation $\sigma$ of $[n]$ let $j+1$ be the spot of the last car $n$. Its capacity is $j+1$: the last car closes the block formed by the $j$ cars to its left. The capacities of the spots to the left depend only on the relative order of the cars there, and so do those to the right, because the last car separates the two sides. Choosing which $j$ cars stand on the left, and the two smaller permutations, gives (2.7). Multiplying by $x^{n-1}/(n-1)!$ and summing gives the first form of (2.8). The series in the exponent is the generating function of the trees weighted by $\Phi_g$: a tree with $t$ vertices is a root, with $t$ possible labels and weight $g(t)$, and a forest on the other $t-1$ labels. $\square$

In the forest, the last car is a root, its subtree is the block on its left, and (2.7) is the statement that a forest is a set of trees and a tree is a root with a forest below it. In the process it says that just before the last car arrives, once the position of the one spot of the street $\{1,\dots,n\}$ that is still empty and the set of cars parked on its left are given, the street consists of two independent parking processes on the two sides of that spot.

### 2.5 Other statistics of the displacements, spare spots, and the displacement of one car

By Theorem 2.6(a), for every function $w$ on the non-negative integers,
$$
\sum_{\alpha\in\mathrm{PF}_n}\prod_{i=1}^nw(d_i)=\sum_{\sigma\in S_n}\prod_{i=1}^n\ \sum_{d<h_i(\sigma)}w(d)=\sum_F\prod_{v\in F}\frac1{t_v}\sum_{d<t_v}w(d).
$$
The maximum displacement is the case $w=\mathbf 1_{\{d<k\}}$. Two other cases are classical.

**Proposition 2.12** [proved; the closed formulas quoted in (b) and in the paragraph after the proof are cited]. Let $[t]_q=1+q+\dots+q^{t-1}$.

(a) *Total displacement.* $\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\in\mathrm{PF}_n}q^{d_1+\dots+d_n}=\sum_{\sigma\in S_n}\prod_i[h_i(\sigma)]_q=\sum_F\prod_{v\in F}\frac{[t_v]_q}{t_v}$.

(b) *Lucky cars.* Call a car lucky if its displacement is $0$. Then $\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\in\mathrm{PF}_n}x^{\#\text{lucky cars}}=\sum_{\sigma\in S_n}\prod_i\bigl(h_i(\sigma)-1+x\bigr)=\sum_F\prod_{v\in F}\frac{t_v-1+x}{t_v}$, and this polynomial equals $x\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\bigl(i+(n-i+1)x\bigr)$ [GS, Theorem 10.1]; the equality with the product was checked for $n\le7$ by enumeration (Appendix B, item C10).

*Proof.* Take $w(d)=q^d$ and $w(d)=x^{\mathbf 1\{d=0\}}$ in the identity above. $\square$

In (a), the right-hand side is a sum over forests of a product over vertices of a function of the subtree size. By a theorem of Kreweras the same polynomial enumerates the labeled trees on $\{0,\dots,n\}$ by a statistic that later authors call the number of inversions [Kr, Théorèmes I and II]; Kreweras states it for sequences that he calls suites majeures, and the translation to parking functions and their total displacement is made in [Kn98], where the recursion (2.7) for the weight $[t]_q/t$ appears as formula (2.1), with the remark that its factor "corresponds to the displacement of the $n$th car". In the present terms Kreweras's theorem is (a) together with the following consequence of Lemma 2.5 [proved]. Fix a rooted forest with $n$ vertices. Its $n!$ labelings by bijections onto $[n]$, counted by $q$ to the number of pairs (ancestor, descendant) in which the descendant has the larger label, give $n!\prod_v[t_v]_q/t_v$, because that number of pairs is $\sum_v\iota_v$ and the $\iota_v$ are independent and uniform; reversing the order of the labels gives the same for the pairs in which the descendant has the smaller label, which are the inversions. The $n!$ labelings yield each labeled forest of the shape $S$ of the given forest exactly $|\mathrm{Aut}(S)|$ times, and the number of inversions does not change under an automorphism. Hence the labeled forests $F$ of shape $S$, counted by $q$ to their number of inversions $\operatorname{inv}(F)$, give $\frac{n!}{|\mathrm{Aut}(S)|}\prod_v[t_v]_q/t_v$, which is the sum of $\prod_{v\in F}[t_v]_q/t_v$ over these forests; summing over the shapes, $\sum_Fq^{\operatorname{inv}(F)}=\sum_F\prod_{v\in F}[t_v]_q/t_v$, the right-hand side of (a). Finally, a forest on $[n]$ is a tree on $\{0,\dots,n\}$ rooted at $0$, and the vertex $0$, which carries the smallest label, takes part in no inversion, so the inversions of the forest are those of the tree. This formula for all labelings of a fixed forest is a theorem of Björner and Wachs [BW, Theorem 1.3]. They state it for the pairs in which the descendant has the larger label, and it is these pairs that they call the inversions of a labeled forest, while the inversions of the present paragraph are the pairs in which the descendant has the smaller label; reversing the order of the labels exchanges the two. They prove the same formula for a second statistic, the major index, which is the sum of the subtree sizes $t_x$ over the vertices $x$ whose label is larger than the label of their parent. For their inversions the proof is an induction on the number of vertices with the two steps of the proof of Lemma 2.5: for a forest with several trees, the distribution of the labels among the trees, and for a tree, the choice of the label of the root [BW, pp. 180–181]. The article, which was read in full, counts the labelings by the total number of inversions in every statement and does not state the joint law of Lemma 2.5.

*Spare spots.*

In this subsection $r$ denotes the number of empty spots of the circle on which the process runs, not the deficit $n-k$ of Section 1.5. Let $m$ cars park by the same rule on a row of $m+r-1$ spots, $r\ge1$; $r-1$ spots stay empty. The case $r=1$ is that of the conjecture.

**Proposition 2.13** (spare spots) [proved; known: the count is [KY, Theorem 2.2 in the numbering of the arXiv version, where it is said to be adapted from Pitman and Stanley], [Kn98, p. 562], [J01, (1.3)] and [AF, (1)]; special case known: for the weight $w(d)=x^d$ the identity is [Kn98, (1.3) and (1.5)], [FPV, p. 504] and [KY, Theorem 2.7 with (2.14)]; differs: for the threshold weight the left side is the number of $\ell$-interval rational parking functions, for which [AF, Theorem 3.8] gives a recursion and not this identity; for a general weight the identity was not found in these articles, which were read in full]. The number of preference lists in $\{1,\dots,m+r-1\}^m$ on which all $m$ cars park is $r(m+r)^{m-1}$. For every function $w$ of the displacements, with $g(t)=\frac1t\sum_{d<t}w(d)$ and $F_g$ as in Proposition 2.11, the sum on the left being over the lists $\alpha$ on which all cars park and the sum on the right over the rooted labeled forests $E$ on $[m]$,
$$
\sum_{\alpha}\prod_{i=1}^mw(d_i)=m!\,[x^m]\,F_g(x)^r=\sum_{E\ \text{on}\ [m]}r^{\,\#\text{trees of }E}\,\Phi_g(E).
$$
The history of this process is the history of the parking process on the circle of $N=m+r$ spots, stopped after $m$ cars, when $r$ blocks remain; the sizes of the blocks have the law of Theorem 2.9 with $q=r$.

*Proof.* On the circle of $N=m+r$ spots every list parks and $r$ spots stay empty. As in Theorem 2.1, a list leaves spot $N$ empty if and only if it is a list on which all cars park on the row $1,\dots,N-1$, and exactly $r$ of the $N$ rotations of a list leave spot $N$ empty; this gives $rN^m/N$ lists. On the row, the $r-1$ empty spots divide the occupied spots into $r$ intervals, some of which may be empty, and, as in the proof of Theorem 2.9, the cars that park in an interval of $c$ spots form a parking function of length $c$ on that interval. Summing $\prod w(d_i)$ over the lists with given intervals and given sets of cars gives the product of the numbers $Z_c[g]$ of the intervals, by Theorem 2.6(a), and the sum over the ways to distribute the cars is $m!\,[x^m]F_g^r$. Finally $F_g$ is the exponential of the generating function of the weighted trees by (2.8), so $F_g^r$ is the generating function of the forests in which every tree carries the additional weight $r$.
