For the last assertion: by the rotation argument a uniform list on which all cars park is obtained from a uniform list on the circle by choosing one of its $r$ empty spots uniformly and rotating it to position $N$; reading the blocks from spot $N$ is then reading them from a uniformly chosen empty spot, and Theorem 2.9 with $t=m$ and $q=r$ gives their law. $\square$

So the spare spots change the law of the forest by a factor $r$ for each tree and change nothing else: the displacements are again independent uniform marks on the fringe subtree sizes [proved: the proposition, as in Theorem 2.6]. The same family arises inside the original problem, as the state of the process before the last $r-1$ cars arrive [proved: the last assertion of the proposition]. The law of the forest has a classical description.

**Remark 2.14** (the forest of the family with spare spots) [proved; the model is known: it is that of Moon as presented in [P99, Section 4.2, (18)], in its rooted form; the identification of the forest of the family with spare spots with it is not stated in [P99], which was read in full, and was not looked for elsewhere]. The law on the rooted labeled forests on $[m]$ in which a forest has probability proportional to $r^{\#\text{trees}}$ is the law of the forest obtained from a uniform random rooted labeled tree on $[m]$ by deleting each of its $m-1$ edges independently with probability $r/(m+r)$. Indeed, there are $\binom{m-1}{k-1}m^{m-k}$ rooted labeled forests with $k$ trees on $[m]$ (Lemma 1.1(b)); so under the weighted law the number $K$ of trees satisfies $\Pr(K=k)\propto\binom{m-1}{k-1}(r/m)^{k-1}$, that is, $K-1$ is binomial with parameters $m-1$ and $p$, where $p/(1-p)=r/m$, and given $K=k$ the forest is uniform among the forests with $k$ trees. In the deletion model with $p=r/(m+r)$ the number of deleted edges has the same binomial law and is independent of the tree, since every tree has $m-1$ edges; and given that it equals $k-1$ the deleted edges form a uniformly random set of $k-1$ edges, so that the forest is uniform among the rooted forests with $k$ trees by [P99, Theorem 4]. For $r=1$ and $m=n$ this describes the uniform forest on $[n]$ as a uniform rooted tree on $[n]$ whose edges are deleted independently with probability $1/(n+1)$.

The rows of this family are the subject of Section 6.3.

*The displacement of one car.*

The uniform forest forgets the order of arrival, so it does not contain the displacement of, say, the last car. The description in time does.

**Proposition 2.15** [proved; differs: [MV, (4.38), in the proof of Lemma 4.10, p. 49] gives the exact law of the size of the component that receives the car arriving at a given time, from which the formula follows by one summation with Lemma 2.2, and does not write the law of the displacement; [J05] prints the three statements from which the formula follows at once, (34) on p. 195, (4) on p. 181 and Lemma 3.1, and states as theorems the limit of this law for a load below one [J05, Theorem 4.5] and the exact law of the displacement of a uniformly chosen key [J05, Theorem 7.2]; the formula for the car that arrives at a given time is not printed there; for the last car its two pieces are in [Pet, p. 458], not combined; [CL, p. 12, with (2.6)] gives the law of the size of the block that contains a given place after a given number of cars, from which the formula follows by one step that the article does not take, and the displacement of a car does not occur in that article, which was read in full]. In a uniform random parking function of length $n$, for $0\le t\le n-1$ and $j\ge0$, the displacement of car $t+1$ satisfies
$$
\Pr(d_{t+1}\ge j)=\frac{q}{N}\sum_{s>j}(s-j)\,\frac{\mathrm{Bor}(s)\,\mathrm{Bor}^{*(q-1)}(N-s)}{\mathrm{Bor}^{*q}(N)},\qquad q=N-t,
$$
(here $q\ge2$).

*Proof.* On the circle the preference of car $t+1$ is uniform and independent of the first $t$ cars. By Lemma 2.2 its displacement is at least $j$ with probability $\sum_i(s_i-j)_+/N$, where the $s_i$ are the sizes of the $q$ blocks present. By Theorem 2.9 each block size has the law of the first of $q$ independent Borel variables conditioned on their sum being $N$. $\square$

For the last car ($t=n-1$, $q=2$) this gives $\Pr(d_n\ge j)=\frac2N\sum_{s>j}(s-j)\,\mathrm{Bor}(s)\mathrm{Bor}(N-s)/\mathrm{Bor}^{*2}(N)$; for $n=2$ and $j=1$ the value is $\frac13$, in agreement with the three parking functions $11,12,21$.

### 2.6 The limit law of the maximum displacement

**Theorem 2.16** (Petersson) [proved; cited: [Pet, Theorem 5.1], with the limit variable described by [Pet, Theorem 4.7], which is [JP, Corollary 2.4]; transferred to parking functions by Theorem 2.1]. Let $M_n$ be the maximum displacement of a uniform random parking function of length $n$. Then $(n+1-M_n)/(n+1)^{2/3}$ converges in distribution to a random variable $X>0$ with
$$
\Pr(X>x)=\mathbb E\exp\bigl(-x^{3/2}\mathcal B\bigr),\qquad \mathcal B=\int_0^1\sup_{0\le s\le t}B(s)\,dt,
$$
where $B$ is a standard Brownian motion.

Petersson's theorem is stated for linear probing hashing on a circle of $m$ cells with $m-1$ items, conditioned on the last cell remaining empty (the displacements have the same law without this conditioning); by Theorem 2.1 this is the uniform parking function of length $n=m-1$. In the source the limit law reads $\Pr(Y\le z)=1-\psi(z^{3/2})$ with $\psi(s)=\mathbb E\,e^{-s\mathcal B}$ the Laplace transform of $\mathcal B$ (there denoted $A$), which is the formula above. Explicitly [JP, Corollary 2.4],
$$
\psi(s)=\frac1{\Gamma(2/3)}\sum_{j\ge0}\frac{\Gamma(j+\frac23)}{\Gamma(\frac{3j}2+1)}\Bigl(-\frac{3\sqrt2}{4}\,s\Bigr)^{j},
$$
a series that converges for every complex $s$. In the summary of Petersson's thesis the same statements are numbered Theorem 2.1 and Corollary 2.11.


## 3. Fringe subtrees and additive functionals

For a rooted tree $T$ and a vertex $v$ of $T$, the subtree $T_v$ consisting of $v$ and all its descendants is called the *fringe subtree* of $T$ at $v$. A functional of rooted trees of the form
$$
F(T)=\sum_{v\in T}f(T_v)
$$
is called an *additive functional*, and the function $f$ its *toll function*; equivalently $F(T)=f(T)+\sum_iF(T_i)$, where the $T_i$ are the branches of $T$, the fringe subtrees at the children of the root. These are the definitions of [J16, p. 58, (1.3), Remark 1.1 and (1.6)], where the fringe subtree at a uniformly random vertex is attributed to Aldous [Ald]; the survey [HJ, pp. 57 and 59] fixes the same vocabulary, with the name *random fringe tree*. The numbers $t_v$ of this paper are the sizes $|T_v|$ of the fringe subtrees, and a toll that depends on its argument only through its size gives a functional of the fringe subtree sizes; this is the case in which the theory was first developed for binary trees, with the tolls $|T|^\alpha$ and $\log|T|$ [FK, Definition 2.1].

This section places the maximum displacement in that theory (Section 3.1), counts the configurations of fringe subtrees of prescribed sizes in the uniform forest (Section 3.2), derives from the count an expansion of the threshold functional over the vertices above the threshold (Section 3.3), treats the largest fringe subtrees and the scale $n^{2/3}$ (Section 3.4), and applies the counts to the total displacement (Section 3.5).

### 3.1 The law of the maximum displacement as an exponential moment of an additive functional

Let $\mathcal T_N$ be the conditioned Galton–Watson tree with Poisson offspring and $N=n+1$ vertices, so that removing its root gives the uniform forest $F$ on $[n]$ (Theorem 2.8 and the paragraph after it), whose trees are the branches $T_1,T_2,\dots$ of $\mathcal T_N$. By Corollary 2.7, for every integer $k\ge1$,
$$
\Pr(M<k)=\mathbb E\prod_{v\in F}\min\Bigl(1,\frac k{t_v}\Bigr)=\mathbb E\exp\Bigl(\sum_iF_k(T_i)\Bigr),\qquad f_k(T)=\log\min\Bigl(1,\frac k{|T|}\Bigr),
$$
where $F_k$ is the additive functional with the toll $f_k$ [proved: this is (2.4) rewritten]. So the probability that all displacements are smaller than $k$ is an exponential moment of an additive functional of a conditioned Galton–Watson tree. The toll depends on the fringe subtree only through its size, vanishes on the fringe subtrees with at most $k$ vertices, and equals $-\log(|T|/k)$ on the larger ones.

Three features of this functional decide what the present paper can take from the theory of additive functionals, and they are the reason why the following subsections count fringe subtrees exactly instead of quoting limit theorems.

*The toll is not small on large trees.* The law of large numbers and the central limit theorem for additive functionals of conditioned Galton–Watson trees are proved for tolls that are small on large trees: the central limit theorem [J16, Theorem 1.5] assumes, in the notation of that paper, $\mathbb Ef(T_n)^2\to0$ and $\sum_n(\mathbb Ef(T_n)^2)^{1/2}/n<\infty$, and its variant [J16, Theorem 1.13] assumes a bounded toll. For the toll $f_k$ with $k$ fixed, $f_k(T)=-\log(n/k)$ on every tree with $n>k$ vertices, which is unbounded and violates both conditions [proved: by inspection of the hypotheses quoted]. That paper describes the dividing line in these words: its conditions are such that the sum "is dominated by the many small subtrees", whereas "for a toll function $f$ that grows too rapidly with the size of $T$, the sum (1.3) [of that paper: the additive functional] will on the contrary be dominated by large subtrees, which are more strongly dependent, and then other limit distributions will appear" [J16, Remark 1.11]. The toll $\log|T|$ is on that line: for uniform binary trees the functional with this toll, the *shape functional*, is asymptotically normal with a variance of order $n\log n$ [FK, Theorem 4.2]; for simply generated families, among them the rooted labeled trees, the mean and the variance of the functional with this toll are known asymptotically, the variance being of order $n\log n$ [MM98, Theorems 3.1 and 4.1]; and for general conditioned Galton–Watson trees a limit theorem of this kind is stated in [J16, Remark 1.11] as a conjecture. For uniform rooted labeled trees, the trees of the present paper, the central limit theorem of Wagner assumes a bounded toll whose mean absolute value over the trees with $n$ vertices decreases exponentially in $n$ [Wa12, Theorem 1], and his theorem for simply generated families assumes the same [Wa, Theorem 2]; the toll $f_k$, which is unbounded, is outside both.

*The threshold moves with $n$.* The positions $(n,k)$ that matter for the conjecture have $k$ between a constant and $n$, so the toll changes with $n$; no limit theorem of [J16] concerns a toll depending on $n$ [the paper was read in full for this statement; Section 1.4].

*The quantity is an exponential moment.* The conjecture concerns the numbers $\Pr(M<k)$ themselves, that is, $\mathbb E\exp(\cdot)$ of the functional, a sum over forests of the product $\prod_vg(t_v)$, and not the law of the functional after centring and scaling. Neither [J16] nor [FK] and [FFK] consider the expectation of a product over the vertices of a function of the fringe subtree size [read in full for this statement; Section 1.4]. Meir and Moon do, for one function: for a simply generated family with degree function $\Phi$ they state, without proof, that the generating function $E(x)=\sum_ne_ny_nx^n$, in which $e_n$ is the expectation of $\prod_v|T_v|^{-1}$ over the trees with $n$ vertices and $y_n$ the total weight of these trees, satisfies $E'=\Phi(E)$, and that for rooted labeled trees with $n$ vertices the expectation is $e_n=n!/n^n$ [MM98, Section 5, (5.1), pp. 210–211]. In the present notation this is the case $k=1$ of (2.4) [proved: the product does not depend on the labels, so the root may be taken to carry the label $0$; the tree on $N=n+1$ vertices has, besides the vertices of the forest, its root, with the factor $1/N$; and $N\cdot N!/N^N=n!/(n+1)^{n-1}=Z_n(1)/p_n$]. That article has no weight with a threshold, and its theorems concern the sum of the logarithms and not the product [read in full for this statement]. Products of this kind for particular weights, for which the sum over the trees has a closed form, are treated in the combinatorial literature under the name of hook length formulas [cited: [CGG], [KP], [JY], each read in part; Section 2.2]; the weight $1/t$ is the simplest of them. No closed form for the threshold weight is given in this paper, and none was looked for in that literature (not verified against [CGG], [KP], [JY]).

What does apply, for every $n$ and every toll, are the exact formulas for the first and second moments of an additive functional of a conditioned Galton–Watson tree [J16, Lemma 5.1 and Lemma 6.1]. They are counts of fringe subtrees of a given size and of pairs of fringe subtrees, nested or disjoint. Section 3.2 gives the count for an arbitrary configuration in the Poisson case, where it becomes a product formula.

*The threshold functional and its heavy vertices.*

For real $y>0$ let $g_y(t)=\min(1,y/t)$ and write
$$
Z_n(y)=Z_n[g_y]=\sum_F\prod_{v\in F}\min\Bigl(1,\frac{y}{t_v}\Bigr).
$$
For an integer $y=k\ge1$ this is the number of parking functions of length $n$ with $M<k$, by (2.4), so the notation agrees with Section 1.5. The function $y\mapsto Z_n(y)$ is continuous, non-decreasing and piecewise polynomial, with $Z_n(y)=p_n$ for $y\ge n$.

Relative to a threshold $y$, a vertex $v$ is *heavy* if $t_v>y$ and *light* if $t_v\le y$; for an integer threshold $k$, a vertex with $t_v=k$ is a *threshold vertex*. The functional $\Phi_{g_y}$ equals $\prod_{v\text{ heavy}}y/t_v$: light vertices contribute the factor $1$. Two consequences will be used constantly. For $y$ in an interval $[j,j+1)$ between two consecutive integers the set of heavy vertices of a given forest is the same, namely the vertices with $t_v\ge j+1$, so that on the closed interval $[j,j+1]$ the functional $\Phi_{g_y}(F)$ is a constant times $y$ raised to the number of these vertices. And the subtrees of heavy vertices are large: two heavy vertices are either nested or have disjoint subtrees of size greater than $y$ each, so a forest on $[n]$ contains at most $n/y$ heavy vertices no one of which is an ancestor of another, and if $n<2y$ the heavy vertices lie on one path descending from a root.

*The tilted law.* For real $y>0$ the *tilted law* on the rooted labeled forests on $[n]$ gives a forest $F$ the probability $\Phi_{g_y}(F)/Z_n(y)$; expectations under it are written $\mathbb E_y$. For an integer $y=k$ it is the law of the shape of the history forest, with uniform labels, of a parking function conditioned on $M<k$ [proved: Theorem 2.6(c)]. For $n\ge2$ it depends on $y$: for $y\le1$ it is the law with probabilities proportional to $\prod_v1/t_v$, and for $y\ge n$ it is the uniform law. In terms of the process it is the law of the shape of the history forest when the parking process is conditioned to survive, where each arriving car survives with probability $\min(1,y/h)$ if it lands in a block of size $h$, independently of the other cars once the blocks are given; for an integer $y=k$ the survival of a car is the event that its displacement is smaller than $k$ [proved: Lemma 2.2 and Theorem 2.6(c)]. Statements proved for the uniform forest do not transfer to the tilted law without an argument; Section 4.2 determines which weights on fringe subtree sizes are compatible with the deletion of a uniform edge, and for an integer threshold $k\ge2$ the threshold weight is not among them (Theorem 4.2, last assertion; for $k=1$ it is the weight $1/s$).

### 3.2 Counting configurations of fringe subtrees: the pattern formula

Let $\tau$ be a rooted tree on $\{0,\dots,n\}$ with root $0$, that is, a forest on $[n]$ with its virtual root; put $N=n+1$.

The count behind all the formulas of this subsection is the following elementary one.

**Lemma 3.1** (cancellation of the exponentials) [proved; elementary (a multinomial count); it is a step of the proof of Theorem 3.2 and was not looked for in the literature as a separate statement]. Suppose that a family of objects on the label set $\{0,\dots,n\}$ is in bijection with the choices of: an ordered partition of the labels into sets of sizes $\gamma_1,\dots,\gamma_r$ with $0$ in the first set, and on the $i$-th set an object from a class having $c_i(\gamma_i)$ members. Then the number of objects divided by $p_n$ is
$$
\frac{\gamma_1}{\mathrm{Bor}(N)}\ \prod_{i=1}^r\frac{c_i(\gamma_i)\,e^{-\gamma_i}}{\gamma_i!},\qquad N=n+1=\gamma_1+\dots+\gamma_r .
$$
Each factor is the normalised count of one class, a Borel-type mass, and no power of $e$ is left because the sizes add up to $N$.

*Proof.* The number of ordered partitions is $\frac{N!}{\gamma_1!\cdots\gamma_r!}\cdot\frac{\gamma_1}{N}$, and $p_n=n!\,e^{N}\mathrm{Bor}(N)=N!\,e^N\mathrm{Bor}(N)/N$. $\square$

**Definition** (pattern, occurrence, layer). A *pattern* is a finite rooted tree $\mathfrak p$ with root $\varrho$ in which every node $u\ne\varrho$ carries a positive integer $t_u$ and a type, *child* or *descendant*. Put $t_\varrho=N$ and, for every node $u$,
$$
\gamma_u=t_u-\sum_{u'\ \text{child of}\ u\ \text{in}\ \mathfrak p}t_{u'};
$$
we require $\gamma_u\ge1$ for all $u$. An *occurrence* of $\mathfrak p$ in $\tau$ is a map $u\mapsto v_u$ from the nodes of $\mathfrak p$ to the vertices of $\tau$ with $v_\varrho=0$ such that, for every node $u\ne\varrho$ with parent $u^-$ in $\mathfrak p$: the subtree of $v_u$ has $t_u$ vertices; $v_u$ is a proper descendant of $v_{u^-}$, and a child of $v_{u^-}$ if $u$ has type child; and the subtrees of $v_u$ and $v_{u'}$ are disjoint whenever $u\ne u'$ have the same parent in $\mathfrak p$. The *layer* of a node $u$ is the set of vertices of the subtree of $v_u$ that lie in none of the subtrees of the $v_{u'}$, $u'$ a child of $u$; it has $\gamma_u$ vertices, contains $v_u$, and is connected.

A pattern with a single non-root node asks for a vertex whose subtree has a given size. A path asks for a chain of nested subtrees of given sizes. A node with two children asks for two disjoint subtrees inside a third.

**Theorem 3.2** (pattern formula) [proved; special case known: for the patterns, with all nodes of type descendant, that have one node besides the root, or two such nodes of which the second is a proper descendant of the first or has a subtree disjoint from that of the first, (3.1) is the Poisson case of the exact formulas of [J16, Lemma 5.1, and Lemma 6.1 with (6.7) and (6.8)], which hold for every critical offspring law with finite variance; related exact formulas, for several fringe subtrees of prescribed shapes, or of one common size, in a plane tree with prescribed degrees, are [BHJ, Lemma 3.2] and [BHJ2, (5.4)]; for one node besides the root, the proof of [Ald, Lemma 9, pp. 241–242] has an exact product formula for the joint law of the fringe subtree at a uniform vertex and of the rest of the tree, valid for every critical offspring law under the hypotheses of that lemma, by which the two pieces are independent given their sizes; that formula prescribes the fringe subtree as a tree and not by its size, has the number of leaves of the rest as a factor, and is not evaluated there for Poisson offspring; the general statement, and part (b) for patterns with more than one node besides the root, were not found in [J16], [HJ], [J12], [P99], [Pa], [J06], [ABH], [AP], [BHJ], [BHJ2], [KV], [Wa12], [Wa], [Ald], which were read in full, nor in the chapters of [CSP] read; see the paragraph after Proposition 3.4]. Let $\tau$ be uniformly distributed on the rooted labeled trees on $\{0,\dots,n\}$ with root $0$, and let $\mathfrak p$ be a pattern. For a node $u$ let $c_u$ be the number of its children in $\mathfrak p$ of type descendant.

(a) The expected number of occurrences of $\mathfrak p$ in $\tau$ is
$$
\frac{1}{\mathrm{Bor}(N)}\ \prod_{u\in\mathfrak p}\mathrm{Bor}(\gamma_u)\,\gamma_u^{\,c_u}. \tag{3.1}
$$

(b) Choose a pair consisting of a tree and an occurrence of $\mathfrak p$ in it uniformly among all such pairs. Conditionally on the sets of labels of the layers, the layers are independent; the layer of $u\ne\varrho$, rooted at $v_u$, is a uniform rooted labeled tree on its label set, the layer of $\varrho$ is a uniform tree on its label set rooted at $0$, and for each node $u'$ of type descendant the vertex of the layer of its parent $u$ to which $v_{u'}$ is attached is uniform on that layer, independently of everything else.

*Proof.* Given $\tau$ and an occurrence, cut the edges between each $v_{u'}$, $u'\ne\varrho$, and its parent in $\tau$. The components are the layers. The layer of $u\ne\varrho$ is a tree with root $v_u$; the layer of $\varrho$ is a tree with root $0$. The parent of $v_{u'}$ lies in the layer of the pattern-parent $u$ of $u'$: it is a descendant of $v_u$ or $v_u$ itself, and it cannot lie in the subtree of another $v_{u''}$ with the same pattern-parent, because that subtree is disjoint from the subtree of $v_{u'}$. Conversely, choose an ordered partition of the labels into sets of sizes $\gamma_u$ with $0$ in the set of $\varrho$, a rooted tree on each set (with root $0$ on the set of $\varrho$), and for each $u'\ne\varrho$ a vertex of the layer of its pattern-parent, which must be the root of that layer if $u'$ has type child; attaching the root of the layer of $u'$ to the chosen vertex, for all $u'$, gives a tree $\tau$ with an occurrence, and the subtree of $v_u$ has $\gamma_u$ plus the sizes of the subtrees below it, that is $t_u$, vertices. The two constructions are inverse to each other. The number of rooted trees on a set of size $\gamma$ is $\gamma^{\gamma-1}$, of those with a prescribed root $\gamma^{\gamma-2}$, and the number of admissible attachment vertices is $\gamma_u^{c_u}$ in total for the children of $u$. By Lemma 3.1, with the classes "rooted tree together with a choice of the attachment vertices" for $u\ne\varrho$ and "tree rooted at $0$ together with a choice of the attachment vertices" for $\varrho$ (where the factor $\gamma_\varrho$ of Lemma 3.1 turns $\gamma_\varrho^{\gamma_\varrho-2}$ into $\gamma_\varrho^{\gamma_\varrho-1}$), the number of pairs (tree, occurrence) divided by $p_n$ is (3.1). Part (b) is the statement that the bijection is with a product set. $\square$

The formula has one factor for each piece: a piece contributes its Borel mass, multiplied by its size once for each further piece that may be attached to it at an arbitrary vertex. The following instances are the ones used later.

**Corollary 3.3** [proved; (a) the first count is known: [J16, Lemma 5.1], [KV, Corollary 2.3], and for every simply generated family [Pa, (13)–(14)] and [KuP, (16)]; the second count, that of the trees of the forest, is the case of a node of type child and was not looked for as a separate statement; (b) special case known: two vertices, [J16, Lemma 6.1 with (6.7)]; (c) known: [MM78, (5.2)], [AP2, (107)], and [CSP, Exercise 10.1.4 with (9.5)]; (d) known: [P01, Section 4.1]]. Let $F$ be a uniform random rooted labeled forest on $[n]$, $N=n+1$.

(a) *Subtrees of a given size.* For $1\le s\le n$ the expected number of vertices $v$ with $t_v=s$ is $\mathrm{Ren}(N-s)\,\mathrm{Bor}(s)/\mathrm{Bor}(N)$, and the expected number of trees of $F$ with $s$ vertices is $\mathrm{Bor}(N-s)\,\mathrm{Bor}(s)/\mathrm{Bor}(N)$. For $s>n/2$ there is at most one vertex with $t_v=s$; the first number is then the probability that such a vertex exists, and the second the probability that $F$ has a tree with $s$ vertices.

(b) *Chains.* For $n\ge t_1>\dots>t_j\ge1$, the expected number of chains $v_1,\dots,v_j$ in which $v_{i+1}$ is a proper descendant of $v_i$ and $t_{v_i}=t_i$ is
$$
\frac{\mathrm{Bor}(t_j)}{\mathrm{Bor}(N)}\prod_{i=1}^{j}\mathrm{Ren}(\gamma_i),\qquad \gamma_1=N-t_1,\quad \gamma_i=t_{i-1}-t_i\ (i\ge2),
$$
and a factor $\mathrm{Ren}(\gamma_i)$ is replaced by $\mathrm{Bor}(\gamma_i)$ if $v_i$ is required to be a child of $v_{i-1}$ (a root of $F$, for $i=1$).

(c) *The ancestral line of a vertex and the birthday law.* The expected number of vertices at distance $d$ from the virtual root is $\mathrm{Bor}^{*(d+1)}(N)/\mathrm{Bor}(N)=(d+1)(N-1)_d/N^{d}$. Hence, if $V$ is a uniform random vertex of a uniform random rooted labeled tree on $N$ vertices and $D$ is its distance from the root,
$$
\Pr(D\ge d)=\frac{(N-1)(N-2)\cdots(N-d)}{N^{d}},
$$
which is the probability that $d+1$ independent uniform draws from $N$ values are distinct.

(d) *The renewal identity.* $\mathrm{Ren}(j)=\sum_{i\ge1}\mathrm{Bor}^{*i}(j)$ for $j\ge1$; in generating functions $zT'(z)=T(z)/(1-T(z))$.

*Proof.* (a) and (b) are (3.1) for a pattern that is a path, with all types descendant, or with the indicated types child; the last assertion of (a) holds because two subtrees with more than $n/2$ vertices each cannot be disjoint, and nested subtrees have different sizes. (c) A vertex at distance $d$ from $0$ is the end of a path of $d$ nodes of type child with arbitrary sizes; summing (3.1) over the sizes gives the sum of $\prod_{i=0}^d\mathrm{Bor}(\gamma_i)$ over the compositions $(\gamma_0,\dots,\gamma_d)$ of $N$, divided by $\mathrm{Bor}(N)$, and Lemma 1.1(b) evaluates it. Dividing by $N$ gives $\Pr(D=d)=(d+1)(N-1)_d/N^{d+1}=\frac{(N-1)_d}{N^d}-\frac{(N-1)_{d+1}}{N^{d+1}}$; the law of the shape of a uniform rooted tree does not depend on which label the root carries. (d) Cut a rooted tree with a marked vertex along the path from the root to the marked vertex: it becomes a sequence of $i\ge1$ rooted trees, the roots of which are the vertices of the path. Counting both sides with Lemma 1.1(a) and (b) gives the identity. $\square$

Part (d) is the reason for the name of $\mathrm{Ren}$: if $X_1,X_2,\dots$ are independent with law $\mathrm{Bor}$, then $\mathrm{Ren}(j)$ is the probability that some partial sum $X_1+\dots+X_i$ equals $j$, so $\mathrm{Ren}$ is the renewal sequence of the random walk with Borel steps [proved: part (d)].

The count of part (c) extends to forests with a prescribed number of trees.

**Proposition 3.4** (depth of a vertex in a forest with $q$ trees) [proved; special case known: the case $q=1$ is Corollary 3.3(c), which is known; for $q\ge2$ not found in [J12], [P99], [ABH], [J06], [Pa], [AP], [Ald], which were read in full, nor in [CSP] in the chapters read]. Let $1\le q\le N$, let $F$ be a uniform random rooted labeled forest with exactly $q$ trees on $N$ vertices, let $V$ be a uniform random vertex of $F$, chosen independently of $F$, and let $D$ be the distance of $V$ from the root of its tree. Then for every integer $d\ge0$
$$
\Pr(D\ge d)=\frac{(N-q)_d}{N^{d}} .
$$

*Proof.* Count the pairs consisting of a forest with $q$ trees on $[N]$ and a vertex $v$ at distance exactly $d$ from the root of its tree. The path from that root to $v$ has $d+1$ vertices $u_0,\dots,u_d=v$. Deleting its $d$ edges turns the tree of $v$ into a sequence of $d+1$ rooted trees, with roots $u_0,\dots,u_d$ in this order; conversely, a sequence of $d+1$ rooted trees on disjoint label sets becomes a rooted tree with a distinguished vertex at distance $d$ from the root when the root of each tree of the sequence is made a child of the root of the preceding one. Hence the pairs correspond bijectively to the pairs consisting of an ordered sequence of $d+1$ rooted trees and a set of $q-1$ further rooted trees, all on disjoint label sets with union $[N]$. Ordered sequences of $d+q$ rooted trees on disjoint label sets with union $[N]$ number $N!\,[z^N]T(z)^{d+q}$, and each of these pairs arises from $(q-1)!$ of them. By Lemma 1.1(b) the number of pairs is therefore
$$
\frac{N!}{(q-1)!}\cdot\frac{d+q}{N}\cdot\frac{N^{N-d-q}}{(N-d-q)!}
$$
if $d+q\le N$, and $0$ otherwise, since each tree has at least one vertex; and the number of forests with $q$ trees is $\frac{N!}{q!}\cdot\frac qN\cdot\frac{N^{N-q}}{(N-q)!}$. Dividing, and dividing by the number $N$ of vertices,
$$
\Pr(D=d)=\frac{(d+q)\,(N-q)_d}{N^{d+1}}=\frac{(N-q)_d}{N^{d}}-\frac{(N-q)_{d+1}}{N^{d+1}},
$$
because $(N-q)_{d+1}=(N-q)_d\,(N-q-d)$ and $N-(N-q-d)=d+q$. The formula holds also for $d+q>N$, where both sides vanish. Summing over the values $d,d+1,\dots$ gives the assertion. $\square$

For $q=1$ this is the law of Corollary 3.3(c).

*The counts of this subsection and the literature* [cited; the labels are those in the brackets of Theorem 3.2, Corollary 3.3 and Proposition 3.4]. For one fringe subtree the count is known in greater generality. For a conditioned Galton–Watson tree with any critical offspring law of finite variance, the expected number of vertices whose fringe subtree has a given size, or is a given tree, is given exactly, for every size of the tree, by [J16, Lemma 5.1]; the first count of Corollary 3.3(a) is its Poisson case. The same count is in [Pa, (13)–(14)] and in [KuP, (16)] for every simply generated family, and for Cayley trees, in terms of the piece cut off by a uniformly chosen edge, in [AP, proof of Lemma 14] and [P99, Corollary 10]. For two fringe subtrees, nested or disjoint, the counts are the terms (6.7) and (6.8) in the proof of [J16, Lemma 6.1], again for every such offspring law; these are the patterns with two nodes of type descendant. That paper computes variances and needs no more than two subtrees; it states nothing for three or more, nothing for the type child, and nothing about the conditional law of the pieces. Aldous's article [Ald], to which [J16] attributes the fringe subtree at a random vertex, is a paper on limits, and two exact identities in it bear on these counts. In the proof of its Lemma 9, for a critical offspring law $\xi$, the Galton–Watson tree $\mathcal T$ with that law, and trees $s$ and $t$ with $n+1$ vertices together, the probability that the fringe subtree at a uniformly chosen vertex of $\mathcal T$ is $t$ and that the rest of the tree, in which the chosen vertex is kept as a leaf, is $s$ is $\frac{[s]}{n}\cdot\frac{\Pr(\mathcal T=s)}{\Pr(\xi=0)}\cdot\Pr(\mathcal T=t)$, where $[s]$ is the number of leaves of $s$ [Ald, pp. 241–242]. This is a product of a factor for each of the two pieces, so that the pieces are independent given their sizes; the tree $t$ is prescribed there, where Theorem 3.2 prescribes a size, the offspring law is general, and the article does not sum the formula over the trees of a given size or evaluate it for Poisson offspring. The other identity holds for every random finite tree: the fringe subtrees at a uniformly chosen vertex and at its first $i$ ancestors are given trees $t_0,\dots,t_i$ with probability equal to the probability that the fringe subtree at a uniformly chosen vertex is $t_i$, multiplied by the product over $j=1,\dots,i$ of the number of children of the root of $t_j$ whose fringe subtree is $t_{j-1}$ [Ald, p. 247]; for conditioned Galton–Watson trees the article describes the limit of this chain, a tree with an infinite line of ancestors and independent branches [Ald, p. 251], and it has no formula in terms of sizes, nothing for two disjoint fringe subtrees and nothing for a finite number of pieces beyond two. The count for one fringe subtree is also in a recent preprint, as an exact identity for the fringe subtree at a uniform vertex of a conditioned Galton–Watson tree [KV, Corollary 2.3]. For several fringe subtrees the nearest statement found is of another kind: for a uniform random plane tree with a prescribed number of vertices of each degree, Berzunza Ojeda, Holmgren and Janson give an exact formula for the expected number of sequences of fringe subtrees with prescribed shapes, each of which is either disjoint from the others or a fringe subtree of another member of the sequence [BHJ, Lemma 3.2, with (3.14) for the disjoint case], proved by replacing each marked subtree by a marked leaf. A sequel prescribes sizes in the same model: it gives the expected number of fringe subtrees with a given number of vertices, and of tuples of distinct fringe subtrees which all have the same number of vertices and are therefore disjoint [BHJ2, (5.3), and (5.4) with the count in the sentence before it]; nested subtrees and subtrees of different sizes do not occur there. In these two preprints the tree is a plane tree conditioned on its degrees, and what is prescribed is a shape or one common size; in Theorem 3.2 the tree is the uniform labeled tree, the sizes are arbitrary, the nesting is prescribed by a pattern with the types child and descendant, and part (b) gives the conditional law of the pieces. For Cayley trees the two halves of Theorem 3.2(b) are in the literature for the pieces left by the deletion of uniformly chosen edges: the pieces are independent uniform trees given their vertex sets [CSP, p. 220], and, given the pieces, the way in which they were joined is described by [P99, Proposition 9 with (28)], which rests on the count of the trees that contain a given forest [P99, Lemma 1, and Lemma 3, there attributed to Moon]. A pattern prescribes ancestor relations between the pieces, which a set of deleted edges does not; apart from this the proof of Theorem 3.2 is the same count. For the depth of a vertex in a forest with a prescribed number of trees, Proposition 3.4, the sources have the case of one tree [MM78, (5.2); AP2, (107)] and, for several trees, the Markov chain of the numbers of vertices at the successive levels [CSP, Lemma 8.6 and Lemma 9.9], without a formula for the depth of a uniform vertex.

### 3.3 The expansion over the vertices above a threshold

Let $k\ge1$ and let $g$ be a weight with $g(t)=1$ for $t\le k$; the threshold weight $g_y$ with $k\le y<k+1$ is of this kind. Let $F$ be a forest on $[n]$ with its virtual root $0$. In this section a vertex is called heavy if $t_v>k$ and light otherwise (this is the notion of Section 3.1 for any threshold $y$ with $k\le y<k+1$). Every ancestor of a heavy vertex is heavy, so the heavy vertices together with $0$ form a tree, the *skeleton* of $F$. Each skeleton vertex $u$ carries, besides its heavy children, light children whose subtrees have at most $k$ vertices; $u$ together with these light subtrees is the *bush* of $u$. Put
$$
S_k(z)=\sum_{t=1}^{k}a_tz^t,\qquad \kappa_k(j)=[z^j]\;z\,e^{S_k(z)} .
$$
Then $j!\,\kappa_k(j)$ is the number of rooted labeled trees on $j$ vertices in which the subtree of every child of the root has at most $k$ vertices, and $\kappa_k(j)=a_j$ for $j\le k+1$, because the restriction is then empty.

**Theorem 3.5** (skeleton expansion) [proved; not found in [J16], [HJ], [FK], [FFK], [MM98], [ABH], [Wa12], [Wa], which were read in full with this statement; the sources [J12], [P99], [Pa], [J06] and the chapters of [CSP] read are not counted among these: they were read in full with other statements of this paper and not with this one, and the record of those readings shows only that no weight on fringe subtree sizes occurs in them; not verified against the literature on hook length formulas [CGG], [KP], [JY], read in part]. Let $g(t)=1$ for $t\le k$. Then
$$
\frac{Z_n[g]}{n!}=\sum_{\mathfrak p}\ \frac{1}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\mathfrak p)|}\ \prod_{u\in\mathfrak p}\kappa_k(\gamma_u)\ \prod_{u\ne\varrho}g(t_u), \tag{3.2}
$$
where the sum is over the isomorphism classes of patterns $\mathfrak p$ (Section 3.2) all of whose nodes $u\ne\varrho$ have type child and size $t_u>k$, and $\mathrm{Aut}(\mathfrak p)$ is the group of automorphisms of $\mathfrak p$ preserving the sizes. If $n\le 2k+1$, the patterns that contribute are paths, they have no automorphisms, and every factor $\kappa_k(\gamma_u)$ except that of the last node equals $a_{\gamma_u}$.

*Proof.* Each forest has exactly one skeleton, and the skeleton with its subtree sizes is an occurrence of a unique pattern class of the kind described; it is counted $|\mathrm{Aut}(\mathfrak p)|$ times as an occurrence. In the bijection of the proof of Theorem 3.2, an occurrence is the skeleton of the tree if and only if in every layer all vertices other than the root of the layer are light, that is, if and only if every layer is a tree in which the subtrees of the children of the root have at most $k$ vertices; a vertex of a layer other than its root has its whole subtree inside the layer, because the pattern-children are attached to the root of the layer. The number of such trees on $\gamma$ labels is $\gamma!\,\kappa_k(\gamma)$, and, with the root prescribed to be $0$, it is $(\gamma-1)!\,\kappa_k(\gamma)$. Counting as in Theorem 3.2 gives $n!\prod_u\kappa_k(\gamma_u)$ pairs (forest, occurrence that is its skeleton) for each pattern, and the weight of the forest is $\prod_{u\ne\varrho}g(t_u)$. If $n\le2k+1$, two heavy vertices cannot have disjoint subtrees, so the skeleton is a path; the layers other than the last have at most $N-(k+1)\le k+1$ vertices in total. $\square$

The theorem says in what way a weight that is trivial below a threshold sees a forest: only through its skeleton. Everything outside the skeleton consists of fringe subtrees with at most $k$ vertices, which are arbitrary rooted trees and carry no factor; they enter through the coefficients $\kappa_k$, which are polynomials in Cayley's coefficients $a_1,\dots,a_k$ and equal $a_j$ as long as a bush has at most $k+1$ vertices [proved: the definition of $\kappa_k$]. Three consequences are used later [proved: each is the theorem applied to the weight named]. The number $Z_n(k')$ is given by the sum (3.2) for every threshold $k'\ge k$, since $g_{k'}(t)=1$ for $t\le k$. A step $d_{n,k}$ with $k\ge2$ involves the thresholds $k-1$, $k$ and $k+1$ and is therefore obtained from (3.2) with $k-1$ in place of $k$. And for $n\le2k+1$ the sum (3.2) runs over paths, so that, once the last layer is set apart with its coefficient $\kappa_k$, it is a sum over the compositions formed by the other layers, in which every part carries a Cayley coefficient and every cut point a factor; this is the form used in Section 5.4, and the laws on compositions that it defines are the subject of Section 4.3. No expansion of this kind was found in the sources named in the bracket of the theorem; the nearest device in them is the truncation of a toll at a given size [J16, (8.4) and (8.6)], which separates the contributions of the small and of the large fringe subtrees without describing the configuration of the large ones.

### 3.4 The largest fringe subtrees and the scale $n^{2/3}$

Two exponents enter every estimate of this paper. Borel masses decay like $m^{-3/2}$; this is Stirling's formula applied to Cayley's coefficients. The maximum displacement falls short of $n$ by an amount of order $n^{2/3}$; the exact scale with its limit law is Petersson's theorem (Theorem 2.16), and one side of it follows from a first moment over the fringe subtrees of nearly full size (Proposition 3.7).

**Lemma 3.6** (Stirling's formula with a remainder) [proved, from Stirling's formula $m!\sim\sqrt{2\pi m}\,(m/e)^m$, for which see [FS, (32), p. 37, with a proof in Appendix B.6]]. For every integer $m\ge1$ write $m!=\sqrt{2\pi m}\,(m/e)^me^{\varepsilon_m}$. Then $0<\varepsilon_m<\frac1{12m}$ and $\varepsilon_m$ is decreasing in $m$. Consequently
$$
\mathrm{Bor}(m)=\frac{e^{-\varepsilon_m}}{\sqrt{2\pi}\,m^{3/2}},\qquad \mathrm{Ren}(m)=\frac{e^{-\varepsilon_m}}{\sqrt{2\pi m}},\qquad e^{-1/12}<e^{-\varepsilon_m}<1,
$$
the sequence $\mathrm{Bor}$ is strictly decreasing, and $1<\mathrm{Bor}(m)/\mathrm{Bor}(m')\le(m'/m)^{3/2}$ for $m<m'$.

*Proof.* From the definition, $\varepsilon_m-\varepsilon_{m+1}=(m+\frac12)\log(1+\frac1m)-1$. With $u=1/(2m+1)$ one has $\log(1+\frac1m)=\log\frac{1+u}{1-u}=2\bigl(u+\frac{u^3}3+\frac{u^5}5+\dots\bigr)$ and $m+\frac12=\frac1{2u}$, so
$$
0<\varepsilon_m-\varepsilon_{m+1}=\frac{u^2}3+\frac{u^4}5+\dots<\frac{u^2}{3(1-u^2)}=\frac1{12m(m+1)}=\frac1{12m}-\frac1{12(m+1)} .
$$
Thus $\varepsilon_m$ is decreasing and $\varepsilon_m-\frac1{12m}$ is increasing. By Stirling's formula $\varepsilon_m\to0$; hence $\varepsilon_m>0$ and $\varepsilon_m-\frac1{12m}<0$ for every $m$. The formulas for $\mathrm{Bor}$ and $\mathrm{Ren}$ are the definitions with $m!$ written in this form, and $e^{-1/12}<e^{-\varepsilon_m}<1$. Finally $\mathrm{Bor}(m+1)/\mathrm{Bor}(m)=e^{-1}(1+1/m)^{m-1}<1$, and for $m<m'$ the ratio $\mathrm{Bor}(m)/\mathrm{Bor}(m')=(m'/m)^{3/2}e^{\varepsilon_{m'}-\varepsilon_m}$ is at most $(m'/m)^{3/2}$ because $\varepsilon$ is decreasing. $\square$

The bounds $\frac1{12m+1}<\varepsilon_m<\frac1{12m}$ are commonly attributed to Robbins [Rob]; that note was not read, and the attribution is not verified here. Only the upper bound and the monotonicity, proved above, are used.

In the language of generating functions the same fact is the square-root singularity of the tree function, $T(z)=1-\sqrt{2(1-ez)}+O(1-ez)$ as $z\to1/e$ [FS, Theorem VI.6, with the expansion for this function in Figure VI.10, p. 406, and Example VII.3, p. 449]; the exponent $3/2$ is that of all tree-like classes with a singularity of this type [FS, Theorems VII.2 and VII.3]. The exponent $3/2$ is thus the exponent of the size of a critical tree, and $1/2$ is the exponent of the renewal sequence: $\sum_{j\le q}\mathrm{Ren}(j)$ is of order $\sqrt q$ [proved: Lemma 3.6].

For $1\le q\le n$ let $\mathsf H_q$ be the number of cars with displacement at least $n-q$ in a uniform random parking function of length $n$; the event $M\ge n-q$ is the event $\mathsf H_q\ge1$.

**Proposition 3.7** (the number of cars near the top) [proved; differs: [Pet, Corollary 4.10] gives the mean $\frac{4}{3\sqrt{2\pi}}z^{3/2}$ of the limit variable in the window $q=zn^{2/3}$, which is the constant of the asymptotic formula here, without a statement on the convergence of the means; an exact formula or a bound for finite $n$ was not found in [Pet], [J01], [J05], [FPV], [Kn98], [KY], [CM], [MV], which were read in full]. For $1\le q\le n/2$,
$$
\mathbb E \mathsf H_q=\sum_{j=1}^{q}\frac{\mathrm{Ren}(j)\,\mathrm{Bor}(N-j)}{\mathrm{Bor}(N)}\cdot\frac{q+1-j}{N-j},\qquad \frac{q^{3/2}}{6N}\ \le\ \mathbb E \mathsf H_q\ \le\ \frac{8\,q^{3/2}}{N},
$$
and as $q\to\infty$ with $q/n\to0$, $\mathbb E\mathsf H_q\sim\frac23\sqrt{\frac2\pi}\,\frac{q^{3/2}}{n}$. Consequently $\Pr(M\ge n-q)\le 8q^{3/2}/N$: the deficit $n-M$ exceeds $\epsilon n^{2/3}$ with probability at least $1-8\epsilon^{3/2}$.

*Proof.* By Theorem 2.6(b), $\mathbb E\mathsf H_q$ is the expectation over the uniform forest of $\sum_v\Pr(D_v\ge n-q\mid F)=\sum_v (t_v-n+q)_+/t_v$. Grouping the vertices by $t_v=N-j$ and using Corollary 3.3(a) gives the formula. By Lemma 3.6, $1\le\mathrm{Bor}(N-j)/\mathrm{Bor}(N)\le(N/(N-j))^{3/2}$ and $\frac1N\le\frac1{N-j}\le\frac1N\cdot\frac N{N-q}$, so $\mathbb E\mathsf H_q$ lies between $\Sigma/N$ and $(N/(N-q))^{5/2}\Sigma/N$ with $\Sigma=\sum_{j=1}^q\mathrm{Ren}(j)(q+1-j)$. Since $x\mapsto(q+1-x)x^{-1/2}$ is decreasing, $\Sigma\le(2\pi)^{-1/2}\int_0^q(q+1-x)x^{-1/2}dx=(2\pi)^{-1/2}\bigl(\frac43q^{3/2}+2q^{1/2}\bigr)\le\frac{10}{3\sqrt{2\pi}}q^{3/2}$, and $\Sigma\ge\frac{e^{-1/12}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,q^{-1/2}\sum_{j=1}^q(q+1-j)\ge\frac{e^{-1/12}}{2\sqrt{2\pi}}q^{3/2}$. With $(N/(N-q))^{5/2}\le2^{5/2}$ this gives the two bounds; the asymptotic statement follows from $\mathrm{Ren}(j)\sim(2\pi j)^{-1/2}$ and $\int_0^q(q-x)x^{-1/2}dx=\frac43q^{3/2}$. The last assertion is the bound of a probability by an expectation. $\square$

The fringe subtrees that matter in this proof are nested, and the numbers of vertices outside them form a renewal sequence, up to a factor that the next proposition makes explicit. Call $N-t_v$ the *deficit* of a vertex $v$.

**Proposition 3.8** (the chain of the largest fringe subtrees) [proved; differs: the sources have the continuum counterpart, a stable subordinator of index $\frac12$, for the largest block [Pet, Theorem 3.2], for the block of the first car [CL, Theorem 1.8] and for the fragment containing a uniform point [Ber2, Proposition 11], and for a finite table only the law of the first split [Pet, p. 458] and the law, at a fixed time, of the size of the block of a given car [CL, (2.6)]; the finite statement was not found in [Pet], [J01], [Ber], [Ber2], [AP], [Mie], [ABH], [J16], [J12], [MV], [BHJ2], [CL], which were read in full, nor in the chapters of [CSP] read; not verified against [CD], on large fringe subtrees of conditioned Galton–Watson trees, which was not read]. Let $F$ be the uniform random rooted labeled forest on $[n]$, $N=n+1$, and let $1\le j_1<\dots<j_i<N/2$ be integers. The probability that the deficits of the vertices of $F$ with deficit at most $j_i$ are exactly $j_1,\dots,j_i$, each occurring once, is
$$
\mathrm{Bor}(j_1)\,\mathrm{Bor}(j_2-j_1)\cdots\mathrm{Bor}(j_i-j_{i-1})\cdot\frac{\mathrm{Bor}(N-j_i)}{\mathrm{Bor}(N)} .
$$

*Proof.* Join the roots of $F$ to the virtual root $0$. Two vertices with deficit smaller than $N/2$ have subtrees with more than $N/2$ vertices each; these cannot be disjoint, since $F$ has $n<N$ vertices, so one of the two vertices is an ancestor of the other, and their sizes differ. Consider the pattern that is a path $\varrho,u_1,\dots,u_i$ whose $i$ non-root nodes are all of type child and have the sizes $N-j_1>\dots>N-j_i$. We show that the event in question occurs if and only if this pattern has an occurrence in $F$. Suppose first that the vertices with deficit at most $j_i$ are $v_1,\dots,v_i$ with deficits $j_1,\dots,j_i$. They are nested, $v_{l+1}$ being a proper descendant of $v_l$. If the parent of $v_{l+1}$ were not $v_l$, it would be a vertex $w$ strictly between them on the path, with $t_{v_{l+1}}<t_w<t_{v_l}$, hence with a deficit strictly between $j_l$ and $j_{l+1}$, which is excluded; in the same way a parent of $v_1$ other than the virtual root would have a deficit smaller than $j_1$. So $(v_1,\dots,v_i)$ is an occurrence. Conversely, let $(v_1,\dots,v_i)$ be an occurrence, and let $w$ be a vertex with deficit at most $j_i$. Then $t_w\ge N-j_i>N/2$, so $w$ and $v_i$ are comparable; $w$ is not a proper descendant of $v_i$, because then $t_w<t_{v_i}=N-j_i$; hence $w$ is $v_i$ or one of its ancestors in $F$, and these are $v_{i-1},\dots,v_1$. So the vertices with deficit at most $j_i$ are exactly $v_1,\dots,v_i$. An occurrence, if there is one, is unique, because a subtree size larger than $N/2$ is attained by at most one vertex. The probability of the event is therefore the expected number of occurrences, which is given by (3.1): the layers have $j_1$, $j_2-j_1$, \dots, $j_i-j_{i-1}$ and $N-j_i$ vertices, and no node has type descendant. $\square$

In terms of the process, the vertices with deficit smaller than $N/2$ correspond to the cars that landed in a block with more than $N/2$ spots, which is then the largest block [proved: by Proposition 2.4(a) the car at a vertex $v$ of the history forest closed a block with $t_v$ spots, the empty spot at its end included, and this is the block in which it landed (Lemma 2.2); two blocks with more than $N/2$ spots each cannot be present together, because the sizes of the blocks add up to $N$; and the shape of the history forest has the law of the shape of the uniform forest (Theorem 2.6(c))]. A car that lands in a small block next to the largest one also enlarges the largest block, but the block it closes is the small one. So Proposition 3.8 says that, read from the end of the process backwards and as long as the largest block has more than $N/2$ spots, the numbers of spots that this block lacks at the successive moments at which a car lands in it are distributed as the partial sums of independent Borel steps, except that the probability of a given initial sequence $j_1<\dots<j_i$ carries the factor $\mathrm{Bor}(N-j_i)/\mathrm{Bor}(N)$, which is close to $1$ when $j_i$ is small compared with $N$ (Lemma 3.6) [proved: Proposition 3.8 with this identification; a car arrives after all the cars in its subtree (Proposition 2.4(b)), so larger deficits belong to earlier moments]. The restriction is the hypothesis $j_i<N/2$ of the proposition. The steps are the numbers of spots that the largest block gains between two such moments; they are not the sizes of the small blocks present at one moment, whose joint law is that of Theorem 2.9. The order of magnitude of $\mathbb E\mathsf H_q$ can be read from this description [proof sketch; the steps that are missing are the uniform estimates, and the statement that is proved is Proposition 3.7]: the probability that such a walk visits $j$ is $\mathrm{Ren}(j)$ (Corollary 3.3(d)), of order $j^{-1/2}$, so about $\sqrt q$ of these moments have a deficit at most $q$; a car closing a block of deficit $j$ has a displacement of at least $n-q$ with probability $(q+1-j)/(N-j)$ (Lemma 2.2), which is about $(q-j)/n$; and the product of the number of chances, $\sqrt q$, and the size of each chance, $q/n$, is $q^{3/2}/n$, which is of order $1$ when $q$ is of order $n^{2/3}$.

**Remark 3.9** (the published proof of the limit law and its finite counterparts) [the statements attributed to [Pet] are cited; the displayed identity is (2.4), proved; the comparison of the two conditional probabilities is proved in the remark]. Petersson's proof of Theorem 2.16 follows the largest block. The items are removed in the reverse order of their arrival, and an item is called *marked* if it belongs to the largest block at the moment of its removal. The proof has three steps.

First, the deficit of the largest block, as a process in the number of items removed, converges after rescaling to the stable subordinator of index $\frac12$ [Pet, Theorem 3.2, proved with Lemmas 3.3 to 3.5; the distributional input is a theorem of Pavlov on the largest tree of a random forest, quoted there as Theorem 3.1]. The inverse of this subordinator has the law of the running maximum of a Brownian motion [Pet, pp. 471–472, where a textbook is cited for it].

Second, given the path of the sizes of the largest block, the events that the successive marked items have a displacement of at least $m-zm^{2/3}$, in a table of $m$ cells, are independent, each with probability $(\eta'-m+zm^{2/3})_+/\eta$, where $\eta$ and $\eta'$ are the sizes of the largest block before and after the removal (the independence is asserted there in one sentence); the number of these events that occur is then approximated by a Poisson variable through Le Cam's inequality [Pet, Lemma 4.2 and its proof, pp. 468–470].

Third, the number of items with such a displacement converges in distribution to a variable that, given the limit process $(\mathsf S_t)$, is Poisson with the random parameter $\int_0^\infty(z-\mathsf S_t)_+\,dt$, and this parameter has the law of $z^{3/2}\mathcal B$ [Pet, Theorems 4.1 and 4.5 and pp. 471–472]; and the maximum displacement is at least $m-zm^{2/3}$ exactly when this number is positive [Pet, proof of Theorem 5.1].

Let $q<n/2$. In the terms of this paper, the items that can have a displacement of at least $n-q$ are then the vertices $v$ with $t_v>n-q$, since a displacement is at most $t_v-1$; they are marked items, and they form the chain of the giant block. Given the forest, the marks of the vertices are independent, so by (2.4)
$$
\Pr(M<n-q)=\mathbb E\prod_{v:\ t_v>n-q}\Bigl(1-\frac{t_v-n+q}{t_v}\Bigr)
$$
for every $n$ and every integer $q$ with $0\le q\le n-1$ (this needs no restriction on $q$; for $q=n$ both sides vanish), and for $q<n/2$ the product is over that chain. The second step of the proof is the form which this identity takes for $q$ of order $n^{2/3}$, with the conditioning on the sizes of the largest block in place of the conditioning on the forest. For $q<n/2$ the two conditional probabilities are consistent: the one of the proof is the one of the identity averaged over the information that the sizes of the largest block do not contain, namely which of the two parts the removed item closed. Only the case $\eta'>n-q$ matters, and then $\eta'>N/2$, so the larger of the two parts into which the removal divides the block is the largest block after the removal. Given the sizes $\eta$ and $\eta'$, the removed item closed that part with probability $\eta'/\eta$, because a car lands in a block with probability proportional to its size and the two orders of the parts are equally likely beforehand (Lemma 2.2 and the last assertion of Theorem 2.9); in that case $t_v=\eta'$ and its displacement reaches the threshold $n-q$, which stands for $m-zm^{2/3}$, with probability $(\eta'-n+q)/\eta'$, which is the quantity subtracted from $1$ in the factor of the identity. If it closed the smaller part, which has at most $n/2<n-q$ spots, its displacement cannot reach the threshold. The product $(\eta'-n+q)_+/\eta$ is the probability used in the second step. The first step has its discrete counterpart in Proposition 3.8, which rests on the count of chains of Corollary 3.3(b): the deficits $N-t_v$ along the chain form a renewal sequence with Borel steps, up to the factors $\mathrm{Bor}(N-j)/\mathrm{Bor}(N)$, and the tail of the Borel law, $\sum_{m>j}\mathrm{Bor}(m)\sim\sqrt{2/\pi}\,j^{-1/2}$ by Lemma 3.6, has the exponent $\frac12$ of that subordinator. Proposition 3.7 is the statement at the level of first moments: the limit variable of the third step has expectation $\frac{4}{3\sqrt{2\pi}}z^{3/2}$ [Pet, Corollary 4.10], and $\frac{4}{3\sqrt{2\pi}}=\frac23\sqrt{2/\pi}=\mathbb E\mathcal B$ is the constant of Proposition 3.7.

So the limit law, with a proof along the largest block, is Petersson's, and the finite statements of this paper that correspond to its steps are the identity (2.4), Proposition 3.8 and Proposition 3.7. The article [Pet], which was read in full, stands to them as follows [cited]. Its Section 2 describes, in the running text, the backward fragmentation of an almost full table, in which the displacement of each removed item is uniform on its left block and the procedure is repeated inside the blocks [Pet, p. 458]; a product formula over the fragmentation follows from this description at once, but the article writes none, defines no tree or forest from the fragmentation ("no explicit representation scheme needs to be introduced", p. 459), and uses the conditional probabilities only for the marked items in the window of the limit law [Pet, proof of Lemma 4.2, p. 469]. For a finite table it gives the law of the first split only [Pet, p. 458]; the sizes of the largest block are described as a process in the limit [Pet, Theorem 3.2]. Where Proposition 3.7 has an exact formula and two-sided bounds for every $n$, the article has the mean and the variance of the limit variable [Pet, Corollary 4.10], without a statement on the convergence of the means. On the law of the maximum displacement itself it contains the limit distribution function, the expectation $2\,\Gamma(\frac13)/3^{2/3}$ of the limit variable and the divergence of its higher moments [Pet, Theorems 5.1 and 5.2]; it has no statement on the probabilities $\Pr(M=k)$, on their monotonicity in $k$, on a density or a mode of the limit law, or on a local limit theorem.


The limit law gives the scale and the limiting distribution function of the row, not the comparison of two neighbouring terms: a sequence of distributions can converge to a unimodal limit without being unimodal [proved: the example of Section 1.2].

### 3.5 An application: the first two moments of the total displacement

For a forest $F$ put $\mathrm{pl}(F)=\sum_{v\in F}(t_v-1)$. This is the number of pairs (ancestor, descendant) of $F$, that is, the sum over the vertices of their distances from the root of their tree (the path length of $F$).

**Proposition 3.10** [proved; the two moments themselves are known: [FPV, Theorem 4 with $m=n+1$] and [Kn98, (5.4), (5.5)]; a decomposition of the mean and of the variance given the sizes of the receiving components is in [MV, Section 4, pp. 41 and 47 to 51]; their expression through the path length of the forest was not found in [FPV], [Kn98], [J01], [KY], [CM], [MV], which were read in full; the exact values for $n\le7$ were also checked by enumeration, Appendix B, item C10]. Let $\mathsf T$ be the total displacement of a uniform random parking function of length $n$, and let $F$ be the uniform random rooted labeled forest on $[n]$. Then
$$
\mathbb E\,\mathsf T=\tfrac12\,\mathbb E\,\mathrm{pl}(F)=\frac n2\sum_{j\ge1}\frac{(n-1)_j}{(n+1)^j},\qquad
\operatorname{Var}\mathsf T=\frac1{12}\,\mathbb E\sum_{v\in F}(t_v^2-1)+\frac14\operatorname{Var}\mathrm{pl}(F).
$$
Both terms of the variance are given by the pattern formula. With $N=n+1$ and
$$
\theta(s)=\frac{\mathrm{Ren}(N-s)\mathrm{Bor}(s)}{\mathrm{Bor}(N)},\quad
\theta_2(s,s')=\frac{\mathrm{Ren}(N-s)\mathrm{Ren}(s-s')\mathrm{Bor}(s')}{\mathrm{Bor}(N)},\quad
\theta_\perp(s,s')=\frac{\gamma^2\,\mathrm{Bor}(\gamma)\mathrm{Bor}(s)\mathrm{Bor}(s')}{\mathrm{Bor}(N)}\ \ (\gamma=N-s-s'\ge1),
$$
one has $\mathbb E\sum_vf(t_v)=\sum_{s=1}^nf(s)\theta(s)$ for every $f$, and
$$
\mathbb E\,\mathrm{pl}(F)^2=\sum_{s}(s-1)^2\theta(s)+2\sum_{s>s'}(s-1)(s'-1)\,\theta_2(s,s')+\sum_{s,s'}(s-1)(s'-1)\,\theta_\perp(s,s').
$$
Here the first sum is over $1\le s\le n$, the second over $1\le s'<s\le n$, and the third over all $s,s'\ge1$ with $s+s'\le n$. For example $\mathbb E\mathsf T=\frac13,\frac{15}{16},\frac{222}{125}$ and $\operatorname{Var}\mathsf T=\frac29,\frac{207}{256},\frac{29716}{15625}$ for $n=2,3,4$.

*Proof.* By Theorem 2.6(b), $\mathsf T$ has the law of $\sum_vD_v$, where, given $F$, the $D_v$ are independent and uniform on $\{0,\dots,t_v-1\}$, with mean $(t_v-1)/2$ and variance $(t_v^2-1)/12$. Hence $\mathbb E[\mathsf T\mid F]=\mathrm{pl}(F)/2$ and $\operatorname{Var}[\mathsf T\mid F]=\sum_v(t_v^2-1)/12$, and the formula for the variance is the decomposition of a variance into the mean of the conditional variance and the variance of the conditional mean. For the mean, join the roots of $F$ to the virtual root $0$; then $\mathrm{pl}(F)=\sum_{v\ne0}(\operatorname{dist}(v,0)-1)$. By Corollary 3.3(c) the distance $D$ of a uniform vertex of the tree on $N$ vertices from its root has $\mathbb E D=\sum_{d\ge1}(N-1)_d/N^d$, so $\mathbb E\,\mathrm{pl}(F)=N\,\mathbb ED-n=N\sum_{d\ge2}(N-1)_d/N^d=n\sum_{j\ge1}(n-1)_j/(n+1)^j$. The expression for $\mathbb E\sum_vf(t_v)$ is Corollary 3.3(a). For $\mathbb E\,\mathrm{pl}(F)^2$, expand the square of $\sum_v(t_v-1)$ into the sum over ordered pairs of vertices: a pair consists of one vertex twice, of two nested vertices (in two orders), or of two vertices with disjoint subtrees. The expected numbers of such pairs with prescribed subtree sizes are given by Theorem 3.2 for the patterns consisting of one node, of a path of two nodes, and of two nodes attached to the root, all of type descendant; these are $\theta$, $\theta_2$ and $\theta_\perp$. $\square$

The first term of the variance is at most of order $n^{5/2}$ [proved]: by Lemma 3.6, $\theta(s)\le e^{1/12}(2\pi)^{-1/2}N^{3/2}s^{-3/2}(N-s)^{-1/2}$, so that $\sum_{s=1}^ns^2\theta(s)\le e^{1/12}(2\pi)^{-1/2}N^{2}\sum_{s=1}^n(N-s)^{-1/2}\le2e^{1/12}(2\pi)^{-1/2}N^{5/2}$. The variance itself is of order $n^3$, by the classical analysis of linear probing hashing [proved from the formula cited]. For a table of $n$ cells with $n-1$ keys, the $r$-th moment of the total displacement is $\frac{2\sqrt\pi}{\Gamma((3r-1)/2)}C_r\,(n/2)^{3r/2}\bigl(1+O(n^{-1/2})\bigr)$ with $C_1=\frac12$ and $C_2=\frac54$ [FPV, (24), p. 501, with Table 1]. Since $\Gamma(1)=1$ and $\Gamma(\frac52)=\frac34\sqrt\pi$, the first two moments are $\sqrt\pi\,(n/2)^{3/2}$ and $\frac{10}3(n/2)^3$ up to the factor $1+O(n^{-1/2})$, so that the variance is $(\frac{10}3-\pi)(n/2)^3+O(n^{5/2})$, with $\frac{10}3-\pi>0$; applying the formula with $n+1$ in place of $n$, that is, to $n$ keys in $n+1$ cells, does not change the leading term, since $((n+1)/2)^3=(n/2)^3(1+O(1/n))$. Hence $\operatorname{Var}\mathsf T\sim\frac{10-3\pi}{24}n^3$. The number $\frac{10}3-\pi$ is the variance of the Airy distribution, the limit law of the total displacement divided by $(n/2)^{3/2}$ [FPV, Theorem 3]; the source prints the constant $\frac{10-3\pi}{24}$ for the completely full table only, in its Theorem 2. It follows that $\operatorname{Var}\mathsf T\sim\frac14\operatorname{Var}\mathrm{pl}(F)$: the randomness that remains once the forest is known contributes a lower order. The formulas agree with that analysis: the exact mean and second moment of the total displacement for $n$ keys in a table of $n+1$ cells are the case $m=n+1$ of [FPV, Theorem 4], whose mean is the closed form above and whose values for $n=2,3,4$ coincide with those given here; numerically the exact variance divided by $n^3$ is $0.02467$, $0.02432$, $0.02414$ for $n=50,100,200$, against $\frac{10-3\pi}{24}=0.02397\ldots$ [computational evidence: the exact variance, with the final division in floating point; Appendix B, item C10].


## 4. Boltzmann models and Gibbs consistency

The statements of Sections 2 and 3 about sizes can be read in two standard languages. In the language of Boltzmann models, the Borel masses are the size law of a random rooted labeled tree of random size, and the uniform objects of a fixed size are obtained from it by conditioning. In the language of Gibbs partitions, the sizes of the blocks of the parking process at a given time, taken without their positions, are the block sizes of a random partition with a product weight, and the process in time, read backwards, is a Gibbs fragmentation. Section 4.1 recalls both and says which statements of this paper they contain; nothing is proved there. The section then proves two statements of consistency, which say how far such product forms persist at two levels finer than the multiset of the block sizes: for laws on trees that carry a weight on each fringe subtree size, under the deletion of uniform edges (Section 4.2), and for compositions, in which the blocks keep their positions, under the removal of one cut at a time, which read in the reverse order is a refinement by one cut at a time (Section 4.3). Both are stated in the form of the theorems of Berestycki and Pitman on Gibbs fragmentations [BP, Theorem 2 and Corollary 3], as an equivalence between the preservation of a family of laws by a rule and a form of the weights, and each is compared with those theorems after its proof.

### 4.1 The Boltzmann model of rooted labeled trees and the Gibbs form of the block sizes

*The Boltzmann model.* Let $\mathcal C$ be a class of labeled objects with $C_n$ objects of size $n$ and exponential generating function $\hat C(x)=\sum_nC_nx^n/n!$. The *Boltzmann model* of parameter $x$ gives an object $\gamma$ of size $|\gamma|$ the probability $x^{|\gamma|}/(|\gamma|!\,\hat C(x))$. The size $N$ of the random object then has the law $\Pr_x(N=n)=C_nx^n/(n!\,\hat C(x))$, and objects of the same size are equally likely, so that the model conditioned on its size is the uniform object of that size, for every $x$ [DFLS, Definition on p. 581, (2.3) on p. 582, and pp. 577–578]. The parameter ranges over the interval of convergence of $\hat C$; its right end, the singularity, is admitted when the series converges there and is called the critical or singular value [DFLS, p. 581]. Under the model, an object that is a set of components has a Poisson number of them, with mean the value of the generating function of the components, and the components are independent; an object that is a sequence of components has a geometric number of them [DFLS, Section 4, with Table 4 on p. 596].

For the class of rooted labeled trees, $C_n=n^{n-1}$ and $\hat C=T$, and a tree is a root with a set of trees. Evaluating the definitions for this class gives the following [proved: Lemma 1.1 and Theorem 2.8 in the vocabulary just recalled; the article [DFLS] names this class only as an example of a specification (pp. 605 and 621) and does not write these evaluations]. At the parameter $x=\lambda e^{-\lambda}$ with $0<\lambda\le1$, where $T(x)=\lambda$, a tree with $m$ vertices has the probability $x^m/(m!\,\lambda)=e^{-\lambda m}\lambda^{m-1}/m!$ of (2.6), and its root has a Poisson($\lambda$) number of subtrees, which are independent copies of the tree: the model is the Poisson($\lambda$) Galton–Watson tree of Theorem 2.8. At the critical value $x=1/e$ the size has the law $\mathrm{Bor}$. So Cayley's coefficient $a_m$ and the Borel mass $\mathrm{Bor}(m)=e^{-m}a_m$ are the weight and the probability of the size $m$ in one model; the uniform forest is the set of the subtrees of the root conditioned on the total size; a tree with a marked vertex is a sequence of trees, and $\mathrm{Ren}$ is the corresponding sequence of masses (Corollary 3.3(d)); and Lemma 3.1 says that the number of assemblies of pieces with a fixed total size, divided by the number $p_n$ of forests, is the product of the critical Boltzmann masses of the pieces, multiplied by the size of the piece that contains the label $0$ and divided by $\mathrm{Bor}(N)$, the powers of $e$ cancelling because the sizes add up to $N$. The exponent $3/2$ of Lemma 3.6 is the general exponent of the size of a recursively specified class at its singular parameter [DFLS, Lemma 7.2 and (7.2), p. 618].

*Gibbs partitions.* A random partition of $[n]$ into $k$ blocks has the *microcanonical Gibbs distribution* with weights $(w_j)$ if the probability of $\{A_1,\dots,A_k\}$ is $\prod_iw_{|A_i|}/B_{n,k}$, where $B_{n,k}$ is the sum of these products over all partitions of $[n]$ into $k$ blocks [BP, (9)–(10), p. 5]; a mixture over $k$ of such laws, with mixing weights proportional to $v_k B_{n,k}$, is a $\mathrm{Gibbs}_{[n]}(v_\bullet,w_\bullet)$ partition in the notation of [CSP, Section 1.5, (1.47)–(1.48)]. The sizes of the blocks, in uniformly random order, are distributed as independent variables with $\Pr(X=j)\propto w_j\xi^j/j!$ conditioned on their sum (Kolchin's representation [CSP, Theorem 1.2]). For the weights $w_j=j^{j-1}$, $B_{n,k}=\binom{n-1}{k-1}n^{n-k}$ is the number of forests of $k$ rooted trees, the Gibbs law is that of the partition of $[n]$ into the vertex sets of the trees of a uniform random forest of $k$ rooted trees [BP, Example 4 with (14); CSP, Example 1.6], and Kolchin's variables are Borel variables; this is Pavlov's representation [P99, Proposition 7 and Section 4.1].

In these terms the statements of Section 2.4 read as follows [proved: Theorems 2.9 and 2.10, restated]. After $t$ cars the block sizes of the parking process on the circle of $N$ spots, in uniformly random order, have the law of the block sizes of the microcanonical Gibbs partition of $[N]$ into $q=N-t$ blocks with the weights $j^{j-1}$. As $t$ increases they form the Marcus–Lushnikov coalescent with the additive kernel, the case $b=1$, $c=0$ of the family of coalescents with kernel $2c+b(i+j)$ in [BP, Theorem 2(iii)]; read backwards in time they form the Gibbs fragmentation of [BP, Example 4], which is realised by deleting the edges of a uniform random tree in uniformly random order [P99, Theorems 4 and 5]. The composition of Lemma 4.4, in which the number of parts is free, is the sequence of block sizes of the $\mathrm{Gibbs}_{[m]}(\bullet!,\bullet^{\bullet-1})$ partition, the partition of the points of a uniform random mapping by its trees [CSP, Example 1.9].

Both languages describe laws that are products over components, with a weight that depends on a component through its size alone. Two features of the parking process lie outside them [proved: by the definitions just recalled]. The threshold weight of Section 3.1 is a product over all vertices of a function of the fringe subtree size, so the weight of a tree is not a function of its size; and a partition forgets the positions of the blocks, which the cut points of Section 4.3 keep. The next two subsections determine what remains of the product form at these two levels.

### 4.2 Weights on fringe subtree sizes and the deletion of uniform edges

Under the uniform law the pieces of a decomposition are uniform. Under a weight $\Phi_g$ they are not, for a simple reason: removing the subtree of a vertex $v$ lowers the subtree sizes of all ancestors of $v$ by $t_v$. Reading this backwards gives the rule by which weights pass through the decomposition.

**Proposition 4.1** (attaching a subtree) [proved; elementary; not looked for as a separate statement; not found in [Pa], [P99], [ABH], [J06], [J12] and the chapters of [CSP] read, in which no weight on fringe subtree sizes occurs; not verified against [CGG], [KP], [JY], which treat products over hook lengths and were read in part]. Let $g$ be a weight with values in a field and without zeros (for instance a positive real weight), let $E$ be a rooted forest, $u$ one of its vertices or the virtual root, and $S$ a rooted tree with $s$ vertices on a disjoint label set. Let $F$ be the forest obtained by making the root of $S$ a child of $u$. Then
$$
\Phi_g(F)=\Phi_g(S)\,\Phi_g(E)\prod_{x}\frac{g(t_x+s)}{g(t_x)},
$$
the product being over $u$ and its ancestors in $E$ (excluding the virtual root), the product being indexed by these vertices $x$, with $t_x$ the subtree size in $E$. For the threshold weight $g_k$ the factor of a vertex $x$ is $\max(t_x,k)/\max(t_x+s,k)$; if $s\ge k$ this is $\max(t_x,k)/(t_x+s)$.

*Proof.* Attaching $S$ changes the subtree size of a vertex of $E$ if and only if the vertex is $u$ or an ancestor of $u$, and then by $s$. $\square$

For the uniform law the product is empty of content, the number of ways to attach $S$ to a forest $E$ on $m$ vertices is $m+1$ whatever $E$ is, and this is why, when an edge of a uniform tree is deleted, the two pieces are independent uniform trees once their sets of labels are given (the case of a constant weight in the theorem below). For which weights does this persist?

**Theorem 4.2** (weights on fringe subtree sizes compatible with the deletion of uniform edges) [proved; differs: the theorems of the literature characterise weights on the numbers of children, [Pa, Lemma 1] and [BP, Corollary 3]; for the constant weight conditions (ii) and (iii) are known, in a stronger form, [P99, Theorem 4] with [CSP, p. 220]; for the weight $c/s$ condition (ii) is known in the form in which the pieces are described given their sizes: it is the splitting property of random recursive trees, [BB, Proposition 1], attributed there to [MM], which could not be obtained; the equivalence for weights on fringe subtree sizes was not found in [Pa], [FKP], [BB], [PP], [KuP], [J06], [P99], [ABH], [AP], [HJ], [J12], [BP], which were read in full, nor in the chapters of [CSP] read; the last assertion, on the threshold weight, is proved in the paragraph that follows the two proofs and was checked by direct computation for $k\le5$, Appendix B, item C4; see the paragraph after the proofs]. Let $w=(w_s)_{s\ge1}$ be a sequence of positive numbers. For a finite label set $A$ let $P^w_A$ be the law on the rooted labeled trees on $A$ with probability proportional to $\prod_vw_{t_v}$. When an edge of a rooted tree is deleted, the component that does not contain the root is rooted at the lower end of the deleted edge. The following conditions are equivalent.

(i) Either $w_s=c$ for all $s$, or $w_s=c/s$ for all $s$, with a constant $c>0$.

(ii) For every $m\ge2$ and every partition of $[m]$ into non-empty sets $A$ and $B$: if a tree is drawn from $P^w_{[m]}$ and one of its edges, chosen uniformly, is deleted, then conditionally on the two components having vertex sets $A$ and $B$ they are independent with laws $P^w_A$ and $P^w_B$.

(iii) For every $m\ge2$ and every $2\le k\le m$: if a tree is drawn from $P^w_{[m]}$ and $k-1$ of its edges, chosen by uniform sampling without replacement, are deleted, then conditionally on the vertex sets $A_1,\dots,A_k$ of the $k$ components, the components are independent with laws $P^w_{A_1},\dots,P^w_{A_k}$.

Moreover, for an integer $k\ge2$ the threshold weight $w_s=\min(1,k/s)$, which satisfies none of these conditions, has the property of condition (ii) for the trees with $m\le k+1$ vertices and fails to have it for every $m\ge k+2$.

*Proof that (i) and (ii) are equivalent.* In this part of the proof, "the property" means condition (ii). For a rooted tree $\tau$ with $a$ vertices and an integer $b\ge1$ put

$$
G_b(\tau)=\sum_{u\in\tau}\ \prod_{v\ \text{on the path from the root to}\ u}\frac{w_{t_v+b}}{w_{t_v}} .
$$
By Proposition 4.1, if $\tau$ and $\tau'$ are trees on $A$ and $B$ with $a$ and $b$ vertices, the total weight of the trees on $[m]$ from which the deletion of one edge produces the pair $(\tau,\tau')$ is $\prod_vw_{t_v(\tau)}\prod_vw_{t_v(\tau')}\,\bigl(G_b(\tau)+G_a(\tau')\bigr)$: the first term for $\tau'$ attached below a vertex of $\tau$, the second for $\tau$ attached below a vertex of $\tau'$. Every tree on $[m]$ has $m-1$ edges. Hence the property holds if and only if, for every $b$, $G_b(\tau)$ depends on $\tau$ only through its number of vertices $a$; write $\pi_b(a)$ for the common value.

Splitting according to whether $u$ is the root or lies in the subtree $\tau_i$ of a child of the root gives
$$
G_b(\tau)=\frac{w_{a+b}}{w_a}\Bigl(1+\sum_iG_b(\tau_i)\Bigr). \tag{4.1}
$$
Assume the property. Comparing, among the trees with $a+1$ vertices, the one whose root has a single child with a subtree of $a$ vertices and the one whose root has $a$ leaves as children, (4.1) gives $\pi_b(a)=a\,\pi_b(1)$. Inserting this into (4.1) for an arbitrary tree with $a$ vertices gives $a\pi_b(1)=\frac{w_{a+b}}{w_a}\bigl(1+(a-1)\pi_b(1)\bigr)$, that is,
$$
\frac{w_{a+b}}{w_a}=\frac{a}{a+\delta_b},\qquad \delta_b=\frac1{\pi_b(1)}-1 .
$$
Write $\delta=\delta_1$. Going from $a$ to $a+2$ in two steps or in one gives $(a+1)(a+\delta_2)=(a+\delta)(a+1+\delta)$ for all $a\ge1$; comparing coefficients, $\delta_2=2\delta$ and $\delta_2=\delta(\delta+1)$, so $\delta\in\{0,1\}$. If $\delta=0$ then $w$ is constant; if $\delta=1$ then $w_{a+1}/w_a=a/(a+1)$ and $w_s=w_1/s$.

Conversely, for constant $w$ one has $G_b(\tau)=a$. For $w_s=c/s$ the claim $G_b(\tau)=a/(b+1)$ follows from (4.1) by induction: $\frac{a}{a+b}\bigl(1+\frac{a-1}{b+1}\bigr)=\frac a{b+1}$. $\square$

*Proof that (ii) and (iii) are equivalent.* Condition (ii) is the case $k=2$ of (iii). Conversely assume (ii); it then holds with any finite label set in place of $[m]$, because the laws $P^w$ depend on the labels only through the shape of the tree. Deleting $k-1$ edges chosen by uniform sampling without replacement is the same as deleting edges one at a time, each chosen uniformly among the edges still present. Let $\mathcal A_j$ be the partition of $[m]$ into the vertex sets of the components after $j-1$ deletions. We show by induction on $k$ that, conditionally on $\mathcal A_2,\dots,\mathcal A_k$, the components after $k-1$ deletions are independent with the laws $P^w$ on their vertex sets; since this conditional law depends on $\mathcal A_k$ only, it is also the conditional law given $\mathcal A_k$, which is (iii). For $k=2$ the statement is (ii). Suppose it holds for some $k<m$, and delete one more edge. A component with vertex set $B$ has $|B|-1$ edges whatever its shape, so, given $\mathcal A_2,\dots,\mathcal A_k$ and the components, the edge lies in the component with vertex set $B$ with probability $(|B|-1)/(m-k)$, and given this it is a uniformly chosen edge of that component. That component has the law $P^w_B$ and is independent of the others. By (ii) for the label set $B$, conditionally on its two pieces having vertex sets $A$ and $B\setminus A$ the pieces are independent with laws $P^w_A$ and $P^w_{B\setminus A}$; the other components are unchanged and independent of the two pieces. As $\mathcal A_{k+1}$ is determined by $\mathcal A_k$, $B$ and $A$, this is the statement for $k+1$. $\square$

The constant weight is the uniform law on rooted labeled trees. For it the property is part of a theorem of Pitman [P99, Theorem 4], which says more: the forest that results from the cut is uniform among the forests with two trees, the sizes of the two pieces included. The weight $c/s$ gives, by Lemma 2.5, the law of the shape of a uniform decreasing tree (a random recursive tree), for which the splitting property is well known (it is stated and proved in [BB, Proposition 1], given the sizes of the pieces, with an attribution to Meir and Moon [MM], an article that could not be obtained; in the form given the vertex sets it is the case $w_s=c/s$ of the theorem above). For this weight the property holds only in the conditional form, given the vertex sets of the pieces, in which conditions (ii) and (iii) state it: the forest that results from the cut does not have the weighted law on the forests with two trees. For example, if a rooted tree on $\{1,2,3,4\}$ is drawn with probability proportional to $\prod_v1/t_v$ and a uniform edge is deleted, the result is the forest in which the vertices $1$ and $2$ are roots and $3,4$ are children of $1$ with probability $7/216$, whereas under the law proportional to $\prod_v1/t_v$ on the forests with two trees this forest has probability $1/33$: the sizes of the pieces are distributed differently (Appendix B, item C4). The threshold weight $g_k(s)=\min(1,k/s)$ is the first of the two weights on subtrees of size at most $k$ and $k$ times the second on larger ones. It interpolates between the two cases in which cutting is harmless, and for $k\ge2$ it is neither: the property holds for trees with at most $k+1$ vertices and fails for trees with $m\ge k+2$ vertices. Indeed, with $b=1$ and $a=m-1\ge k+1$, put $r(t)=w_{t+1}/w_t$, so that $r(t)=1$ for $t<k$ and $r(t)=t/(t+1)$ for $t\ge k$; by (4.1) the tree in which the root has $a-1$ leaves as children has $G_1=a\,r(a)$, the tree in which the root has one child carrying $a-2$ leaves has $G_1=r(a)\bigl(1+(a-1)r(a-1)\bigr)$, and these differ because $r(a-1)<1$. For $m\le k+1$ the only factor different from $1$ is that of the root, which depends only on the sizes. (For $k=2,3,4,5$ the smallest sizes at which the property fails are $4,5,6,7$, also by direct computation; Appendix B, item C4.) So under the tilted law of Section 3.1 the pieces of a cut at a uniform edge are not independent members of the same family, and the weights have to be carried through the decomposition by Proposition 4.1; this is what Section 5 does. The cut at the top is different: for every weight $g$, removing the root of a tree drawn with probability proportional to $\Phi_g$ leaves a forest drawn with probability proportional to $\Phi_g$, because the factor of the root depends only on the size, and the trees of such a forest are independent given their vertex sets. This is the content of Proposition 2.11. The decomposition in the order of arrival is thus compatible with every subtree-size weight, and the decomposition at a uniform edge with only two.

*Theorem 4.2 and the literature.* The property of condition (ii) has a name: for families of random trees indexed by their size it is the *randomness-preservation property* of Panholzer [Pa, p. 213], also called a consistency relation [ABH, pp. 3–4]. For the constant weight, that is, for uniform Cayley trees, conditions (ii) and (iii) are known, and in a stronger form: the forest obtained from a uniform rooted labeled tree by deleting $k-1$ uniformly chosen edges is uniform among all rooted forests with $k$ trees [P99, Theorem 4], and its components are independent uniform trees given their vertex sets [CSP, p. 220]. The characterisations in the literature concern weights of another kind. In a simply generated family the weight of a tree is the product over its vertices of a function of the number of children.
