Panholzer proved that such a family has the randomness-preservation property if and only if its degree-weight generating function is of one of three forms, $\phi_0e^{\alpha_0t}$, $\phi_0(1+\alpha_0t/d)^d$ or a negative power of a linear function [Pa, Lemma 1]; Berestycki and Pitman proved, for a conditioned Galton–Watson tree, the equivalence of a condition on the offspring law, $p_j/p_0=\frac1{j!}\prod_{i=1}^{j}(b-(i-2)c)$, with the statement that cutting one uniformly chosen edge leaves two independent conditioned copies, and with the same statement for $k-1$ edges [BP, Corollary 3]; for all sizes the laws in question are the Poisson, binomial and negative binomial laws. Fill, Kapur and Panholzer start from the same property, which they call randomness preservation, and call the simply generated families that have it very simple [FKP, p. 3, with Table 1]. For families of increasing trees, again with weights on the numbers of children, the class that is called very simple is characterised by three equivalent properties, none of which concerns the deletion of an edge [PP, Lemma 5]; random recursive trees belong to it. For the families treated in [KuP], which include this class, the preservation of randomness under the removal of a random edge is stated for the piece that does not contain the root, given its size [KuP, p. 4]. Pitman's forests with a weight $p_a$ raised to the number of children of the vertex labeled $a$ are a further family preserved by the deletion of uniform edges [P99, Theorem 11]. For random recursive trees the property is known as the splitting property: when a uniformly chosen edge of a random recursive tree on $n+1$ vertices is removed, the two pieces, relabeled in increasing order, are independent random recursive trees given their sizes, and the piece that does not contain the root has $j$ vertices with probability $(n+1)/(nj(j+1))$ [BB, Proposition 1], where it is attributed to Meir and Moon [MM]. In Theorem 4.2 the weight of a tree is the product over its vertices of a function of the number of descendants, and the form of the statement, a condition on the weights, the property for one edge, the property for several edges, follows [BP, Corollary 3]. The two classes of laws have exactly one member in common that has the property, the uniform law, which is the Poisson case there and the constant weight here. The second solution of Theorem 4.2, the random recursive tree, is not a simply generated tree [proved: by the example that follows]: among the rooted trees on four vertices, the tree in which the root has one child and that child has two children, and the tree in which the root has two children, one of which has a child, have the same numbers of children at their vertices, namely $2,1,0,0$, and therefore the same weight in every simply generated family, whereas their weights $\prod_v1/t_v$ are $\frac1{12}$ and $\frac18$. One difference in what is asserted should be kept in mind: in [Pa] and [BP] the pieces are described given their sizes, and for the uniform law the sizes themselves have the law of the tree sizes of a uniform forest; in Theorem 4.2 the pieces are described given their vertex sets, and for the weight $c/s$ nothing more is true, as the example above shows. That example also bears on a question of [BP]. For the weight $c/s$ the total weight of the trees on a set of $j$ labels is proportional to $(j-1)!$, the number of recursive trees on $j$ vertices, so if the forest after the cut had the weighted law, the partition of the labels into the vertex sets of the two pieces would have the microcanonical Gibbs distribution with the block weights $(j-1)!$. It does not: in the example the partition $\{1,3,4\},\{2\}$ has probability $\frac7{36}$ after the cut and $\frac2{11}$ under that Gibbs distribution [proved: by condition (ii) the forest of the example has the conditional probability $\frac16$ given this partition, and $\frac7{216}\big/\frac16=\frac7{36}$, $\frac1{33}\big/\frac16=\frac2{11}$; both values were also computed directly, Appendix B, item C16]. So the deletion of uniform edges of a random recursive tree is not a fragmentation process that has the Gibbs distributions with the weights $(j-1)!$ at all its stages; whether any such process exists is stated as an open problem in [BP, Problem 3, p. 30].

### 4.3 Compositions with Cayley weights: refinement one cut at a time

The laws of this subsection are laws on the compositions of an integer $m$, that is, on the ways of cutting a segment of $m$ unit cells into consecutive parts. They arise from the uniform forest in the range $n\le2k+1$, where the vertices with more than $k$ vertices in their fringe subtree lie on one path (Theorem 3.5): hold the lowest of these vertices fixed, and let $m$ be its deficit $N-t_v$ (Section 3.4), the number of vertices outside its fringe subtree in the tree with its root; the deficits of the vertices above it are the cut points of a composition of $m$, the parts are the sizes of the layers of the pattern, each of which carries a Cayley coefficient, and the cut point $s$ carries the threshold factor of the vertex with fringe subtree size $N-s$ [proved: Theorem 3.5, last sentence; the explicit formulas, for the positions of the first layer, are (5.5) and (5.6)]. The comparison of such laws for different factors is what the proof of Theorem 5.9 needs, and it rests on the following fact about the laws without factors: they can be refined one cut at a time without moving the cuts already made.

Throughout this subsection a composition of $m$ is identified with its set $S\subseteq\{1,\dots,m-1\}$ of cut points, the partial sums of its parts that lie strictly between $0$ and $m$; its parts are the gaps between consecutive elements of $\{0\}\cup S\cup\{m\}$, and $Q=|S|+1$ is the number of parts. For positive factors $\lambda=(\lambda_s)_{1\le s\le m-1}$ put
$$
\mathcal W_\lambda(S)=\prod_{\text{parts}}a_{\text{part}}\ \prod_{s\in S}\lambda_s, \tag{4.2}
$$
and let $\mathbb P_\lambda$ be the probability law on the subsets of $\{1,\dots,m-1\}$ proportional to $\mathcal W_\lambda$, with expectation $\mathbb E_\lambda$; $\mathbb P_1$ is the law with all factors equal to $1$, and $\mathbb P_\rho$ the law with all factors equal to a constant $\rho$. The total mass of the compositions of $m$ with $q$ parts under $\mathcal W_1$ is, by Lemma 1.1(b),
$$
T_{m,q}=[z^m]T^q=\frac qm\,\frac{m^{m-q}}{(m-q)!},\qquad \sum_{q=1}^mT_{m,q}=[z^m]\frac{T}{1-T}=m\,a_m ,
$$
the last equality by Corollary 3.3(d). For a sequence $w=(w_t)_{t\ge1}$ of positive numbers and $1\le q\le m$ let $\pi^w_q$ be the law on the compositions of $m$ with $q$ parts in which a composition has probability proportional to the product of $w_\gamma$ over its parts $\gamma$, and write $\pi_q$ for $\pi^a_q$. Thus $\pi_q$ is $\mathbb P_1$ conditioned on $Q=q$: the law of $q$ independent Borel variables conditioned on their sum being $m$, and the law of the block sizes of Theorem 2.9 in the order in which that theorem reads them.

The *deletion rule* acts on a composition of $m$ with $q+1\ge2$ parts, that is, with $q$ cut points: choose one of the $m$ unit cells and one of the $q$ cut points, independently and uniformly, and delete the first cut point met when moving from the cell towards the chosen cut point. If the $i$-th cut point from the left has the parts $\alpha$ and $\beta$ on its left and right, it is the first one met exactly when the chosen cell lies in the left part and the chosen cut point is the $i$-th or a later one, or the cell lies in the right part and the chosen cut point is the $i$-th or an earlier one; so it is deleted with probability $\bigl((q+1-i)\alpha+i\beta\bigr)/(qm)$.

**Theorem 4.3** (refinement of conditioned compositions one cut at a time) [proved; differs: [BP, Theorem 2] is the corresponding equivalence for partitions of a set, with a larger family of weights; [Mie], [Ber] and [ABH, Theorem 1.3] treat ordered or cyclic versions with other rules; the rule on a segment and its converse were not found in [BP], [Mie], [Ber], [Ber2], [P99], [ABH], [AP], [J12], [MV], [CL], which were read in full, nor in the chapters of [CSP] read; see the paragraph after Lemma 4.5]. Let $w=(w_t)_{t\ge1}$ be a sequence of positive numbers. The following conditions are equivalent.

(i) For all $m\ge2$ and all $1\le q<m$, the deletion rule maps the law $\pi^w_{q+1}$ on the compositions of $m$ with $q+1$ parts to the law $\pi^w_q$.

(ii) $w_t=c\,\vartheta^ta_t$ for all $t$, with constants $c,\vartheta>0$; equivalently, $\pi^w_q=\pi_q$ for all $m$ and $q$, the laws of independent Borel variables conditioned on their sum.

In particular, for every $m\ge1$ there are random compositions $X_1,\dots,X_m$ of $m$ on one probability space such that $X_q$ has the law $\pi_q$ and the set of cut points of $X_q$ is contained in that of $X_{q+1}$, for every $q<m$: start from the composition of $m$ into $m$ parts and apply the deletion rule $m-1$ times, with independent choices at each application.

*Proof that (ii) implies (i).* The product of the weights $c\,\vartheta^ta_t$ over the parts of a composition of $m$ with $q$ parts is $c^q\vartheta^m$ times the product of the $a_\gamma$, so $\pi^w_q=\pi_q$, and it suffices to treat $w=a$. Let $X$ have the law $\pi_{q+1}$, $1\le q<m$, and apply the deletion rule. Let $\mathbf j=(j_1,\dots,j_q)$ be a composition of $m$ with $q$ parts. The compositions from which $\mathbf j$ arises are obtained by dividing one part $j_i=t$ into $\alpha+\beta=t$, and then the cut point between $\alpha$ and $\beta$ is the $i$-th one. By Lemma 1.1(b), $[z^t]T^2=2(t-1)a_t/t$, so by symmetry $\sum_{\alpha+\beta=t}\alpha\,a_\alpha a_\beta=\sum_{\alpha+\beta=t}\beta\,a_\alpha a_\beta=(t-1)a_t$ for $t\ge2$; for $t=1$ both sides are $0$. Hence the total $\mathcal W_1$-mass that the rule sends to $\mathbf j$, divided by $\mathcal W_1(\mathbf j)$, is
$$
\sum_{i=1}^q\frac{(q+1-i)(j_i-1)+i(j_i-1)}{qm}=\frac{(q+1)(m-q)}{qm},
$$
which does not depend on $\mathbf j$. So the result has the law $\pi_q$. Starting from the composition of $m$ into $m$ parts, which is the only one and has the law $\pi_m$, and applying the rule $m-1$ times gives $X_m,X_{m-1},\dots,X_1$ with decreasing sets of cut points.

*Proof that (i) implies (ii).* Put $\Xi(t)=\sum_{\alpha+\beta=t}\alpha\,w_\alpha w_\beta/w_t$, the sum being over the pairs of positive integers; by the symmetry $\alpha\leftrightarrow\beta$ it equals the same sum with the factor $\beta$ in place of $\alpha$. The computation above, with $w$ in place of $a$, shows that the mass which the rule sends to a composition $\mathbf j=(j_1,\dots,j_q)$, divided by the mass of $\mathbf j$, is $\frac{q+1}{qm}\sum_i\Xi(j_i)$. By (i) this number is the same for all compositions of $m$ with $q$ parts, in particular for the compositions $(1,t+1)$ and $(2,t)$ of $t+2$, $t\ge1$. As $\Xi(1)=0$, this gives $\Xi(t+1)=\Xi(2)+\Xi(t)$, hence $\Xi(t)=(t-1)\,\Xi(2)$ for all $t\ge1$. Put $c=\Xi(2)=w_1^2/w_2>0$ and $G(z)=\sum_{t\ge1}w_tz^t$, a formal power series. Since $\sum_{t}\bigl(\sum_{\alpha+\beta=t}\alpha w_\alpha w_\beta\bigr)z^t=zG'G$ and $\sum_t(t-1)w_tz^t=zG'-G$, the relation reads $zG\,G'=c\,(zG'-G)$. With $G=c\,H$ it becomes $zHH'=zH'-H$. Comparing the coefficients of $z^t$ on both sides expresses $(t-1)h_t$ through $h_1,\dots,h_{t-1}$ for $t\ge2$, so a formal solution $H=\sum_{t\ge1}h_tz^t$ is determined by $h_1$. The tree function satisfies $zT'-T=zTT'$ (differentiating $T=ze^T$ gives $T'=T/z+TT'$), and so does $T(\vartheta z)$ for every $\vartheta$; its first coefficient is $\vartheta$. Hence $H(z)=T(\vartheta z)$ with $\vartheta=h_1=w_1/c>0$, that is, $w_t=c\,\vartheta^ta_t$. $\square$

**Lemma 4.4** (the number of parts under $\mathbb P_1$) [proved; the law of $Q$ is known: it is the law of the number of cyclic points of a uniform random mapping, [P99, (21)], attributed there to Harris, and [CSP, Example 1.9 with (9.5)], and of one plus the depth of a uniform vertex of a uniform rooted tree (Corollary 3.3(c)); the moment identities and the bounds for $D_m$ are computed here]. Under $\mathbb P_1$ on the compositions of $m$: $\Pr(Q\ge j)=P_j:=(m)_j/m^j$ for $1\le j\le m$. With $s_m=\mathbb EQ=\sum_{j=1}^mP_j$,
$$
\mathbb E\,Q(Q-1)=2(m-s_m),\qquad\mathbb E\,Q(Q-1)(Q-2)=3\bigl((m+2)s_m-3m\bigr),
$$
and for $m\ge2$ the number $D_m=\mathbb E\,Q(Q-1)(Q-2)/\mathbb E\,Q(Q-1)$ satisfies $D_m\le\frac32(s_m-1)\le\frac32\sqrt{\pi m/2}<\frac{19}{10}\sqrt m$ and $D_m\le D_{m+1}$.

*Proof.* $\Pr(Q=q)=T_{m,q}/(ma_m)=\frac qmP_q=P_q-P_{q+1}$, which gives the tail. Hence $\sum_jjP_j=m\sum_j\Pr(Q=j)=m$ and $\sum_jj^2P_j=m\,\mathbb EQ=ms_m$, and summation by parts gives $\mathbb EQ(Q-1)=\sum_j2(j-1)P_j$ and $\mathbb EQ(Q-1)(Q-2)=\sum_j3(j-1)(j-2)P_j$, which are the two formulas. From $\mathbb EQ^2=2m-s_m\ge s_m^2$ one gets $(m+2)s_m-3m\le(s_m-1)(m-s_m)$, that is, $D_m\le\frac32(s_m-1)$. Further $s_m-1=\sum_{j\ge1}P_{j+1}$ and $P_{j+1}=\prod_{i=1}^j(1-\frac im)\le e^{-j^2/(2m)}$, so $s_m-1\le\int_0^\infty e^{-x^2/(2m)}dx=\sqrt{\pi m/2}$, and $\frac32\sqrt{\pi/2}<\frac{19}{10}$. Finally $T_{m+1,q}/T_{m,q}=m(1+\frac1m)^{m-q}/(m+1-q)$ is increasing in $q\le m$, so the law of $Q$ for $m+1$ has a non-decreasing ratio to the law for $m$; more precisely, the law for $m+1$ conditioned on $Q\le m$ has a non-decreasing ratio to the law for $m$, and its remaining mass sits at the largest value $m+1$. This persists after reweighting by $Q(Q-1)$, and $D_m$ is the mean of $Q-2$ under the reweighted law; so $D_m\le D_{m+1}$. $\square$

**Lemma 4.5** (comparison) [proved; a consequence of Theorem 4.3 and Lemma 4.4; not looked for in the literature as a separate statement]. Let $\lambda$ be positive factors on $\{1,\dots,m-1\}$. (a) If $\lambda_s\le1$ for all $s$, then $\mathbb P_\lambda(Q=q)/\mathbb P_1(Q=q)$ is non-increasing in $q$. (b) If $\lambda_s\ge\rho>0$ for all $s$, then $\mathbb P_\lambda(Q=q)/\mathbb P_\rho(Q=q)$ is non-decreasing in $q$. In case (a), $\mathbb E_\lambda\phi(Q)\le\mathbb E_1\phi(Q)$ for every non-decreasing $\phi$, and, for $m\ge2$, the same holds for the laws obtained from $\mathbb P_\lambda$ and $\mathbb P_1$ by reweighting with $Q(Q-1)$; in case (b), $\mathbb E_\lambda\phi(Q)\ge\mathbb E_\rho\phi(Q)$.

*Proof.* $\mathbb P_\lambda(Q=q)$ is proportional to $T_{m,q}\,\mathbb E\prod_{s\in S(X_q)}\lambda_s$, with $X_q$ as in Theorem 4.3 and $S(X_q)$ its set of cut points. Since $S(X_q)\subseteq S(X_{q+1})$, the product does not increase with $q$ if all factors are at most $1$; this is (a). For (b) apply the same argument to the factors $\lambda_s/\rho\ge1$, noting that $\mathcal W_\lambda(S)=\mathcal W_\rho(S)\prod_{s\in S}(\lambda_s/\rho)$. If two laws on a finite set of integers have a non-decreasing ratio $\ell$, then $\mathbb E'\phi=\mathbb E[\phi\ell]/\mathbb E[\ell]\ge\mathbb E\phi$ for non-decreasing $\phi$, because two non-decreasing functions of one random variable are non-negatively correlated; reweighting both laws by the same function does not change their ratio. $\square$

*How these statements are used.* If each cut point $s$ carries an additional factor $\lambda_s\le1$, the number of parts becomes smaller in the likelihood-ratio order, because under the coupling of Theorem 4.3 the product of the factors does not increase when a cut is added; if $\lambda_s\ge\rho$ for all $s$, the number of parts is larger than under the homogeneous factor $\rho$ (Lemma 4.5). With the explicit moments of Lemma 4.4 these comparisons are what the proof of Theorem 5.9 takes from this section; the correlation inequality that it also needs is in Section 5.3.

*Theorem 4.3 and the literature.* The laws $\pi_q$ are classical: they are Pavlov's representation of the tree sizes of a uniform forest [P99, Proposition 7 and Section 4.1], and by Theorem 2.9 the laws of the block sizes of linear probing. Couplings of these laws for all $q$ at once are classical as well, at the level of partitions: when the edges of a uniform random tree are deleted in uniformly random order, the vertex sets of the components form a decreasing family of partitions whose block sizes, put in uniformly random order, have the laws $\pi_q$, and in the reversed process any two blocks may merge, with probability proportional to the sum of their sizes [P99, Theorems 4 and 5; CSP, Theorem 10.1 and Corollary 10.2; BP, Example 4]. Theorem 4.3 concerns compositions instead: the parts lie on a segment whose two ends are fixed, only neighbouring parts merge, and the sets of cut points are nested. Three statements of the literature are close to it, and each differs. They are stated here from [BP], [Mie] and [ABH], all three read in full.

The first is the theorem of Berestycki and Pitman from which the form of Theorem 4.3 is taken. For partitions of a set, with a weight $w_j$ on each block of size $j$, the fragmentation that chooses a block with probability proportional to its size minus one and splits it by the Gibbs rule has the microcanonical Gibbs law with the same weights at every stage if and only if $w_j=\prod_{i=2}^{j}(ic+jb)$, and then its reversal is the coalescent with kernel $2c+b(i+j)$ [BP, Theorem 2; CSP, Proposition 1.7]. The family contains $w_j=j^{j-1}$ (for $c=0$, $b=1$), which corresponds to $a_j$, and also $w_j=j!$. Theorem 4.3 has the same shape, a rule that preserves a family of product laws if and only if the weights have a given form, and its proof of necessity proceeds as theirs, by reducing the preservation to a functional equation, here $\sum_i\Xi(j_i)=\text{const}$ and there a relation of the same kind for sums over pairs of blocks [BP, Lemmas 8 and 9]. The objects differ: partitions, in which every pair of blocks can merge, there; compositions with fixed positions and a rule that deletes one cut, here; and the conclusion differs accordingly, a two-parameter family there and Cayley's coefficients alone here.

The second is Miermont's ordered additive coalescent, in which a cluster chosen with probability equal to its mass absorbs a uniformly chosen other cluster and is placed to its left [Mie, Section 2.1]. Its realisation on a bridge with exchangeable increments, cut at its minimum, is a family of intervals that only merge with neighbours as time runs [Mie, Lemma 1 and Theorem 1], and so it is a nested family of the kind considered here. But the origin from which the intervals are read is the minimum of the bridge, so that the first fragment is a size-biased pick [Mie, Corollary 1]; the article states no law of the ordered sizes at a fixed time for a finite number of fragments, no deletion rule on a segment, and no characterisation of weights.

The third is the theorem of Addario-Berry, Broutin and Holmgren that the trees pruned from a uniform Cayley tree as it is cut down towards its root, kept in the order in which they are cut off, form a uniformly random ordered forest [ABH, Theorem 1.3]. The sizes of the trees of such a forest are a composition with Cayley weights and a free number of parts, the law $\mathbb P_1$ of this subsection, and their number has the law of Lemma 4.4; the cuts are added one after another at fixed positions, but the article does not state the law of the forest after a given number of cuts, and it has no coupling of the laws $\pi_q$ for different $q$.

The parking process itself gives a nested family of cut points on the circle in which the composition read from a uniformly chosen empty spot has the law $\pi_q$ at every time (Theorem 2.9). The spot from which it is read is not the same at all times, however: a spot fixed in advance is not empty at all times, and read from the spot that remains empty to the end the composition does not have the law $\pi_q$ (for $N=4$, after one car, the compositions $(2,1,1)$, $(1,2,1)$ and $(1,1,2)$ then have the probabilities $\frac12$, $\frac5{16}$ and $\frac3{16}$) [counterexample; Appendix B, item C15]. The rule of Theorem 4.3 keeps both ends of the segment fixed and passes through the laws $\pi_q$ exactly. The circular rule is the case of equal rates of Bertoin's model of aggregating servers, in which a server chosen with probability equal to its rate aggregates with its successor on a cycle [Ber, p. 345 and Proposition 2] [proved: the two rules coincide when all rates are equal; the article does not make the identification and says that its dynamics "bear some striking similarities with hashing with linear probing" (p. 359)]; that article describes the ranked sizes through a bridge with exchangeable increments and states no law of the sizes in their cyclic order at a fixed time. Chassaing and Louchard state the circular rule as the probability that two given blocks merge at the next arrival [CL, (1.5)] and recover the parking schemes of the earlier times from the final one by erasing edges of their tree, one for each car in the reverse order of arrival [CL, Section 8]; their article, read in full, gives the law of the block sizes at a fixed time in the order in which the blocks were born [CL, Proposition 5.1], and it states no law of the sequence of block sizes read from a fixed place at the earlier times, no rule that deletes one cut and preserves a product law, and no characterisation of weights.

*An open point.* Under $\mathbb P_1$ the cut points are those of a renewal sequence with Borel steps conditioned to hit $m$, and the Borel law has a tail of index $\frac12$ (Lemma 3.6). Whether the coupling of Theorem 4.3 has a continuum limit is asked in Problem 6.6.


## 5. The second difference of the cumulative counts, and the conjecture

By (1.1) the row of $n$ is unimodal exactly when the sequence of steps $d_{n,1},\dots,d_{n,n-1}$ has no negative term followed later by a positive one. The step is the second difference of the cumulative counts,
$$
d_{n,k}=Z_n(k+1)-2Z_n(k)+Z_n(k-1),
$$
so Conjecture P says that the sequence $k\mapsto Z_n(k)$, which increases from $n!$ at $k=1$ to $(n+1)^{n-1}$ at $k=n$, is convex up to some index and concave from that index on.

The standard sufficient conditions for unimodality are log-concavity and its strengthenings [cited]: a log-concave sequence of positive terms is unimodal [St, p. 500]; the coefficients of a polynomial with non-negative coefficients and only real zeros are log-concave [St, Theorem 2, p. 504]; and such polynomials are exactly those whose coefficients form a Pólya frequency sequence, that is, a sequence whose kernel $(n,t)\mapsto a_{n-t}$ is totally positive [Br94, Section 3, with Theorem 3.1, there attributed to Edrei]. They are not available here, and Sections 5.3 and 5.7 say exactly where they fail. The rows are not log-concave for $80\le n\le300$ [counterexample; Section 5.7, item 1]; so no argument that would give the log-concavity of every row, and in particular none through real zeros or Pólya frequency sequences, can prove the conjecture. And the sequence from which all the exact formulas of this paper are built, Cayley's coefficients $a_j=j^{j-1}/j!$, is log-convex, so that the kernels formed from it are not totally positive of order $2$ [proved; Section 5.3]. The same log-convexity has a positive side, which the proofs use: it is the lattice condition of the FKG inequality for the laws on sets of cut points that arise in the first layer [proved: Proposition 5.7].

For sequences that are not log-concave the survey of Stanley names injective arguments, analytic estimates, and, for symmetric sequences only, the methods of linear algebra and of representation theory [St, pp. 501–502, 509–510 and 513–530]; the rows considered here are not symmetric. The section therefore proceeds by direct comparison and by estimates. It writes the step as the difference of two positive quantities, one that counts pairs of vertices above the threshold and one that counts the vertices at the threshold (Section 5.1); proves that the first dominates far on the left and the second far on the right (Section 5.2); records the order properties of Cayley's coefficients (Section 5.3); derives, in the range where the vertices above the threshold lie on one path, exact formulas for both quantities as sums over compositions (Section 5.4); proves that in a wide part of that range a decrease, once it has occurred, continues (Section 5.5); and deduces the reduction of the conjecture to Target T, together with a second, conditional reduction (Section 5.6). Section 5.7 lists the exact facts that close other routes, Section 5.8 treats the regime of a fixed threshold, in which the language is that of meromorphic generating functions, and Section 5.9 gives an exact expansion for the left half of a row. The three theorems are proved in Appendix A; each is preceded here by an overview of its proof.

### 5.1 The step as a curvature minus a loss

For a forest $F$ and a real $y>0$ write $\chi_y(F)=\#\{v:t_v>y\}$ for the number of heavy vertices, and for an integer $k\ge1$ write $\nu_k(F)=\#\{v:t_v=k\}$ for the number of threshold vertices.

**Proposition 5.1** (curvature and loss) [proved; not looked for as a separate statement; not found in [CEHHMPU], [Pet] and [AF], which were read in full; none of them builds a forest from the parking process ([Pet] recalls that hash tables correspond to random rooted forests and uses a theorem of Pavlov on the largest tree of such a forest, without constructing the correspondence)]. Let $1\le k\le n-1$.

(a) $\displaystyle d_{n,k}=\sum_F\Phi_{g_k}(F)\Bigl[\bigl(1+\tfrac1k\bigr)^{\chi_k(F)}+\bigl(1-\tfrac1k\bigr)^{\chi_k(F)+\nu_k(F)}-2\Bigr]$, the sum being over the rooted labeled forests on $[n]$.

(b) The function $y\mapsto Z_n(y)$ is a polynomial on each interval $[j,j+1]$, $j\ge0$ an integer, with $Z_n''(y)=\sum_F\Phi_{g_y}(F)\,\chi_y(F)(\chi_y(F)-1)/y^2$ in the interior, and
$$
d_{n,k}=V_{n,k}-L_{n,k},\qquad V_{n,k}=\int_{k-1}^{k+1}\bigl(1-|y-k|\bigr)Z_n''(y)\,dy,\qquad L_{n,k}=\frac1k\sum_F\Phi_{g_k}(F)\,\nu_k(F). \tag{5.1}
$$

*Proof.* For $k=1$ the threshold $k-1$ is $0$; then $Z_n(0)=0$ by the convention of Section 1.5, in agreement with the formulas below, since every vertex has $t_v\ge1$ and $Z_n(y)$ is a constant times $y^n$ on $(0,1]$. (a) For a fixed forest, passing from the threshold $k$ to $k+1$ multiplies the factor $k/t_v$ of every vertex with $t_v>k$ by $1+\frac1k$ and changes nothing else; passing from $k$ to $k-1$ multiplies the factors of the vertices with $t_v\ge k$ by $1-\frac1k$. Sum $\Phi_{g_{k+1}}-2\Phi_{g_k}+\Phi_{g_{k-1}}$ over the forests. (b) On $(j,j+1)$ the set of heavy vertices of each forest is constant and $\Phi_{g_y}(F)$ is a constant times $y^{\chi_y(F)}$; this gives the first assertion. By Taylor's formula on $[k,k+1]$ and on $[k-1,k]$,
$Z_n(k\pm1)-Z_n(k)=\pm Z_n'(k\pm)+\int(1-|y-k|)Z_n''(y)\,dy$, the integrals being over the two intervals, and adding the two gives $d_{n,k}=V_{n,k}-\bigl(Z_n'(k-)-Z_n'(k+)\bigr)$. The left derivative at $k$ of $\Phi_{g_y}(F)$ is $\Phi_{g_k}(F)$ times $\#\{v:t_v\ge k\}/k$ and the right derivative is $\Phi_{g_k}(F)\,\chi_k(F)/k$. $\square$

So the sign of a step is decided by a competition. The *curvature* $V_{n,k}$ is positive and counts, with weights, ordered pairs of distinct vertices that are heavy for the running threshold $y$ of the integral: pairs of cars each of which closed a block with more than $k$ spots when $y$ lies in $(k,k+1)$, and with at least $k$ spots when $y$ lies in $(k-1,k)$. Vertices with $t_v=k$ thus enter the curvature as well as the loss. The *loss* $L_{n,k}$ counts single vertices at the threshold: cars that closed a block of size exactly $k$. In the language of Section 3.1, $L_{n,k}=Z_n(k)\,\mathbb E_k[\nu_k]/k$ and, for $k<y<k+1$, $Z_n''(y)=Z_n(y)\,\mathbb E_y[\chi_y(\chi_y-1)]/y^2$, with expectations under the tilted law.

A pair of heavy vertices is either nested or has disjoint subtrees. Disjoint pairs can occur only if $n\ge2k$; on the far left their contribution alone exceeds the loss (Theorem 5.3). For $n\le2k-1$ every pair is nested, and the comparison is between the chain of large nested blocks and the one block at the threshold; this is the regime of Sections 5.4 and 5.5.

Formula (a) displays the competition forest by forest: a forest with a threshold vertex and no heavy vertex contributes negatively, one with many heavy vertices positively. The sign of the sum is not decided forest by forest; Counterexample 5.14 gives a single outcome whose own sequence is not unimodal.

The loss is a count of occurrences of a one-node pattern under the weighted law, and Proposition 4.1 evaluates it.

**Proposition 5.2** (loss formula) [proved; not looked for as a separate statement; not found in [CEHHMPU], [Pet] and [AF], which were read in full; none of them builds a forest from the parking process ([Pet] recalls that hash tables correspond to random rooted forests and uses a theorem of Pavlov on the largest tree of such a forest, without constructing the correspondence)]. For $1\le k\le n$, with $L_{n,k}=\frac1k\sum_F\Phi_{g_k}(F)\,\nu_k(F)$ as in (5.1) (the formula defines $L_{n,n}$ as well),
$$
L_{n,k}=\binom nk\,k^{k-2}\sum_{E}\Phi_{g_k}(E)\sum_{u}\ \prod_{x\in P(u)}\frac{\max(t_x,k)}{t_x+k}, \tag{5.2}
$$
where $E$ runs through the rooted labeled forests on $n-k$ vertices, $u$ through the vertices of $E$ and the virtual root, and $P(u)$ is the set consisting of $u$ and its ancestors in $E$ (empty if $u$ is the virtual root). Consequently
$$
\frac{n+1}{k+1}\ \le\ \frac{L_{n,k}}{\binom nk k^{k-2}\,Z_{n-k}(k)}\ \le\ n-k+1. \tag{5.3}
$$

*Proof.* A pair (forest $F$, vertex $v$ with $t_v=k$) is obtained exactly once by choosing the $k$ labels of the subtree of $v$, a rooted tree $S$ on them, a forest $E$ on the other labels and the vertex $u$ of $E$, or the virtual root, below which $S$ is attached. All vertices of $S$ have subtree size at most $k$, so $\Phi_{g_k}(S)=1$ and there are $k^{k-1}$ choices of $S$; by Proposition 4.1 the weight of $F$ is $\Phi_{g_k}(E)$ times the product in (5.2), since $g_k(t+k)/g_k(t)=\max(t,k)/(t+k)$. Dividing by $k$ gives (5.2). Each factor of the product lies between $t_x/(t_x+k)$ and $1$. With all factors equal to $1$ the inner sum is $n-k+1$. With the factors $t_x/(t_x+k)$ the sum over the vertices $u$ of a tree of $E$ with $a$ vertices is $a/(k+1)$, by the last step of the proof of Theorem 4.2 (the case $w_s=c/s$, $b=k$), so the inner sum is at least $1+(n-k)/(k+1)$. $\square$

The factor $k^{k-2}$ is the number of unrooted labeled trees on $k$ vertices: the choice of the root among the $k$ labels cancels the factor $1/k$ of the slope. The two bounds in (5.3) correspond to the two weights of Theorem 4.2: the upper bound is what the formula gives when attaching costs nothing, as under the uniform law, and the lower bound what it gives when every ancestor is charged the factor $t/(t+k)$, as in a recursive tree. For $1<k<n$ both inequalities are strict [proved: the weights $\Phi_{g_k}(E)$ are positive, so it suffices that the inner sum of (5.2) satisfies the strict inequality for one forest $E$ and the weak one for the others; for $k<n$ every forest $E$ has a vertex $u$, whose own factor $\max(t_u,k)/(t_u+k)$ is smaller than $1$, which gives the upper one; and for $k>1$ the forest $E$ without edges has $t_x=1<k$ at every vertex, so that its factors $k/(1+k)$ exceed $1/(1+k)$, which gives the lower one].

### 5.2 Two regions in which the sign of the step is known

**Theorem 5.3** (increase far on the left) [proved; Appendix A.1; not found in [CEHHMPU], [AF], [Pet], [J01], [J05], [Kn98], [KY], [CM], [FPV], [MV], [BEHLMMO], [CTT], [FHMS], [ABHJM], which were read in full; the search of Section 1.2, which is a search by titles and abstracts, returned no such result]. If $k\ge1$ and $n\ge21k^2$, then $d_{n,k}>0$, that is, $b_{n,k}>b_{n,k-1}$.

*Overview of the proof.* Only pairs of heavy vertices lying in different trees of the forest are used. Under the tilted law the trees are independent given their vertex sets, and a tree with $a>k$ vertices contains on average at least $a/(2k)$ heavy vertices; this follows from the last-car recursion by induction. The expected sum of $|A|\,|B|$ over the ordered pairs of distinct trees with more than $k$ vertices is computed from the generating function $F_k$ of Proposition 5.16 through one algebraic identity, and is at least $(n-1)(n-4k^2)/2$. Hence the curvature is at least $Z_n(k)(n-1)(n-4k^2)/(16k^4)$, while the loss is at most $Z_n(k)\,n/k^2$ because subtrees of size exactly $k$ are disjoint.

**Theorem 5.4** (decrease far on the right) [proved; Appendix A.2; not found in the sources named in the bracket of Theorem 5.3, nor returned by the search of Section 1.2]. If $1\le k<n$ and $k\ge4(n-k+1)^{3/2}$, then $d_{n,k}<0$, that is, $b_{n,k}<b_{n,k-1}$.

*Overview of the proof.* Here $n<2k$, so two heavy vertices are nested, and the expected number of nested pairs is given by Corollary 3.3(b): it is a sum of products $\mathrm{Bor}\cdot\mathrm{Ren}\cdot\mathrm{Ren}$, which Lemma 3.6 bounds. The loss is evaluated by (5.2): the forest $E$ has fewer than $k$ vertices and is unweighted, and the cost of the attachment is controlled by the expected sum of the subtree sizes along the path to the attachment vertex, again a pattern count. With $R=n-k+1$, the ratio of the curvature to the loss is at most a constant times $R^{3/2}/k$.

The theorem bounds from one side the position of the first decrease of a row: the loss dominates as soon as $n-k$ is below a constant times $k^{2/3}$, which is the scale $n^{2/3}$ of Section 3.4. That the first decrease does not occur much earlier is not proved; it is the content of Target T below.

**Remark 5.5** (the first two steps and the last step) [proved, using the exact computation of the rows with $n\le300$, script `c13_rows.py`; the inequalities are those stated in [CEHHMPU, Section 6, before Conjecture 6.5]]. For all $n\ge3$, $d_{n,1}>0$; for all $n\ge6$, $d_{n,2}>0$; for all $n\ge2$, $d_{n,n-1}<0$. Indeed Theorem 5.3 gives the first for $n\ge21$ and the second for $n\ge84$, Theorem 5.4 with $k=n-1$ gives the third for $n\ge13$, and the remaining cases are in the computed rows. The exceptions are real: $d_{2,1}<0$ and $d_{n,2}<0$ for $n=3,4,5$ (Section 1.1). These are the inequalities $b_{n,0}\le b_{n,1}\le b_{n,2}$ and $b_{n,n-2}\ge b_{n,n-1}$ which the paper that states the conjecture gives as consequences of its enumerative results "for all $n\ge1$" [CEHHMPU, Section 6, before Conjecture 6.5]; as the rows of Section 1.1 show, they hold from $n=3$, $n=6$ and $n=2$ on, respectively. That paper also identifies $Z_n(2)$, the number of parking functions with all displacements at most $1$, with the Fubini numbers [CEHHMPU, Example 2.8]. In the notation of the limit law, Theorem 5.3 concerns thresholds of order $\sqrt n$ and Theorem 5.4 deficits $n-k$ below about $(k/4)^{2/3}$, asymptotically $4^{-2/3}n^{2/3}\approx0.40\,n^{2/3}$; in the computed rows the first decrease lies in between: for $n=300$ it is at $n-k=35\approx0.85\,k^{2/3}$ (Section 5.6).

### 5.3 Order properties of Cayley's coefficients

The comparison arguments of Section 5.5 and Appendix A.3 apply general theorems, the FKG inequality and comparisons in the likelihood-ratio order, to the laws $\mathbb P_\lambda$ of Section 4.3 on the sets of cut points of a composition, whose weight is (4.2): a Cayley coefficient for each gap and a factor for each cut point. What the general theorems need from these laws are two properties of Cayley's coefficients. The correlation inequalities and the comparison of laws with different factors need the log-convexity proved in this subsection. The comparisons of the number of parts need the refinement of Theorem 4.3, which rests on the convolution identity $\sum_{\alpha+\beta=t}\alpha\,a_\alpha a_\beta=(t-1)a_t$. A law $\mu'$ on the integers is said to be larger than $\mu$ in the likelihood-ratio order if $\mu'(j)/\mu(j)$ is non-decreasing in $j$.

**Lemma 5.6** [proved; known in the weak form $a_ja_{j+2}\ge a_{j+1}^2$: [So, Section 6.1, p. 44] states as a known fact that $\bigl((n+1)^{n-1}/n!\bigr)_{n\ge0}$, which is the sequence $(a_j)_{j\ge1}$, is a Stieltjes moment sequence, and the Hankel minors of order $2$ of such a sequence are non-negative; the monotone ratio and the strict inequality are proved here]. The ratio $a_{j+1}/a_j=(1+1/j)^{j-1}$ is strictly increasing in $j\ge1$. Thus $(a_j)$ is log-convex: $a_ja_{j+2}>a_{j+1}^2$.

*Proof.* The derivative of $x\mapsto(x-1)\log(1+1/x)$ is $\log(1+1/x)-\frac{x-1}{x(x+1)}$, and $\log(1+1/x)>\frac1{x+1}>\frac{x-1}{x(x+1)}$ for $x>0$. $\square$

**Proposition 5.7** (lattice condition) [proved; the implication from log-convexity to the lattice condition is known, for these weights: [Lac, Proposition 3.2 and its proof, (3.13) and (3.14), pp. 8 and 9]; the converse was not found in [St], [Br94], [So], which were read in full; [CCP, after Lemma 4.2] asserts a converse for a related property without proof; not verified against the parts of [Lac] and [CCP] that were not read, nor against [Kar], [BuW], [Lig], which were not read]. Let $(a_j)_{j\ge1}$ be any positive sequence. The weight (4.2) satisfies $\mathcal W(S\cup S')\,\mathcal W(S\cap S')\ge\mathcal W(S)\,\mathcal W(S')$ for all $m\ge1$, all positive $\lambda$ and all $S,S'\subseteq\{1,\dots,m-1\}$ if and only if $(a_j)$ is log-convex in the weak sense, $a_ja_{j+2}\ge a_{j+1}^2$ for all $j\ge1$.

*Proof.* On the Boolean lattice it suffices to verify the inequality for $S\cup\{i\}$ and $S\cup\{j\}$ with $i<j$ not in $S$. If an element of $S$ lies between $i$ and $j$, the two insertions concern different gaps and equality holds. Otherwise let the gap containing $i$ and $j$ be divided by them into lengths $u,v,w$; the factors $\lambda$ cancel and the inequality reads $a_v\,a_{u+v+w}\ge a_{u+v}\,a_{v+w}$, which holds for all $u,v,w\ge1$ if and only if $a_{j+1}/a_j$ is non-decreasing. $\square$

By the FKG inequality [FKG, Proposition 1], a strictly positive law on the subsets of $\{1,\dots,m-1\}$ whose weight satisfies the inequality of Proposition 5.7 makes any two non-decreasing functions of $S$ non-negatively correlated. One consequence is used in Appendix A.3: raising the factors makes $S$ stochastically larger [proved]. Indeed, if $\lambda'_s\ge\lambda_s$ for all $s$, then the law with the factors $\lambda'$ has, with respect to the law with the factors $\lambda$, a density proportional to $\varpi(S)=\prod_{s\in S}\lambda'_s/\lambda_s$, which is non-decreasing in $S$; so for every non-decreasing function $\phi$ of $S$, $\mathbb E_{\lambda'}\phi=\mathbb E_\lambda[\phi\varpi]/\mathbb E_\lambda[\varpi]\ge\mathbb E_\lambda\phi$ by the correlation inequality for $\phi$ and $\varpi$ under the law with the factors $\lambda$. For Cayley's coefficients the hypothesis holds by Lemma 5.6. In words: when the gaps carry Cayley's coefficients, the presence of a cut makes further cuts more likely, whatever the factors attached to the positions.

The same property works against the most direct positivity arguments. A kernel of the form $(n,t)\mapsto a_{n-t}$ (with $a_j=0$ for $j\le0$) is totally positive of order $2$ if and only if the positive sequence $(a_j)$ is log-concave [proved; known: it is the criterion for Pólya frequency sequences of order $2$, [BrT, Proposition 2.5.1, p. 47]; the survey [Br94] refers for it to [Br89, Proposition 2.5.1], a memoir that was not obtained; one direction is used in [St, proof of Proposition 2, p. 503]; the short proof is kept]: for rows $n<n'$ and columns $t<t'$ the minor of order $2$ is $a_xa_y-a_pa_q$ with $x=n-t$, $y=n'-t'$, $p=n-t'$, $q=n'-t$, so that $p<x,y<q$ and $x+y=p+q$, and it is non-negative for all such indices exactly when $(a_j)$ is log-concave. By Lemma 5.6 the sequence of Cayley's coefficients is strictly log-convex, so a kernel built in this way from Cayley's coefficients is not totally positive of order $2$; the same holds for the Borel masses $\mathrm{Bor}(j)=e^{-j}a_j$, because the factor $e^{-(x+y)}=e^{-(p+q)}$ is common to both terms of the minor [proved]. A positive statement of this kind exists for a different array: the triangular array of the numbers of forests of rooted labeled trees by their number of trees is totally positive [So, Theorem 1.1(a)]; it is not used in this paper.

### 5.4 The first layer

A position $(n,k)$ with $k+2\le n\le2k-1$ is said to lie in the *first layer*; equivalently $r=n-k$ satisfies $2\le r\le k-1$. In the first layer two vertices with subtrees of size at least $k$ are nested, a forest has at most one vertex with $t_v=k$ and at most one with $t_v=k+1$, and the positions $(n,k)$ and $(n,k+1)$ can be compared through one polynomial. Throughout this subsection and the next, $N=n+1$.

For a first-layer position $(n,k)$ put
$$
K(y)=K_{n,k}(y)=\sum_F\ \prod_{v:\ t_v>k}\frac{y}{t_v},
$$
the *frozen polynomial*: the set of heavy vertices is frozen at the threshold $k$, and $y$ is a free variable. It is a polynomial in $y$ with non-negative coefficients, $K(k)=Z_n(k)$ and $K(k+1)=Z_n(k+1)$. Define
$$
\begin{aligned}
C_-&=K(k+1)-2K(k)+K(k-1), &\qquad B_-&=K(k-1)-Z_n(k-1),\\
C_+&=K(k+2)-2K(k+1)+K(k), &\qquad B_+&=K(k+2)-Z_n(k+2).
\end{aligned}
$$
For a composition $(\gamma_0,\dots,\gamma_{j})$ of an integer $m$, its *cut points* are the partial sums $\gamma_0,\ \gamma_0+\gamma_1,\ \dots$ strictly between $0$ and $m$. Put
$$
\Lambda_m(y)=\Lambda_m(y;N)=\sum_{(\gamma_0,\dots,\gamma_j)}\ \prod_{i=0}^{j}a_{\gamma_i}\ \prod_{s\ \text{cut point}}\frac{y}{N-s},
$$
the sum being over all compositions of $m$ (with any number of parts).

**Proposition 5.8** (the first layer) [proved; not looked for as a separate statement; not found in [CEHHMPU], [Pet] and [AF], which were read in full; none of them builds a forest from the parking process ([Pet] recalls that hash tables correspond to random rooted forests and uses a theorem of Pavlov on the largest tree of such a forest, without constructing the correspondence)]. Let $(n,k)$ be in the first layer, $r=n-k$. Then
$$
d_{n,k}=C_--B_-,\qquad d_{n,k+1}=C_+-B_+, \tag{5.4}
$$
$$
\frac{K(y)}{n!}=\kappa_k(N)+\sum_{m=1}^{r}\frac{\kappa_k(N-m)}{N-m}\;y\,\Lambda_m(y), \tag{5.5}
$$
$$
\frac{B_-}{n!}=\frac{a_k}{k}\,\Lambda_{r+1}(k-1),\qquad \frac{B_+}{n!}=\frac{a_{k+1}}{k+1}\,\Lambda_r(k+2), \tag{5.6}
$$
with $\kappa_k$ as in Section 3.3. All four quantities $B_\pm$, $C_\pm$ are positive, and
$$
d_{n,k+1}=\frac{B_+}{B_-}\,d_{n,k}-\frac{\mathcal S_{n,k}}{B_-},\qquad \mathcal S_{n,k}=B_+C_--B_-C_+ . \tag{5.7}
$$

*Proof.* The factor of a vertex in $\Phi_{g_{k-1}}$ differs from its factor in the product defining $K(k-1)$ only if $t_v=k$, where it is $\frac{k-1}k$ instead of $1$; there is at most one such vertex. Hence $B_-=\frac1k\sum_F\mathbf 1\{\nu_k(F)=1\}\prod_{t_v>k}\frac{k-1}{t_v}$. In the same way, a vertex with $t_v=k+1$ has the factor $\frac{k+2}{k+1}$ in $K(k+2)$ and $1$ in $\Phi_{g_{k+2}}$, so $B_+=\frac1{k+1}\sum_F\mathbf 1\{\nu_{k+1}(F)=1\}\prod_{t_v>k+1}\frac{k+2}{t_v}$. With $K(k)=Z_n(k)$ and $K(k+1)=Z_n(k+1)$ this gives (5.4), and (5.7) follows from (5.4) by elimination.

Formula (5.5) is Theorem 3.5 for the weight equal to $y/t$ for $t>k$ and to $1$ otherwise. The skeleton is a path with sizes $t_1>\dots>t_j>k$. If $j\ge1$, put $m=N-t_j$, so that $1\le m\le r$; the layers above the last node form a composition $(\gamma_0,\dots,\gamma_{j-1})$ of $m$, each with $\gamma_i\le r\le k+1$ and therefore with $\kappa_k(\gamma_i)=a_{\gamma_i}$; the node $i<j$ has size $N-s$ with $s$ the $i$-th cut point, and the last node has size $N-m$ and layer coefficient $\kappa_k(N-m)$. The skeleton without heavy vertices gives $\kappa_k(N)$.

For (5.6), let $v$ be the vertex with $t_v=k$. A vertex $w$ with $t_w>k$ is not a descendant of $v$, and if it were not an ancestor of $v$ its subtree would be disjoint from that of $v$ and have at most $n-k<k$ vertices; so the heavy vertices are exactly the ancestors of $v$. The forests with such a vertex therefore correspond to the occurrences of the patterns that are paths of nodes of type child ending in a node of size $k$, with no condition on the layers: the layers above $v$ form a composition of $N-k=r+1$, the layer of $v$ is an arbitrary rooted tree on $k$ vertices, and the ancestors of $v$ have the sizes $N-s$, $s$ a cut point. By the count in the proof of Theorem 3.2 the number of forests for a given composition is $n!\,a_k\prod a_{\gamma_i}$. The case of $B_+$ is the same with $k+1$ in place of $k$: the vertices with $t_w>k+1$ are the ancestors of the vertex of size $k+1$. Positivity of $B_\pm$ is clear from (5.6), and that of $C_\pm$ follows from (5.5): for $r\ge2$ the polynomial $K$ has a term of degree $2$ with positive coefficient, so $K''>0$ on $y>0$, and $C_\pm$ are integrals of $K''$ against a positive weight. $\square$

In (5.4) the quantity $C_-$ is the curvature of the frozen polynomial: by (5.5) it counts ordered pairs of distinct parts of a composition, that is, pairs of heavy vertices on the chain. The quantity $B_-$ is the boundary term: the one vertex at the threshold. They play for the frozen polynomial the roles that the curvature and the loss play in (5.1). The two decompositions of $d_{n,k}$ are not the same term by term: $L_{n,k}$ is a jump of the slope at $k$, while $B_-$ is a difference of two values at $k-1$ and contains the factors $\frac{k-1}{t}$ of the ancestors. The sums in (5.5) and (5.6) run over compositions with the same weights $a_\gamma$ of the parts and with cut factors of the same form $y/(N-s)$. They differ in the value of $y$ and in the length of the composed segment, which takes the values $m=1,\dots,r$ in (5.5) and is $r+1$ or $r$ in (5.6). This common form is what makes a comparison possible; the proof of Theorem 5.9 passes between the lengths $r+1$ and $r$ by removing the first unit of a composition (Appendix A.3, Step 2).

*Example.* For $(n,k)=(5,3)$: $K(2),K(3),K(4),K(5)=697,918,1171,1456$ and $Z_5(1),\dots,Z_5(5)=120,541,918,1171,1296$. Hence $C_-=C_+=32$, $B_-=697-541=156$, $B_+=1456-1296=160$, $d_{5,3}=32-156=-124=b_{5,3}-b_{5,2}$, $d_{5,4}=32-160=-128=b_{5,4}-b_{5,3}$, and $\mathcal S_{5,3}=160\cdot32-156\cdot32=128>0$ (Appendix B, item C6).

### 5.5 A decrease continues

By (5.7), if $\mathcal S_{n,k}>0$ and $d_{n,k}\le0$ then $d_{n,k+1}<0$. The cross product $\mathcal S_{n,k}$ is positive when the boundary term grows faster than the curvature, $B_+/B_->C_+/C_-$.

**Theorem 5.9** (propagation of a decrease) [proved; not found in the sources named in the bracket of Theorem 5.3, nor returned by the search of Section 1.2; the proof uses two finite exact computations, script `c08_propagation.py`: the rational numbers $\mathcal M(r,k)$ of Appendix A.3 for $2\le r\le255$, from $k=\lceil r^{3/2}\rceil$ up to the first positive value for each $r$, and the cross product at the $122$ positions with $\mathcal M(r,k)\le0$, all of which have $n\le102$; Appendix A.3]. Let $r=n-k\ge2$ and $k\ge r^{3/2}$. Then $(n,k)$ lies in the first layer and
$$
\frac{B_+}{B_-}>\frac{C_+}{C_-}\ge1 ,
$$
so that $\mathcal S_{n,k}>0$. Consequently, if $d_{n,k}\le0$ at such a position, then $d_{n,k'}<0$ for every $k<k'\le n-1$, and moreover $d_{n,k+1}<d_{n,k}$.

The proof is in Appendix A.3. It rests on the three statements of Section 4.3 about compositions with Cayley weights: the refinement of the conditioned Borel compositions one cut at a time (Theorem 4.3), the birthday law of the number of parts when no factor is present (Lemma 4.4), and the comparison of the number of parts under different cut factors that follows from the refinement (Lemma 4.5).

*Overview of the proof of Theorem 5.9.* The exponent of $y$ in a term of $y\Lambda_m(y)$ is the number of parts. Hence $yK'''/K''$ is the mean of $Q-2$, where $Q$ is the number of parts, under the law that gives a composition its weight in $K$ multiplied by $Q(Q-1)$, the number of ordered pairs of its parts; and since all cut factors $y/(N-s)$ are at most $1$ for $y\le k+2$, the comparison bounds it, for the compositions of each length $m\le r$, by its value $D_m$ under the birthday law, and $D_m\le D_r<1.9\sqrt r$; since $K$ is a sum over the lengths $m\le r$ with positive coefficients, $yK'''/K''$ is an average of the quotients of the single lengths and is at most $D_r$ as well. Integrating, $1\le C_+/C_-\le\exp(D_r/(k-1))$. For the boundary terms, (5.6) gives $B_+/B_-=(1+\frac1k)^{k-2}\Lambda_r(k+2)/\Lambda_{r+1}(k-1)$. Removing the first cell of a composition and the monotonicity in the cut factors that follows from Proposition 5.7 and the FKG inequality give $\Lambda_{r+1}(k-1)\le(1+\frac1r)^r\Lambda_r(k)$, and the comparison with the homogeneous factor $\rho=k/(k+r)$ together with Jensen's inequality gives $\Lambda_r(k+2)/\Lambda_r(k)\ge(1+\frac2k)^{\mu}$, where $\mu$ is the mean number of cuts under the homogeneous law; a moment identity shows $\mu>\frac67\sqrt r-1$ when $k\ge r^{3/2}$. The two bounds combine to $k\log\frac{B_+C_-}{B_-C_+}>\mathcal M(r,k)$ for an explicit rational function $\mathcal M$, which is positive for $r\ge256$ by these estimates and, for $2\le r\le255$, for all $k\ge r^{3/2}$ except $122$ pairs $(r,k)$; at those pairs, all with $n\le102$, the cross product is computed exactly and is positive.

The hypothesis $k\ge r^{3/2}$ is what makes the balance of these estimates positive: the gain of the boundary ratio over $1$ is about $\frac{12}{7}\sqrt r/k$ from the cuts and $\frac1{2r}$ from the Cayley coefficients, and the excess of the curvature ratio is at most $1.9\sqrt r/k$; the balance is positive when $k/r$ is at least of the order of $\sqrt r$.

### 5.6 The reduction of the conjecture, and two conjectures

**Corollary 5.10** (reduction) [proved; it uses Theorem 5.9, whose proof uses the finite computations of the script `c08_propagation.py`; the label is that of Theorem 5.9]. Let $n\ge2$ and suppose that $d_{n,k}\ge0$ for every $k$ with $1\le k\le n-1$ and $k<(n-k)^{3/2}$. Then the row of $n$ is unimodal.

*Proof.* If no step is negative, the row is non-decreasing. Otherwise let $k_1$ be the smallest index with $d_{n,k_1}<0$; by hypothesis $k_1\ge(n-k_1)^{3/2}$. If $n-k_1\ge2$, Theorem 5.9 at $(n,k_1)$ shows that all later steps are negative; if $n-k_1=1$ there is no later step. By (1.1) the row is unimodal. $\square$

The rows of all $n\le300$ have been computed in exact integer arithmetic and are unimodal [computational evidence; Appendix B, item C5]. Therefore:

> **Conjecture 5.11** (Target T) [conjecture; the grounds are stated after the corollary that follows this statement]. For every $n\ge301$ and every $k$ with $1\le k\le n-1$ and $k^2<(n-k)^3$: $d_{n,k}\ge0$.

**Corollary** (Target T implies the conjecture; this corollary has no number and stands in the second table of Appendix C) [proved, by Corollary 5.10, using the exact computation of the rows with $n\le300$, script `c13_rows.py`, and, through Theorem 5.9, the computations of the script `c08_propagation.py`]. Target T implies Conjecture P.

What is known about Target T is the following. It holds for $n\ge21k^2$ (Theorem 5.3) [proved]. The same inequality, asked for smaller $n$ and all $k$ with $k^2<(n-k)^3$, holds for all $95\le n\le300$, and fails for exactly $81$ values of $n\le94$, the smallest being $4$ and the largest $94$; those rows are unimodal all the same [computational evidence; item C5]. In the computed range the first negative step sits just behind the curve $k=(n-k)^{3/2}$ for every $n\ge95$ and moves away from it slowly; the row $n=94$ is the last in which it lies before the curve:

| $n$ | first $k$ with $d_{n,k}<0$ | $r=n-k$ | $k/r^{3/2}$ |
|---|---|---|---|
| 94 | 76 | 18 | 0.995 |
| 95 | 77 | 18 | 1.008 |
| 100 | 82 | 18 | 1.074 |
| 150 | 127 | 23 | 1.151 |
| 200 | 173 | 27 | 1.233 |
| 300 | 265 | 35 | 1.280 |

No step $d_{n,k}$ with $n\le300$ is zero. The curve $k=(n-k)^{3/2}$ is the scale of the limit law: on it $n-k=k^{2/3}$, so that $(n+1-k)/(n+1)^{2/3}$, the normalisation of Theorem 2.16 applied to the position $k$ in place of the maximum, tends to $1$ as $n\to\infty$; and Target T says that the row has no decrease at the positions where the deficit is larger than on the curve. For large $n$ this is what one expects if the mode of the limit law lies below $1$ and the convergence is regular enough to be read off neighbouring terms; neither is proved here.

A second computation, made for Section 6.3 by a script written independently of the one used for item C5 (for these rows both use the recursion (2.7)), carries these facts to $n\le600$ [computational evidence; Appendix B, item C14]: every row with $301\le n\le600$ is unimodal and has no zero step, the inequality of Target T holds in each of them, and the first negative step continues to move away from the curve, with $k=359,453,547$ and $k/r^{3/2}=1.367,1.406,1.418$ for $n=400,500,600$. Target T is nevertheless stated for $n\ge301$, because the corollary above rests on the rows of item C5; with item C14 in its place the statement for $n\ge601$ would suffice.

The propagation theorem covers the part of the first layer behind the curve. The exact computations suggest that the cross product is positive on the whole first layer.

> **Conjecture 5.12** [conjecture; the grounds follow]. $\mathcal S_{n,k}>0$ for every position of the first layer, that is, for all $k\ge3$ and $k+2\le n\le2k-1$.

Grounds: the cross product is positive at all $15{,}252$ first-layer positions with $n\le250$ [computational evidence; item C5]; it is proved for $k\ge(n-k)^{3/2}$ (Theorem 5.9), which contains the two infinite families at the end of the layer, $n=k+2$ for all $k\ge3$ and $n=k+3$ for all $k\ge6$. This is the sharpest proved form; the conjecture is the open form. The proof of Appendix A.3 does not extend to the whole layer as it stands [proved: it uses the hypothesis $k\ge r^{3/2}$ in (A.5) and in its Steps 5 and 6, and already in that range its lower bound $\mathcal M(r,k)$ is not positive at $122$ pairs, which are settled by computation]. Whether the two bounds (A.3) and (A.4) can be sharpened so as to cover the layer is not determined here [open].

**Corollary 5.13** [proved; the label is that of Theorem 5.9]. Let $n\ge4$. Suppose that $d_{n,k}\ge0$ for $1\le k\le\lfloor n/2\rfloor$ and that $\mathcal S_{n,k}>0$ for all first-layer positions $(n,k)$. Then the row of $n$ is unimodal.

*Proof.* The first-layer positions of the row are those with $\lfloor n/2\rfloor+1\le k\le n-2$. If $k_1$ is the first index with $d_{n,k_1}<0$, then $k_1\ge\lfloor n/2\rfloor+1$, and (5.7) applied at $k_1,k_1+1,\dots,n-2$ shows that all later steps are negative. $\square$

Thus Conjecture P follows from Conjecture 5.12 together with the non-decrease of the rows $n\ge15$ on their left halves, $d_{n,k}\ge0$ for $k\le\lfloor n/2\rfloor$, the rows with $n\le14$ being known [proved, by Corollary 5.13, using the exact computation of the rows with $n\le14$, script `c13_rows.py`]. The non-decrease on the left half holds for $n=1,3,13$ and for all $15\le n\le300$, and fails for $n\in\{2,4,5,6,7,8,9,10,11,12,14\}$ [computational evidence; item C5]. Compared with Target T, this route replaces the sign of the step in the part of the first layer before the curve by the sign of the cross product there.

### 5.7 Obstructions: what a proof cannot be

The following facts are exact computations. Each closes a route by which one might hope to prove the conjecture, and each entry says what it refutes and what it does not.

**Counterexample 5.14** (a fixed outcome) [counterexample; Appendix B, item C9]. For the outcome $\sigma=(1,2,3,5,4,6,7)$, with capacities $(1,2,3,4,1,6,7)$, the numbers of parking functions with outcome $\sigma$ and maximum displacement $0,1,\dots,6$ are $1,31,130,222,216,264,144$. This sequence is not unimodal. Seven is the smallest length at which an outcome with this property exists. The same numbers divided by $1008$ are the distribution of $\max_vD_v$ for any forest with subtree sizes $1,1,2,3,4,6,7$. Hence unimodality cannot be proved outcome by outcome or forest by forest: it is a property of the sum.

**Counterexample 5.15** (another offspring law) [counterexample; Appendix B, item C9]. Replace the uniform random forest by the forest formed by the two subtrees of the root of a uniformly random full binary plane tree with $n+1$ vertices ($n$ even), and let the statistic be $\max_v\lfloor U_vt_v\rfloor$ over its vertices, as in Corollary 2.7. For $n=8$ the probabilities of the values $0,\dots,6$ are $\frac{31}{2205},\frac{31}{315},\frac{589}{2205},\frac{47}{245},\frac{11}{49},\frac{5}{49},\frac5{49}$, which decrease from the third to the fourth and increase from the fourth to the fifth; the sequence is not unimodal for any even $n$ from $8$ to $20$. The forest here is a conditioned Galton–Watson forest with another offspring law, and the statistic is defined in the same way, as the maximum of independent uniform marks on the fringe subtree sizes. The counterexample refutes the statement that the representation of Corollary 2.7 implies unimodality for conditioned Galton–Watson forests in general; it does not bear on Conjecture P, in which the offspring law is Poisson.

The remaining facts are stated as a list.

1. *The rows are not log-concave* [counterexample; Appendix B, item C5]. The first failure is at $n=80$: $b_{80,40}^2<b_{80,39}\,b_{80,41}$, and every row with $80\le n\le300$ fails to be log-concave. So unimodality cannot be obtained from log-concavity of the rows, and a fortiori not from real-rootedness of their generating polynomials, which would imply log-concavity [St, Theorem 2, p. 504]. No derivation of this failure from the log-convexity of Lemma 5.6 is given here.
2. *The ratio of curvature to boundary is not log-concave in $k$* [counterexample; Appendix B, item C9]. For all $1\le k\le n-1$ define $C_{n,k}$ and $E_{n,k}$ by the formulas for $C_-$ and $B_-$ of Section 5.4 with the frozen polynomial $K_{n,k}$ of the same $k$; then $d_{n,k}=C_{n,k}-E_{n,k}$ for every position [proved: $K_{n,k}(k)=Z_n(k)$ and $K_{n,k}(k+1)=Z_n(k+1)$ hold for all $1\le k\le n-1$, by the definition of the frozen polynomial], and $d_{n,k}>0$ exactly when $R_n(k)=C_{n,k}/E_{n,k}>1$. For $n=172$: $R_{172}(87)^2<R_{172}(86)\,R_{172}(88)$. So the passage of the ratio through $1$ cannot be controlled by log-concavity in $k$.
3. *The representation is shared by models whose rows are not unimodal.* Besides Counterexample 5.15, the family with spare spots of Proposition 2.13 has rows that are not unimodal for each of the values $r=5,\dots,12$ of its parameter, at the sizes listed in Section 6.3 [counterexample; Appendix B, item C14]. In both cases the statistic is the maximum of independent uniform marks on the fringe subtree sizes of a random forest; for the family with spare spots the marks are the displacements (Proposition 2.13). A proof of the conjecture must therefore use a property of the uniform forest that these laws lack [proved: the two counterexamples]; which property is not known (Problem 6.3).
4. *The proved regions contain no violation in the computed range, and they are far from sharp* [computational evidence; Appendix B, item C5]. For $n\le300$ there is no position with $n\ge21k^2$ and $d_{n,k}\le0$ among the $609$ such positions, and none with $k\ge4(n-k+1)^{3/2}$ and $d_{n,k}\ge0$ among the $2629$ such positions. For $n=300$ the two regions are $k\le3$ and $k\ge284$, while the row increases up to $k=264$ and decreases from $k=265$ on. The region of Theorem 5.4 begins at a deficit of order $n^{2/3}$, which is the scale of the limit law and the scale at which the first decrease is observed in the computed rows; the region of Theorem 5.3 reaches only thresholds of order $\sqrt n$.

### 5.8 The regime of a fixed threshold

For a fixed threshold $k$ and $n\to\infty$ the tool is the exponential generating function $F_k(x)=\sum_{n\ge0}Z_n(k)x^n/n!$, and the language is that of meromorphic functions and their dominant poles [FS, Sections IV.5 and V.2]. The function $F_k$ has two exact descriptions: a Riccati equation, which comes from the recursion (2.7), and a decomposition into a sequence of prime pieces, which comes from the street.

**Proposition 5.16** (the threshold weight: Riccati equation and integrating factor) [proved; differs: for the weight $x^d$ of the total displacement the corresponding equations are [FPV, Lemma 2, (9)] and [Kn98, (2.14)]; the equation for the threshold weight was not found in [FPV], [Kn98], [KY], [CEHHMPU], [AF], which were read in full]. For an integer $k\ge1$ let $F_k(x)=\sum_{n\ge0}Z_n(k)x^n/n!$ and $\Omega_k(x)=\sum_{t=1}^{k-1}(k-t)\,p_{t-1}\frac{x^{t-1}}{(t-1)!}$. Then, as formal power series,
$$
F_k'=kF_k^2-\Omega_kF_k,\qquad F_k(x)=\frac{\eta_k(x)}{1-k\int_0^x\eta_k(u)\,du},\qquad \eta_k(x)=\exp\Bigl(-\sum_{t=1}^{k-1}(k-t)\,\frac{a_t}{t}\,x^t\Bigr),
$$
and for $k\ge2$
$$
\log\eta_{k+1}-2\log\eta_k+\log\eta_{k-1}=-\frac{a_k}{k}\,x^k=-\frac{k^{k-2}}{k!}x^k. \tag{5.8}
$$

*Proof.* For $g=g_k$ one has $t\,g(t)=\min(t,k)$ and $Z_{t-1}(k)=p_{t-1}$ for $t\le k$, so the series in (2.8) is $kF_k-\Omega_k$. The function $1/F_k$ then satisfies a linear equation, which the integrating factor $\eta_k=\exp(-\int \Omega_k)$ solves; $\int_0^x\Omega_k=\sum_{t<k}(k-t)p_{t-1}x^t/t!$ and $p_{t-1}/t!=t^{t-2}/t!=a_t/t$. The second difference in $k$ of the coefficient $(k-t)_+$ is $1$ for $t=k$ and $0$ otherwise. $\square$

The coefficient $a_k/k$ in (5.8) is the normalised number of unrooted labeled trees on $k$ vertices. Identity (5.8) is a property of the truncated linear weights $(k-t)_+$ and holds for any coefficients in place of $a_t/t$; what is specific to the forest is which coefficient appears. The identity does not transfer to $F_k$ itself, because of the denominator in the closed formula.

A *breakpoint* of a parking function $\alpha$ of length $n$ is an integer $j\in\{0,\dots,n\}$ such that exactly $j$ cars prefer a spot in $\{1,\dots,j\}$; the cars preferring the spots between two consecutive breakpoints park exactly on those spots. A parking function of length $n\ge1$ with no breakpoint other than $0$ and $n$ is *prime*.

**Proposition 5.17** (street decomposition) [proved; known: [GS, Section 10] for the decomposition; the count is the case $u=1$ of [GS, Corollary 10.3]]. Cutting a parking function at its breakpoints represents it uniquely as a sequence of prime parking functions on consecutive intervals of spots, with the cars distributed among the intervals, and every car keeps its displacement. Hence, for every $k\ge1$, with $C_k(x)$ the exponential generating function of the prime parking functions with $M<k$,
$$
F_k(x)=\sum_{n\ge0}Z_n(k)\frac{x^n}{n!}=\frac{1}{1-C_k(x)},
$$
and without restriction $e^{T(x)}=1/(1-C_\infty(x))$, that is, $C_\infty=1-e^{-T}$; the number of prime parking functions of length $m\ge1$ is $(m-1)^{m-1}$ (equal to $1$ for $m=1$).

*Proof.* Let $j$ be a breakpoint. A spot in $\{1,\dots,j\}$ can only be taken by a car that prefers a spot at most $j$, there are exactly $j$ such cars, and all $n$ spots are taken in the end; so these $j$ cars take exactly the spots $1,\dots,j$, and the other $n-j$ cars take the spots $j+1,\dots,n$. The first group parks as it would on a street of length $j$, and the second as it would on a street of length $n-j$, since a car of one group never meets a car of the other on its way. Iterating gives the decomposition, and the converse construction is clear. The maximum displacement of $\alpha$ is the maximum over its prime pieces, which gives the first identity; the second is the case without threshold, by (2.8) with $g=1$, and $[x^m](1-e^{-T})=(m-1)^{m-1}/m!$ by Lagrange inversion. $\square$

So the generating function of the cumulative counts for a fixed threshold has two exact factorisations: as a set of trees (Proposition 2.11), which comes from the order of arrival, and as a sequence of prime pieces, which comes from the street. The places at which the second cuts the street do not depend on the order of arrival, and it cuts the street into smaller intervals than the first, in the following sense.

**Proposition 5.18** [proved; not found in the two parts of [GS] that were read, its second and its tenth section, which contain the decomposition of Proposition 5.17 and not the forest of the present paper; the statement was not looked for in the other sources, and is not verified against the rest of [GS]]. Every tree of the history forest occupies an interval of spots that is a union of consecutive prime pieces.

*Proof.* The trees of $\Psi(\sigma)$ are the intervals that end at the spots $i$ with $\sigma_i>\sigma_j$ for all $j>i$. Let $i$ be such a spot. Every car parked to the right of $i$ arrived before the car $\sigma_i$, at a time when spot $i$ was empty; it cannot have passed an empty spot, so it preferred a spot to the right of $i$. Hence exactly $i$ cars prefer spots at most $i$, and $i$ is a breakpoint. $\square$

**Proposition 5.19** (the dominant pole) [proved; (b) known: it is the conclusion of the theorem on supercritical sequence schemas [FS, Theorem V.1] for $G=C_k$, whose hypotheses are verified in (a) and at the beginning of the proof of (b), and the proof given here is the proof of that theorem, from Pringsheim's theorem [FS, Theorem IV.6], the lemma on series with non-negative coefficients [FS, Lemma IV.1] and the expansion of the coefficients of a meromorphic function [FS, Theorem IV.10]; (a) and (c), with the value $C_k'(\rho_k)=k$: not found in [CEHHMPU], [AF], [FPV], [Kn98], [KY], [HKMNY], which were read in full; not verified against [FS] outside the pages read, which do not contain them]. Let $k\ge1$, and let $C_k$ be as in Proposition 5.17.

(a) $F_k$ is meromorphic in the plane. It has a simple pole at a real number $\rho_k\in(0,1]$, the smallest positive zero of $1-k\int_0^x\eta_k(u)\,du$, and no singularity of smaller modulus. The function $C_k$ is entire with non-negative coefficients, $C_k(\rho_k)=1$, $C_k'(\rho_k)=k$, and $x\mapsto C_k(\rho_kx)$ is the generating function of a probability law on the positive integers.

(b) There is $\rho_k'>\rho_k$ such that $\rho_k$ is the only singularity of $F_k$ in the disc $|x|<\rho_k'$, and for every $R<\rho'_k$
$$
\frac{Z_n(k)}{n!}=\frac{\rho_k^{-n-1}}{k}+O(R^{-n})\qquad(n\to\infty).
$$

(c) $\rho_{k+1}<\rho_k$. Consequently $Z_n(k)/Z_n(k+1)\to0$ as $n\to\infty$, at a geometric rate, and for every fixed $k\ge1$ there is $n_0(k)$ with $d_{n,k}>0$ for all $n\ge n_0(k)$.

*Proof.* (a) By Proposition 5.16, $F_k=\eta_k/(1-k\int_0^x\eta_k)$ with $\eta_k$ entire and without zeros, so $F_k$ is meromorphic and its poles are the zeros of the entire function $1-k\int_0^x\eta_k$. The coefficients $Z_n(k)/n!$ are at least $1$, because $Z_n(k)\ge Z_n(1)=n!$; so the radius of convergence of $F_k$ is at most $1$. It is positive, because $Z_n(k)\le p_n$ and $\sum_np_nx^n/n!=e^{T(x)}$ converges for $|x|<1/e$. Since the coefficients are non-negative, the radius of convergence $\rho_k$ is itself a singularity (Pringsheim's theorem [FS, Theorem IV.6]); it is therefore a zero of $1-k\int_0^x\eta_k$, and the smallest positive one, since $F_k$ is analytic on $[0,\rho_k)$. The zero is simple because the derivative there is $-k\,\eta_k(\rho_k)\ne0$. By Proposition 5.17, $C_k=1-1/F_k=1-(1-k\int_0^x\eta_k)/\eta_k$, which is entire; its coefficients are numbers of prime parking functions divided by factorials, hence non-negative; and $C_k(\rho_k)=1$ because $1/F_k$ vanishes at the pole. So the coefficients of $C_k(\rho_kx)$ are non-negative with sum $1$, and the constant term is $0$. Differentiating, $C_k'=k+(1-k\int_0^x\eta_k)\,\eta_k'/\eta_k^2$, and the bracket vanishes at $\rho_k$; hence $C_k'(\rho_k)=k$.

(b) Write $C_k(x)=\sum_{m\ge1}c_mx^m$. Then $c_1=1$, since the list $(1)$ is prime with maximum displacement $0$. For $k\ge2$ also $c_2=\frac12$: of the three parking functions of length $2$, the lists $(1,2)$ and $(2,1)$ have the breakpoint $1$, and $(1,1)$ is prime with maximum displacement $1<k$. Let $|x|\le\rho_k$ and $C_k(x)=1$. Then $1=|C_k(x)|\le C_k(|x|)\le C_k(\rho_k)=1$, so $|x|=\rho_k$, because $C_k$ is strictly increasing on $[0,\infty)$, and equality holds in the triangle inequality for the absolutely convergent series $\sum_mc_mx^m$, which requires all non-zero terms $c_mx^m$ to have the same argument. For $k\ge2$ the terms $x$ and $x^2/2$ have the same argument only if $x>0$, so $x=\rho_k$. For $k=1$, $Z_n(1)=n!$ gives $F_1=1/(1-x)$ and $C_1(x)=x$, and again $x=1=\rho_1$. Thus $\rho_k$ is the only zero of $1-C_k$ in the closed disc $|x|\le\rho_k$. The zeros of the entire function $1-C_k$, which is not identically zero, have no accumulation point; hence there is $\rho'_k>\rho_k$ such that $1-C_k$ has no zero other than $\rho_k$ in $|x|<\rho'_k$. The poles of $F_k=1/(1-C_k)$ are the zeros of $1-C_k$. Near $\rho_k$ one has $1-C_k(x)=\rho_kC_k'(\rho_k)\,(1-x/\rho_k)+O((x-\rho_k)^2)$, and $C_k'(\rho_k)=k$ by (a). Therefore
$$
F_k(x)-\frac{1}{k\,\rho_k}\cdot\frac{1}{1-x/\rho_k}
$$
is analytic in $|x|<\rho'_k$, and its coefficients are $O(R^{-n})$ for every $R<\rho_k'$ by Cauchy's inequalities; the coefficient of $x^n$ in $(1-x/\rho_k)^{-1}$ is $\rho_k^{-n}$.

(c) A prime parking function with $M<k$ has $M<k+1$, so $C_{k+1}-C_k$ has non-negative coefficients. Its coefficient of $x^{k+1}$ is positive: the list $(1,\dots,1)$ of length $k+1$ is a parking function; for $1\le j\le k$ all $k+1$ cars prefer a spot at most $j$, so it has no breakpoint other than $0$ and $k+1$; and its maximum displacement is $k$, that of its last car. Hence $C_{k+1}(\rho_k)>C_k(\rho_k)=1$. The function $C_{k+1}$ is continuous and increasing on $[0,\infty)$ and vanishes at $0$, so it takes the value $1$ at a point of $(0,\rho_k)$; and by (a) $\rho_{k+1}$ is the smallest positive zero of $1-C_{k+1}=(1-(k+1)\int_0^x\eta_{k+1})/\eta_{k+1}$. So $\rho_{k+1}<\rho_k$. By (b), applied to $k$ and to $k+1$ with $R$ chosen larger than $\rho_k$, respectively $\rho_{k+1}$, $Z_n(k)/Z_n(k+1)\sim\frac{k+1}{k}(\rho_{k+1}/\rho_k)^{n+1}$, which tends to $0$ at a geometric rate. Finally $d_{n,k}=Z_n(k+1)-2Z_n(k)+Z_n(k-1)\ge Z_n(k+1)-2Z_n(k)$, since $Z_n(k-1)\ge0$, and this is positive as soon as $Z_n(k)/Z_n(k+1)<\frac12$. $\square$

Part (c) is the qualitative form of Theorem 5.3, which gives the explicit range $n\ge21k^2$. What the pole adds is the exponential rate of each column of the table of counts: for fixed $k$, $Z_n(k)\sim n!\,\rho_k^{-n-1}/k$ [proved: part (b)]. In probabilistic terms, under the change of variable $x\mapsto\rho_kx$ the prime pieces form a renewal process: pieces with independent lengths of the law generated by $C_k(\rho_kx)$ are laid one after another, and $\rho_k^{\,n}Z_n(k)/n!$, the coefficient of $x^n$ in $F_k(\rho_kx)=\sum_{i\ge0}C_k(\rho_kx)^i$, is the probability that some partial sum of the lengths equals $n$ [proved: this is the expansion of $1/(1-C_k(\rho_kx))$]. This regime is not developed further in this paper.

### 5.9 An exact expansion over minimal violators

One more exact tool belongs here, because it is the natural expansion for the left half, where disjoint heavy subtrees matter. Call a vertex $v$ of the marked forest of Theorem 2.6(b) a *minimal violator* (for the threshold $k$) if $D_v\ge k$ and $D_w<k$ for all proper descendants $w$ of $v$. The event $M<k$ is the event that there is no minimal violator.

**Proposition 5.20** (expansion over minimal violators) [proved; checked against the recursion for $n\le14$, Appendix B, item C7; not found in [CEHHMPU], [AF], [CM], [KY], [J05], [Kn98], [FPV], which were read in full]. For $n\ge0$ and $k\ge1$,
$$
Z_n(k)=\sum_{j\ge0}(-1)^j\beta_j(n,k),\qquad
\beta_j(n,k)=\frac{n!}{j!}\sum_{s_1,\dots,s_j}\ a_{\gamma}\,\gamma^{\,j}\ \prod_{i=1}^{j}\frac{(s_i-k)\,Z_{s_i-1}(k)}{s_i!},
$$
where the sum is over the $j$-tuples of integers $s_i\ge k+1$ with $\gamma=n+1-s_1-\dots-s_j\ge1$; $\beta_0(n,k)=p_n$, and $\beta_j(n,k)=0$ when $j(k+1)>n$. In particular $Z_n(k)=p_n-\sum_{s=k+1}^{n}\binom ns(s-k)\,Z_{s-1}(k)\,(n-s+1)^{n-s}$ for $n\le2k+1$.

*Proof.* Minimal violators have pairwise disjoint subtrees, each with at least $k+1$ vertices. By inclusion and exclusion, the indicator of the event that there is none is $\sum_j(-1)^j$ times the number of $j$-element sets of minimal violators. The expected number of ordered $j$-tuples of vertices with disjoint subtrees of sizes $s_1,\dots,s_j$ is given by Theorem 3.2 for the pattern with $j$ nodes of type descendant attached to the root: multiplied by $p_n$ it is $n!\,a_\gamma\gamma^j\prod_ia_{s_i}$. By Theorem 3.2(b) the subtrees are independent uniform rooted trees, and the probability that the root of a uniform rooted tree with $s$ vertices is a minimal violator is $\frac{s-k}{s}\cdot Z_{s-1}(k)/p_{s-1}$, because the forest below the root is uniform on $s-1$ vertices. Finally $a_s\cdot\frac{s-k}s\cdot\frac1{p_{s-1}}=\frac{s-k}{s!}$. $\square$


## 6. Open problems

The conjectures and questions posed in this section are not settled here. Each item is either a conjecture, with the grounds for it, or an open question, for which no answer is suggested; the status is written at the head of each item, and where an item contains a proved implication between two unproved statements, this is said and the proof is given or named. Throughout, $d_{n,k}=b_{n,k}-b_{n,k-1}$.

### 6.1 Target T and its three parts

**Problem 6.1** (Target T) [conjecture; this is Conjecture 5.11, and the grounds are those given in Section 5.6]. For every $n\ge301$ and every $k$ with $1\le k\le n-1$ and $k^2<(n-k)^3$: $d_{n,k}\ge0$.

Target T implies Conjecture P (Section 5.6). By Theorem 5.3 only the positions with $n<21k^2$ remain, and they fall into three groups, which can be taken up separately: Target T is equivalent, given Theorem 5.3, to the conjunction of the statements (a), (b) and (c) below [proved: for $n\ge301$ the positions with $k<\lceil n/2\rceil$, that is, with $n\ge2k+1$, satisfy $k^2<(n-k)^3$, and those with $k>\lceil n/2\rceil$ and $k^2<(n-k)^3$ have $n-k\ge2$ and lie in the first layer].

(a) *The left region* [conjecture]. For all $n\ge301$ and all $k$ with $2k+1\le n<21k^2$: $d_{n,k}\ge0$. Grounds: in the computed rows the inequality holds at all positions with $2k+1\le n$ for $15\le n\le300$ [computational evidence; Appendix B, item C5]; it is proved for $n\ge21k^2$ (Theorem 5.3) and, for each fixed $k$, for all sufficiently large $n$ (Proposition 5.19(c)). In this region pairs of heavy vertices with disjoint subtrees contribute to the curvature, and the available tools are Proposition 5.1, the proof of Theorem 5.3 in Appendix A.1, and the expansion of Proposition 5.20.

(b) *The centre* [conjecture]. For every $n\ge301$, with $m=\lceil n/2\rceil$: $d_{n,m}\ge0$, that is, $b_{n,m}\ge b_{n,m-1}$. This is the instance $k=m$ of Target T, since $m^2<(n-m)^3$ for these $n$. For even $n$ the position is $n=2k$, which lies between the left region and the first layer; for odd $n$ it is the first position of the first layer, $n=2k-1$. Grounds: $d_{n,m}>0$ for $16\le n\le300$, while $d_{n,m}<0$ for $2\le n\le15$ [computational evidence; Appendix B, item C5]. At the position $n=2m$ two disjoint fringe subtrees with $m$ vertices each are possible for the first time. For $n\le2k+1$ the skeleton is a path, so the expansion (5.5) of the frozen polynomial holds at $n=2k$ as well, with $r=k$, and so does the closed formula of Proposition 5.20 [proved: Theorem 3.5 and Proposition 5.20 need only $n\le2k+1$]; the formula (5.6) for $B_-$ does not hold at $n=2k$, because a forest can then contain two vertices with $t_v=k$, in disjoint subtrees, and the formula has to be corrected for these forests.

(c) *The first layer before the curve* [conjecture]. For all $n\ge301$ and all $k$ with $\lceil n/2\rceil<k$ and $k^2<(n-k)^3$: $d_{n,k}\ge0$, that is, $C_-\ge B_-$ in the notation of Proposition 5.8. Grounds: in the computed rows the inequality holds at all such positions for $95\le n\le300$ [computational evidence; Appendix B, item C5]. The tools are (5.5), (5.6) and Theorem 4.3 with Lemmas 4.4 and 4.5.

### 6.2 The cross product on the whole first layer

**Problem 6.2** [conjecture; this is Conjecture 5.12, with the grounds given there]. $\mathcal S_{n,k}>0$ for all $k\ge3$ and $k+2\le n\le2k-1$.

With the non-decrease of the rows $n\ge15$ on their left halves, $d_{n,k}\ge0$ for $k\le\lfloor n/2\rfloor$, and the computed rows with $n\le14$, it implies Conjecture P (Corollary 5.13 and the paragraph after it). A question of method is attached to it [open]. Theorem 4.3 refines the conditioned Borel compositions one cut at a time, and Lemma 4.5 uses it to compare the number of parts under different cut factors. The cross product compares two different functionals of compositions with the same weights: the curvature terms $C_\pm$, which by (5.5) count ordered pairs of heavy vertices on the chain, that is, pairs among the parts of a composition, and the boundary terms $B_\pm$, in which by (5.6) the one vertex at the threshold contributes a factor outside the composition. Is there a coupling, or a monotone rearrangement, that compares these two directly on the whole first layer? The proof of Theorem 5.9 bounds the two ratios $C_+/C_-$ and $B_+/B_-$ separately, by (A.3) and (A.4) [proved: Appendix A.3, Step 4]; an answer would be a proof of the inequality $B_+C_->B_-C_+$ on the whole first layer, or on a part of it that Theorem 5.9 does not cover.

### 6.3 What separates the original model from the family with spare spots

The family is that of Proposition 2.13. Let $m\ge1$ and $r\ge1$ be integers, and let $m$ cars park, by the rule of Section 1.1, on a row of $m+r-1$ spots. A preference list in $\{1,\dots,m+r-1\}^m$ is *admissible* if all $m$ cars park; then $r-1$ spots stay empty. There are $r(m+r)^{m-1}$ admissible lists. For $0\le\ell\le m-1$ let $c^{(r)}_{m,\ell}$ be the number of admissible lists whose maximum displacement is exactly $\ell$; the sequence $c^{(r)}_{m,0},\dots,c^{(r)}_{m,m-1}$ is the *row* of $(r,m)$. For $r=1$ it is the row of $m$ of Conjecture P. In the terminology of [AF] the admissible lists with all displacements at most $\ell$ are the $\ell$-interval rational parking functions with $m$ cars and $m+r-1$ spots; that paper gives a recursion for their number [AF, Theorem 3.8]. These numbers are cumulative counts, and the rows of this section are their differences in $\ell$. The paper [AF] contains no table of the cumulative counts for more spots than cars; about their dependence on $\ell$ it says only that they increase; and it says nothing about the rows [cited; the paper was read in full]. For every $r$ the displacements of a uniformly random admissible list are, as a multiset, independent uniform marks on the fringe subtree sizes of a random rooted labeled forest on $[m]$; the law of that forest gives a forest a probability proportional to $r^{\#\text{trees}}$, and it is the law of a uniform rooted labeled tree on $[m]$ whose edges are deleted independently with probability $r/(m+r)$ [proved: Proposition 2.13 and Remark 2.14]. The original model is the member $r=1$ of the family.

**Problem 6.3** (the separating property) [open; the facts on which the question rests are exact computations: Appendix B, item C14]. The rows of this family were computed in exact integer arithmetic for $1\le r\le12$ and $1\le m\le600$. The table gives, for each $r$, the values of $m$ in this range at which the row is not unimodal, and the first $m\ge6$ at which the largest term of the row lies at an index $\ell\ge m/2$.

| $r$ | rows that are not unimodal, $m\le600$ | number of such rows | first $m\ge6$ with the largest term at $\ell\ge m/2$ |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | none | 0 | 16 |
| 2 | none | 0 | 34 |
| 3 | none | 0 | 58 |
| 4 | none | 0 | 87 |
| 5 | $m=121,122$ | 2 | 122 |
| 6 | $159\le m\le165$ | 7 | 162 |
| 7 | $203\le m\le216$ | 14 | 207 |
