| 8 | $251\le m\le275$ | 25 | 258 |
| 9 | $303\le m\le341$ | 39 | 314 |
| 10 | $359\le m\le416$ | 58 | 375 |
| 11 | $420\le m\le500$ | 81 | 441 |
| 12 | $485\le m\le593$ | 109 | 512 |

In each of the $335$ rows of the table that are not unimodal there are exactly two local maxima, and $m/2$ lies strictly between their indices. For each $r$ from $5$ to $12$ the values of $m$ with a row that is not unimodal form one interval; at its beginning the largest term of the row is the local maximum with the smaller index, from the value of $m$ in the last column on it is the local maximum with the larger index, and so the position of the largest term jumps across $m/2$ inside the interval: just before the interval the largest term is at an index between $0.44\,m$ and $0.46\,m$, just after it at an index between $0.55\,m$ and $0.69\,m$. No row in the computed range contains two equal neighbouring terms. For $r=1,2,3,4$ the largest term passes the index $m/2$ at $m=16,34,58,87$ and the rows stay unimodal throughout the computed range [computational evidence and counterexamples; Appendix B, item C14]. The index $m/2$ is the one at which the configuration of the large fringe subtrees changes [proved: two disjoint subtrees with at least $k$ vertices each need $2k\le m$ vertices; for $2k\le m$ a forest with two trees of $k$ vertices exists; and two fringe subtrees of a forest are nested or disjoint, so that for $k>m/2$ any two vertices whose fringe subtrees have at least $k$ vertices are ancestor and descendant]: a forest on $[m]$ can contain two disjoint fringe subtrees with at least $k$ vertices each exactly when $k\le m/2$, and for $k>m/2$ the vertices whose fringe subtree has at least $k$ vertices lie on one path.

*The question.* Which property of the law with $r=1$ makes its rows unimodal, as they are for $m\le600$ and as Conjecture P asserts for all $m$, and is absent from the laws with the values of $r$ for which the table shows failures? An answer would be a property of a law on rooted forests that holds for the uniform forest, fails for the forests with weight $r$ per tree at the pairs $(r,m)$ of the table, and implies a comparison of neighbouring terms of the row. Nothing is asserted here about values of $r$ or $m$ outside the computed range; in particular it is not known whether the rows with $r\le4$ remain unimodal for $m>600$, whether further rows fail for $5\le r\le12$ and $m>600$, or what happens for $r\ge13$. The forest with binary offspring of Counterexample 5.15 raises the same question for another law.

### 6.4 The limit shape of the step

**Problem 6.4** (a local form of the limit law) [open; the implication stated at the end is proved]. Section 5.6 records that in the computed rows with $n\ge95$ the first negative step lies behind the curve $k=(n-k)^{3/2}$. For all sufficiently large $n$, the part of Target T in which $n-k$ is at most a fixed multiple of $n^{2/3}$ would follow from two statements, neither of which is proved here. Let $G(x)=\Pr(X\le x)=1-\psi(x^{3/2})$ be the distribution function of the limit variable of Theorem 2.16, with $\psi$ the series given after that theorem.

(i) *The shape of the limit law.* There is a number $x_0<1$ such that $G''>0$ on $(0,x_0)$ and $G''<0$ on $(x_0,\infty)$. Numerically this is what the series shows: on the grid $x=0.01,0.02,\dots,6$ one finds $G''>0$ for $x\le0.59$ and $G''<0$ for $x\ge0.60$, with a zero at $x_0=0.59962\ldots$ [computational evidence: floating-point evaluation of the series; Appendix B, item C12].

(ii) *A local limit theorem for the step.* With $x=(n+1-k)/(n+1)^{2/3}$,
$$
(n+1)^{4/3}\,\frac{d_{n,k}}{p_n}=-G''(x)+o(1)\qquad(n\to\infty),
$$
uniformly for $x$ in every compact subset of $(0,\infty)$.

The form of (ii) is what convergence in distribution suggests: $b_{n,k}/p_n=\Pr(M=k)$ should be close to $(n+1)^{-2/3}G'(x)$, and a step of $k$ by one is a step of $x$ by $-(n+1)^{-2/3}$. From (i) and (ii) it follows that for every compact interval $I\subset(x_0,\infty)$ there is $n_0$ with $d_{n,k}>0$ for all $n\ge n_0$ and all $k$ with $x\in I$ [proved: $-G''$ is continuous and positive on $I$, hence bounded below there by a positive number]; since the curve corresponds to $x$ near $1$, this would settle, for all sufficiently large $n$, the part of Target T in which $n-k$ is at most a fixed multiple of $n^{2/3}$. The positions between the left region and that window would still have to be treated. The published proof of the limit law rests on the convergence of the process of the largest block [Pet, Theorem 3.2] and on a Poisson approximation for conditionally independent events along it [Pet, Lemma 4.2]; a proof of (ii) would have to sharpen both to statements about single values of $k$, and that article contains no local statement (Remark 3.9). In the first layer the present paper provides exact finite formulas for all the quantities involved, (5.5) and (5.6). In the computed rows the peak is at the values $0.92$, $0.85$, $0.82$ of $(n+1-\ell)/(n+1)^{2/3}$ for $n=100,200,300$ [computational evidence; Appendix B, item C5]; these values are still far from the numerical value of $x_0$ and approach it slowly. Nothing is inferred here about the rate of the convergence in (ii).

### 6.5 Further questions

**Problem 6.5** (the process conditioned to stay below the threshold) [open]. The tilted law of Section 3.1 is the law of the shape, with uniform labels, of the history forest of a parking process conditioned on $M<k$. The conditioned process is again a Markov chain on the configurations, that is, on the sets of occupied spots [proved: a path of configurations determines, for each car, the block in which it lands, and given the path the preferences of the cars are independent and uniform on these blocks; so, given the path, the events that the successive cars have displacements smaller than $k$ are independent, the event of a step having the probability $\min(1,k/h)$ when the car lands in a block of size $h$ (Lemma 2.2); so the probability of a path together with the event $M<k$ is a product over the steps of a function of two consecutive configurations, and a law on paths of this form is that of a Markov chain]. Its transition probabilities are those of the unconditioned chain multiplied by the survival probability of the step and by the ratio of the probabilities, from the new and from the old configuration, that all later cars survive; in this way they involve the future. The last assertion of Theorem 4.2 shows that for $k\ge2$ its history does not split under the deletion of a uniform edge into independent pieces of the same family, once the trees have at least $k+2$ vertices. Is there a description of this conditioned merging process, for instance of the law of the block sizes at intermediate times, that is as explicit as Theorem 2.9? The first-layer identities (5.5) and (5.6) are statements about the last stage of this process; an explicit description at all stages would extend them beyond the first layer. An answer would be a formula for the law of the block sizes after $t$ cars under this conditioning, or a proof that this law is not of the product form of Theorem 2.9.

**Problem 6.6** (a continuum limit of the refinement of Theorem 4.3) [open]. Under the law $\mathbb P_1$ of Section 4.3 the cut points of a composition of $m$, together with $0$ and $m$, are the renewal epochs in $[0,m]$ of a random walk with Borel steps started at $0$ and conditioned to have a renewal at $m$ [proved: the weight $\prod a_{\text{gap}}$ is $e^{m}$ times the product of the Borel masses of the gaps]. The Borel law has a tail of index $\frac12$ (Lemma 3.6), which is also the index of the stable subordinator whose range is the zero set of Brownian motion. Does the set of cut points, divided by $m$, converge as $m\to\infty$ to the zero set of a Brownian bridge, and does the coupling of Theorem 4.3, which removes the cut points one at a time, converge to a process that thins this set and that can be described without reference to the discrete rule? Neither convergence is proved here. The interval fragmentations of the literature that are attached to the additive coalescent, those of the Brownian excursion with a drift [Ber2] and of bridges with exchangeable increments [Ber], [Mie], are read from the minimum of a bridge, where the first fragment is a size-biased pick; the segment of Theorem 4.3 has two fixed ends, and its relation to those fragmentations is part of the question. The reason for asking is Theorem 4.3 itself: the rule determines Cayley's coefficients, which suggests that it is canonical.

**Problem 6.7** (other threshold statistics) [open; the case $j=1$ of the first question is proved below]. For $j\ge1$ let $X^{(j)}(\alpha)$ be the number of cars of $\alpha$ with displacement at least $j$, and for $n\ge2$ let $M_2(\alpha)$ be the second largest displacement, that is, the second term when the displacements are arranged in non-increasing order. Two questions: for $n\ge3$ and $2\le j\le n-1$, is the sequence $\ell\mapsto\#\{\alpha\in\mathrm{PF}_n:X^{(j)}(\alpha)=\ell\}$ unimodal? And is the sequence $\ell\mapsto\#\{\alpha\in\mathrm{PF}_n:M_2(\alpha)=\ell\}$ unimodal for every $n\ge2$? Both statistics are functionals of the same forest: the generating polynomial of $X^{(j)}$ is given by Theorem 2.6(a) with $w(d)=x^{\mathbf 1\{d\ge j\}}$ and can be computed by the recursion (2.7), and $M_2<j$ exactly when $X^{(j)}\le1$. The weight belonging to $w$ equals $1$ on fringe subtrees with at most $j$ vertices, so Theorem 3.5 applies; Proposition 3.7 computes the mean of $X^{(j)}$ for $j=n-q$ with $q\le n/2$. Both sequences are unimodal in the range computed, the first for all $1\le j\le n-1$ with $n\le40$ and the second for $2\le n\le120$ [computational evidence; Appendix B, item C13]. The case $j=1$ is settled [proved]: $X^{(1)}$ is $n$ minus the number of lucky cars, so its generating polynomial is obtained from the product of Proposition 2.12(b) by reversing the order of the coefficients, and is $\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\bigl((n-i+1)+ix\bigr)$. The coefficients of a product of factors $a+bx$ with $a,b>0$ form a log-concave sequence of positive numbers, because multiplication by such a factor preserves this property: if $c_0,\dots,c_L$ are positive and log-concave, $c_i=0$ for the other $i$, and $e_i=ac_i+bc_{i-1}$, then $e_0,\dots,e_{L+1}$ are positive and $e_i^2-e_{i-1}e_{i+1}=a^2(c_i^2-c_{i-1}c_{i+1})+b^2(c_{i-1}^2-c_{i-2}c_i)+ab(c_ic_{i-1}-c_{i+1}c_{i-2})\ge0$, the last bracket being non-negative because the ratios $c_{i+1}/c_i$ do not increase. A log-concave sequence of positive numbers is unimodal. An answer to either question would be a proof, or a counterexample, given by its $n$ and, for the first question, its $j$; the answers can be compared with Conjecture P and with the families of Section 6.3.


## Appendix A. Proofs of Theorems 5.3, 5.4 and 5.9

The notation is that of Sections 1.5, 3.1 and 5. In particular $a_j=j^{j-1}/j!$, $\mathrm{Bor}(j)=e^{-j}a_j$, $\mathrm{Ren}(j)=j\,\mathrm{Bor}(j)$ and $N=n+1$. In A.1 the symbol $\mathbb E_k$ denotes expectation under the tilted law of Section 3.1 with $y=k$, in which a forest $F$ on $[m]$ has probability $\Phi_{g_k}(F)/Z_m(k)$. In A.3 the letters $\mathbb P$ and $\mathbb E$ with a subscript refer to the laws on compositions of Section 4.3. Each proof is preceded in Section 5 by an overview; the overview is not repeated here.

### A.1 Proof of Theorem 5.3 (increase far on the left)

Fix $k\ge1$. Put $c_i=\min(i+1,k)$ and $f_m=Z_m(k)/m!$, so that $F_k(z)=\sum_{m\ge0}f_mz^m$; we write $F$ for $F_k$, and in this subsection forests are denoted by $\mathcal F$. For a forest let $\chi=\chi_k$ be the number of its vertices with $t_v>k$, and let $\mu_m=\mathbb E_k\chi$ for the forests on $m$ vertices ($\mu_0=0$).

*Step 1: $\mu_m\ge\frac{m+1}{2k}-1$ for all $m\ge0$.* For real $y$ with $k\le y<k+1$ the recursion (2.7) with $g=g_y$ reads
$$
Z_m(y)=\sum_{i+j=m-1}\binom{m-1}{i}\min(i+1,y)\,Z_i(y)\,Z_j(y),
$$
and on this interval $Z_m(y)=\sum_{\mathcal F}\Phi_{g_k}(\mathcal F)(y/k)^{\chi(\mathcal F)}$. Applying $y\frac{d}{dy}$ at $y=k$ from the right gives
$$
Z_m(k)\,\mu_m=\sum_{i+j=m-1}\binom{m-1}{i}c_i\,Z_i(k)Z_j(k)\,\bigl(\mu_i+\mu_j+\mathbf 1\{i\ge k\}\bigr),
$$
because at $y=k$, from the right, $y\frac d{dy}\min(i+1,y)$ equals $k=c_i$ if $i\ge k$ and $0$ otherwise. Since $Z_m(k)=\sum\binom{m-1}ic_iZ_iZ_j$, the number $\mu_m$ is a weighted average of the brackets. Group the terms $(i,j)$ and $(j,i)$: they have the same binomial coefficient and the same product $Z_iZ_j$, and weights $c_i$ and $c_j$. We prove the claim by induction on $m$; it holds for $m=0$. If $i,j\ge k$, the bracket is at least $\frac{i+1}{2k}-1+\frac{j+1}{2k}-1+1=\frac{m+1}{2k}-1$. If $i\ge k>j$, the average of the indicator over the two orders is $c_i/(c_i+c_j)=k/(k+j+1)\ge\frac12\ge\frac{j+1}{2k}$, and $\mu_i\ge\frac{i+1}{2k}-1$, $\mu_j\ge0$, so the average of the two brackets is at least $\frac{m+1}{2k}-1$. If $i,j<k$, then $m+1\le2k$, so the bracket, which is non-negative, is at least $\frac{m+1}{2k}-1\le0$.

Consequently, a tree with $a>k$ vertices drawn with probability proportional to $\Phi_{g_k}$ has on average at least $a/(2k)$ vertices with $t_v>k$: its root is one, the factor $k/a$ of the root does not depend on the tree, and the forest below the root follows the tilted law on $a-1$ vertices, so the mean is $1+\mu_{a-1}\ge\frac a{2k}$.

*Step 2: pairs of large trees.* By (2.8), $F'=F\,\Theta$ with $\Theta=\sum_{i\ge0}c_if_iz^i$; $\Theta$ is the generating function of the weighted trees with a marked vertex, in the sense that $z\Theta(z)=\sum_{a\ge1}a\,\omega_az^a/a!$ with $\omega_a$ the total weight of the rooted trees on $a$ labels. Put
$$
P=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}(i+1)f_iz^i,\qquad C=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}(k-i-1)f_iz^i,\qquad U=\Theta-P=\sum_{i\ge k}k\,f_iz^i .
$$
Then $\Theta=kF-C$, and $zU$ marks a vertex in a tree with more than $k$ vertices. Since a forest is a set of trees, the sum over the forests $\mathcal F$ on $[n]$ of $\Phi_{g_k}(\mathcal F)\sum|A|\,|B|$, the inner sum over the ordered pairs of distinct trees $A\ne B$ of $\mathcal F$ with more than $k$ vertices each, is $n!\,[z^n]\,(zU)^2F$. We claim
$$
2FU^2=F''-(C+4P)F'+(C'+2P^2)F. \tag{A.1}
$$
Indeed $F'=F\Theta=F(U+P)$, so $FU=F'-PF$ and $UF'=FU^2+P\cdot FU=FU^2+PF'-P^2F$. Differentiating $F'=F\Theta$ and using $\Theta'=kF'-C'$ and $kF=U+P+C$,
$$
F''=F\Theta^2+F(kF'-C')=\bigl(FU^2+2P(F'-PF)+P^2F\bigr)+\bigl(UF'+(P+C)F'\bigr)-C'F=2FU^2+(4P+C)F'-(2P^2+C')F .
$$

Next, $f_if_j\le f_{i+j}$ for all $i,j\ge0$. The recursion (2.7) divided by $(m-1)!$ is $mf_m=\sum_{s=0}^{m-1}c_sf_sf_{m-1-s}$. By induction on $i+j$ (the cases $i=0$ or $j=0$ being trivial),
$$
(i+j)f_if_j=\sum_{s=0}^{i-1}c_sf_s\,f_{i-1-s}f_j+\sum_{s=0}^{j-1}c_s\,f_if_s\,f_{j-1-s}\le\sum_{s=0}^{i-1}c_sf_sf_{i+j-1-s}+\sum_{s=0}^{j-1}c_{i+s}f_{i+s}f_{j-1-s}=(i+j)f_{i+j},
$$
using $f_{i-1-s}f_j\le f_{i+j-1-s}$, $f_if_s\le f_{i+s}$ and $c_s\le c_{i+s}$. In particular $f_{m-1}\le f_m$, since $f_1=1$.

Now take the coefficient of $z^{n-2}$ in (A.1), for $n>k$. The coefficients of $(C'+2P^2)F$ are non-negative. $[z^{n-2}]F''=n(n-1)f_n$. Since $C+4P=\sum_{i<k}(k+3i+3)f_iz^i$,
$$
[z^{n-2}](C+4P)F'=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}(k+3i+3)\,f_i\,(n-1-i)f_{n-1-i}\le(n-1)f_n\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}(k+3i+3)=(n-1)f_n\,\frac{5k^2+3k}{2}\le4k^2(n-1)f_n .
$$
Hence $[z^{n-2}]FU^2\ge\frac12(n-1)(n-4k^2)f_n$, that is,
$$
\mathbb E_k\sum_{A\ne B,\ |A|,|B|>k}|A|\,|B|\ \ge\ \frac{(n-1)(n-4k^2)}2 . \tag{A.2}
$$

*Step 3: comparison.* Let $n\ge21k^2$. For $k<y<k+1$ and a forest $\mathcal F$ containing two vertices with $t_v>k$, $\Phi_{g_y}(\mathcal F)/y^2=\Phi_{g_k}(\mathcal F)\,y^{\chi(\mathcal F)-2}/k^{\chi(\mathcal F)}\ge\Phi_{g_k}(\mathcal F)/k^2$. Keeping in $Z_n''(y)$ only the ordered pairs of such vertices that lie in different trees, and in $V_{n,k}$ only the interval $(k,k+1)$, on which the weight $1-|y-k|$ has integral $\frac12$,
$$
V_{n,k}\ \ge\ \frac{Z_n(k)}{2k^2}\ \mathbb E_k\sum_{A\ne B}\chi(A)\chi(B),
$$
where $\chi(A)$ is the number of vertices of the tree $A$ with $t_v>k$. Under the tilted law the trees of the forest are independent given their vertex sets, each with probability proportional to $\Phi_{g_k}$; by Step 1 the conditional mean of $\chi(A)$ is at least $|A|/(2k)$ if $|A|>k$. By the independence, the conditional mean of $\chi(A)\chi(B)$ for $A\ne B$ is the product of the two conditional means, and the pairs in which one of the trees has at most $k$ vertices contribute nothing negative. With (A.2), $V_{n,k}\ge Z_n(k)\,(n-1)(n-4k^2)/(16k^4)$. On the other hand the subtrees of the vertices with $t_v=k$ are pairwise disjoint, so $\nu_k\le n/k$ and $L_{n,k}\le Z_n(k)\,n/k^2$. Therefore
$$
\frac{V_{n,k}}{L_{n,k}}\ \ge\ \frac{n-1}{n}\cdot\frac{n-4k^2}{16k^2}\ \ge\ \frac{20}{21}\cdot\frac{17}{16}=\frac{85}{84}>1,
$$
and $d_{n,k}=V_{n,k}-L_{n,k}>0$ by (5.1). $\square$

### A.2 Proof of Theorem 5.4 (decrease far on the right)

Let $1\le k<n$ and $k\ge4R^{3/2}$ with $R=n-k+1\ge2$; then $N=k+R$, $k\ge12$ and $n-k<k$. Put $w_d=\sum_{h=1}^{d-1}\mathrm{Ren}(h)\mathrm{Ren}(d-h)$. We use four facts, all consequences of Lemma 3.6: $\mathrm{Bor}$ is decreasing; $\mathrm{Ren}(j)<(2\pi j)^{-1/2}$; $R^{3/2}\mathrm{Bor}(R)>e^{-1/24}/\sqrt{2\pi}>\frac38$ for $R\ge2$; and $w_d<\frac12$ for all $d\ge2$. For the last one, the function $x\mapsto(x(d-x))^{-1/2}$ is convex on $(0,d)$ (its logarithm is convex), so its value at an integer $h$ with $1\le h\le d-1$ is at most its integral over $[h-\frac12,h+\frac12]$, and $\sum_{h=1}^{d-1}(h(d-h))^{-1/2}\le\int_0^d(x(d-x))^{-1/2}dx=\pi$.

*The curvature.* For non-integer $y\in(k-1,k+1)$, a vertex with $t_v>y$ has $t_v\ge k$, and $\Phi_{g_y}\le1$; so by Proposition 5.1(b), $Z_n''(y)\le(k-1)^{-2}$ times the number of triples (forest, ordered pair of distinct vertices with subtree sizes at least $k$); and the weight $1-|y-k|$ has integral $1$ over $(k-1,k+1)$, so $V_{n,k}$ satisfies the same bound. Since $n<2k$ the two vertices are nested. By Corollary 3.3(b), the number of pairs (forest, chain $v_1,v_2$ with $v_2$ a proper descendant of $v_1$, $t_{v_2}=i$ and $t_{v_1}=i+h$) is $p_n\,\mathrm{Bor}(i)\mathrm{Ren}(h)\mathrm{Ren}(N-i-h)/\mathrm{Bor}(N)$, and $p_n/\mathrm{Bor}(N)=n!\,e^N$. Summing over $h\ge1$ with $N-i-h\ge1$ and over $k\le i\le n-1$, and counting both orders,
$$
\frac{V_{n,k}}{n!\,e^N}\ \le\ \frac{2}{(k-1)^2}\sum_{i=k}^{n-1}\mathrm{Bor}(i)\,w_{N-i}\ <\ \frac{2}{(k-1)^2}\cdot(R-1)\cdot\mathrm{Bor}(k)\cdot\frac12\ <\ \frac{R\,\mathrm{Bor}(k)}{(k-1)^2}.
$$

*The loss.* In (5.2) the forest $E$ has $R-1<k$ vertices, so $\Phi_{g_k}(E)=1$ and every factor is $k/(t_x+k)$. There are $R^{R-2}$ forests $E$ and $R$ attachment places for each. Hence, with $(E,u)$ uniformly distributed,
$$
L_{n,k}=\binom nk k^{k-2}R^{R-1}\ \mathbb E\prod_{x\in P(u)}\Bigl(1+\frac{t_x}{k}\Bigr)^{-1},\qquad \binom nkk^{k-2}R^{R-1}=n!\,e^N\,\frac{\mathrm{Bor}(k)}{k}\,R\,\mathrm{Bor}(R).
$$
Given $E$, a vertex $x$ belongs to $P(u)$ with probability $t_x/R$. By Corollary 3.3(a) for the uniform forest on $R-1$ vertices,
$$
\mathbb E\sum_{x\in P(u)}t_x=\frac1R\sum_{s=1}^{R-1}s^2\,\frac{\mathrm{Ren}(R-s)\mathrm{Bor}(s)}{\mathrm{Bor}(R)}=\frac{\sum_{s}s\,\mathrm{Ren}(s)\mathrm{Ren}(R-s)}{R\,\mathrm{Bor}(R)}=\frac{w_R}{2\,\mathrm{Bor}(R)},
$$
the last step by the symmetry $s\leftrightarrow R-s$. Since $\prod_i(1+x_i)^{-1}\ge1-\sum_ix_i$ for non-negative $x_i$,
$$
\frac{L_{n,k}}{n!\,e^N}\ \ge\ \frac{\mathrm{Bor}(k)}{k}\,R\,\mathrm{Bor}(R)\Bigl(1-\frac{w_R}{2k\,\mathrm{Bor}(R)}\Bigr)=\frac{R\,\mathrm{Bor}(k)}{k^2}\Bigl(k\,\mathrm{Bor}(R)-\frac{w_R}2\Bigr).
$$

*Conclusion.* By the hypothesis, $k\,\mathrm{Bor}(R)\ge4R^{3/2}\mathrm{Bor}(R)>\frac32$; also $w_R/2<\frac14$ and $k^2/(k-1)^2\le\frac{144}{121}<\frac54$. Hence
$$
\frac{L_{n,k}-V_{n,k}}{n!\,e^N}>\frac{R\,\mathrm{Bor}(k)}{k^2}\Bigl(k\,\mathrm{Bor}(R)-\frac{w_R}2-\frac{k^2}{(k-1)^2}\Bigr)>0,
$$
and $d_{n,k}=V_{n,k}-L_{n,k}<0$. $\square$

The same sum $w_d$ counts the ways to nest two marked subtrees and gives the mean cost of re-attaching one; in the bound above the two effects are compared through the quantity $k\,\mathrm{Bor}(R)$, which is of order $k/R^{3/2}$.

### A.3 Proof of Theorem 5.9 (propagation of a decrease)

The proof uses the notation of Section 4.3: a composition of $m$ is identified with its set $S\subseteq\{1,\dots,m-1\}$ of cut points, $Q=|S|+1$ is its number of parts, $\mathcal W_\lambda$ is the weight (4.2), $\mathbb P_\lambda$ the corresponding law, $T_{m,q}=[z^m]T^q$, and $\Lambda_m(y;N)$ of Section 5.4 is the total mass of $\mathcal W_\lambda$ for $\lambda_s=y/(N-s)$.

**Proof of Theorem 5.9.** Let $r=n-k\ge2$ and $k\ge r^{3/2}$; then $k\ge r+1$, $k\ge3$, and $(n,k)$ is in the first layer. We use Proposition 5.8.

*Step 1: the curvature ratio.* In $y\Lambda_m(y)$ the composition $S$ contributes a constant times $y^{Q}$. Hence $\frac{d^2}{dy^2}(y\Lambda_m)$ and $\frac{d^3}{dy^3}(y\Lambda_m)$ have non-negative coefficients, and for $y>0$ and $m\ge2$
$$
\frac{y\,(y\Lambda_m)'''}{(y\Lambda_m)''}=\frac{\mathbb E_\lambda\,Q(Q-1)(Q-2)}{\mathbb E_\lambda\,Q(Q-1)},\qquad\lambda_s=\frac{y}{N-s}\quad(1\le s\le m-1).
$$
For $m\le r$ the cut points satisfy $N-s\ge N-r+1=k+2$, so $\lambda_s\le1$ whenever $y\le k+2$, and by Lemmas 4.5(a) and 4.4 the quotient is at most $D_m\le D_r$. The term $m=1$ of (5.5) is linear in $y$. Since the coefficients in (5.5) are positive, $K'''\ge0$ and $yK'''(y)\le D_rK''(y)$ for $0<y\le k+2$, and $K''>0$. As $K$ is a polynomial, $C_-=\int_{k-1}^{k+1}(1-|y-k|)K''(y)\,dy$ and $C_+=\int_{k-1}^{k+1}(1-|y-k|)K''(y+1)\,dy$. From $K'''\ge0$ and $(\log K'')'\le D_r/y\le D_r/(k-1)$ on $[k-1,k+2]$,
$$
1\ \le\ \frac{C_+}{C_-}\ \le\ \exp\Bigl(\frac{D_r}{k-1}\Bigr). \tag{A.3}
$$

*Step 2: the boundary ratio.* By (5.6) and $a_{k+1}/a_k=(1+\frac1k)^{k-1}$,
$$
\frac{B_+}{B_-}=\Bigl(1+\frac1k\Bigr)^{k-2}\,\frac{\Lambda_r(k+2)}{\Lambda_{r+1}(k-1)} .
$$
We first show $\Lambda_{r+1}(k-1)\le(1+\frac1r)^r\Lambda_r(k)$. Remove the first unit cell from a composition of $r+1$. If its first part is $1$, the part and the cut point $1$ disappear; otherwise the first part $j+1$ becomes $j$. What remains is a composition of $r$ whose cut points carry the inherited factors $\lambda'_i=(k-1)/(N-i-1)$, $1\le i\le r-1$. Reading this backwards, with $h=(k-1)/(N-1)$ the factor of the cut point $1$ and $\gamma_0$ the first part of the composition of $r$,
$$
\Lambda_{r+1}(k-1)=\Lambda'\cdot\Bigl(h+\mathbb E_{\lambda'}\frac{a_{\gamma_0+1}}{a_{\gamma_0}}\Bigr),\qquad\Lambda'=\sum_S\mathcal W_{\lambda'}(S).
$$
(i) $\lambda'_i\le k/(N-i)$, because $N-i\ge k$; hence $\Lambda'\le\Lambda_r(k)$. (ii) $\lambda'_i\ge h$ for all $i$. By Proposition 5.7 and Lemma 5.6, $\mathcal W_h$ satisfies the lattice condition, so by the FKG inequality [FKG, Proposition 1] two non-decreasing functions of $S$ are non-negatively correlated under $\mathbb P_h$. The law $\mathbb P_{\lambda'}$ has with respect to $\mathbb P_h$ a density proportional to $\varpi(S)=\prod_{s\in S}\lambda'_s/h$, which is non-decreasing in $S$. Hence $\mathbb E_{\lambda'}\phi=\mathbb E_h[\phi\varpi]/\mathbb E_h[\varpi]\ge\mathbb E_h\phi$ for every function $\phi$ that is non-decreasing in $S$ (this is the argument of Section 5.3). The first part $\gamma_0=\min(S\cup\{r\})$ is non-increasing in $S$ and $a_{\gamma_0+1}/a_{\gamma_0}$ is increasing in $\gamma_0$ (Lemma 5.6), so $\mathbb E_{\lambda'}[a_{\gamma_0+1}/a_{\gamma_0}]\le\mathbb E_h[a_{\gamma_0+1}/a_{\gamma_0}]$. (iii) For the homogeneous factor $h$ on both lengths the same removal of the first cell gives $h+\mathbb E_h[a_{\gamma_0+1}/a_{\gamma_0}]=\Upsilon(h)$ with
$$
\Upsilon(h)=\frac{\sum_{q=1}^{r+1}T_{r+1,q}h^{q-1}}{\sum_{q=1}^{r}T_{r,q}h^{q-1}}=\mathbb E_h\Bigl[\frac{T_{r+1,Q}}{T_{r,Q}}\Bigr]+\frac{T_{r+1,r+1}}{\sum_{q=1}^rT_{r,q}h^{q-1-r}} .
$$
Both terms are non-decreasing in $h$: the first because $T_{r+1,q}/T_{r,q}$ increases with $q$ (proof of Lemma 4.4) and the law of $Q$ under $\mathbb P_h$ increases with $h$ in the likelihood-ratio order, the second because the exponents $q-1-r$ are negative. Since $h<1$, $\Upsilon(h)\le \Upsilon(1)=(r+1)a_{r+1}/(ra_r)=(1+\frac1r)^r$. Together, $\Lambda_{r+1}(k-1)\le\Lambda_r(k)\,\Upsilon(h)\le(1+\frac1r)^r\Lambda_r(k)$.

Next, $\Lambda_r(k+2)/\Lambda_r(k)=\mathbb E_\lambda(1+\frac2k)^{Q-1}$ with $\lambda_s=k/(N-s)$, and $\lambda_s\ge k/(N-1)=\rho$ with $\rho=k/(k+r)$. By Lemma 4.5(b) and Jensen's inequality, $\Lambda_r(k+2)/\Lambda_r(k)\ge\mathbb E_\rho(1+\frac2k)^{Q-1}\ge(1+\frac2k)^{\mu}$, where $\mu=\mathbb E_\rho(Q-1)$ on the compositions of $r$. Altogether
$$
\log\frac{B_+}{B_-}\ \ge\ (k-2)\log\Bigl(1+\frac1k\Bigr)-r\log\Bigl(1+\frac1r\Bigr)+\mu\log\Bigl(1+\frac2k\Bigr). \tag{A.4}
$$

*Step 3: a lower bound for $\mu$.* Under $\mathbb P_\rho$ on the compositions of $r$, the variable $J=Q-1$ has $\Pr(J=j)$ proportional to $T_{r,j+1}\rho^j$, that is, to $(j+1)v_j$ with $v_j=\rho^j(r-1)_j/r^j$, $0\le j\le r-1$. From $r\,(j+1)\Pr(J=j+1)=\rho\,(j+2)(r-1-j)\Pr(J=j)$, summed over $j$, $r\mu=\rho\,\mathbb E[(J+2)(r-1-J)]$, and since $1/\rho=1+r/k$,
$$
\mathbb EJ^2+\Bigl(3+\frac{r^2}{k}\Bigr)\mu=2(r-1).
$$
Moreover $\mathbb E\,J(J-1)\le\frac32\mu^2$. To see this, give the pair $(I,H)$ of non-negative integers with $I+H\le r-1$ a probability proportional to $v_{I+H}$. Then $I+H$ has the law of $J$, and given $I+H=j$ the variable $I$ is uniform on $\{0,\dots,j\}$, so $\mathbb E[IH\mid I+H=j]=j(j-1)/6$ and $\mathbb EI=\mathbb EH=\mu/2$. Because $v_{j+1}/v_j$ is decreasing in $j$, the conditional law of $H$ given $I=i$ decreases in the likelihood-ratio order as $i$ grows, so $I$ and $H$ are non-positively correlated: $\frac16\mathbb EJ(J-1)=\mathbb E\,IH\le\mu^2/4$.

Therefore $2(r-1)\le\frac32\mu^2+\bigl(4+\frac{r^2}k\bigr)\mu\le\frac32\mu^2+(4+x)\mu$ with $x=\sqrt r$, since $k\ge r^{3/2}$. If $\mu\le\frac67x-1$, the right side would be at most $\frac{96}{49}x^2-\frac x7-\frac52<2(x^2-1)$. Hence
$$
\mu>\tfrac67\sqrt r-1. \tag{A.5}
$$

*Step 4: the criterion.* For $t>0$, $t-\frac{t^2}2\le\log(1+t)\le t-\frac{t^2}2+\frac{t^3}3$. Inserting these into (A.3) and (A.4),
$$
k\log\frac{B_+C_-}{B_-C_+}\ \ge\ \mathcal M(r,k)+\frac1k,\qquad \mathcal M(r,k)=k\Bigl(\frac1{2r}-\frac1{3r^2}\Bigr)-\frac52+2\Bigl(1-\frac1k\Bigr)\mu-\frac{k}{k-1}D_r,
$$
where $\mu=\mu(r,k)$ is the mean of Step 3 and $D_r$ is as in Lemma 4.4. Hence $\mathcal S_{n,k}>0$ whenever $\mathcal M(r,k)\ge0$. Steps 5 and 6 show that $\mathcal M(r,k)>0$ except at finitely many pairs $(r,k)$, and at those pairs the cross product is computed directly.

*Step 5: $r\ge256$.* Here $x=\sqrt r\ge16$ and $k\ge x^3$. By (A.5) and Lemma 4.4: $k(\frac1{2r}-\frac1{3r^2})\ge\frac x2-\frac1{3x}$; $2(1-\frac1k)\mu\ge2(1-x^{-3})(\frac67x-1)\ge\frac{12}7x-2-\frac{12}{7x^2}$; and $\frac k{k-1}D_r\le\frac{19}{10}x+\frac{19}{10}\cdot\frac{x}{x^3-1}\le\frac{19}{10}x+\frac2{x^2}$. Hence
$$
\mathcal M(r,k)\ \ge\ \frac{11}{35}x-\frac92-\frac1{3x}-\frac4{x^2}\ >0\qquad(x\ge16),
$$
since the right side is increasing and exceeds $0.49$ at $x=16$.

*Step 6: $2\le r\le255$.* For fixed $r$, $\mathcal M(r,k)$ is strictly increasing in $k$: the first term increases; $\mu(r,k)\ge0$ increases with $\rho=k/(k+r)$, because the law of $J$ increases with $\rho$ in the likelihood-ratio order; and $k/(k-1)$ decreases while $D_r\ge0$. So for each $r$ it suffices to find the smallest integer $k_0(r)\ge r^{3/2}$ with $\mathcal M(r,k_0(r))>0$ and to treat the integers $k$ with $r^{3/2}\le k<k_0(r)$ directly. The numbers $\mathcal M(r,k)$ are rational and were computed exactly: there are $122$ pairs $(r,k)$ with $2\le r\le255$, $k\ge r^{3/2}$ and $\mathcal M(r,k)\le0$; the largest value of $n=k+r$ among them is $102$; and at each of them the integer $\mathcal S_{n,k}=B_+C_--B_-C_+$, computed from the definitions of Section 5.4, is positive (Appendix B, item C8).

*Conclusion.* In all cases $B_+/B_->C_+/C_-\ge1$, so $\mathcal S_{n,k}>0$. If $d_{n,k}\le0$, then by (5.7) $d_{n,k+1}=\frac{B_+}{B_-}d_{n,k}-\mathcal S_{n,k}/B_-<d_{n,k}\le0$. The position $(n,k+1)$ has $r-1$ in place of $r$ and again satisfies $k+1\ge(r-1)^{3/2}$; if $r-1\ge2$ the argument applies again, and after $r-1$ applications all steps $d_{n,k+1},\dots,d_{n,n-1}$ are seen to be negative. $\square$


## Appendix B. Index of computations

All computations cited in this paper were carried out by scripts written for this purpose from the definitions in the text, in Python with exact integer and rational arithmetic (the places where decimal arithmetic with 60 significant digits or floating-point arithmetic was used are marked). Parking functions were generated by running the parking process on all lists; forests by enumerating all trees on $\{0,\dots,n\}$ through their Prüfer sequences. The scripts and their recorded outputs accompany the paper; the file `computation/README.md` gives their locations. The table gives, for each item cited in the text, the scripts, what was checked, the range, and the result. The item numbers C1 to C13 are those under which the outputs are recorded; items C14, C15 and C16 are additional computations, made for Section 6.3, Section 4.3 and Section 4.2; statements are referred to by their numbers in this paper.

| Proposition 2.4 | the history forest | proved; parts (a) to (c) known: [GL, Lemma 3.2, and Lemma 3.3 with its proof]; the same forest, indexed by the cars, is the auxiliary forest of [KY, Section 2.4], which that paper refers to a construction of Knuth [Kn3]; part (d): differs: [CL, Section 8] obtains the forests of the earlier times by erasing, in the reverse order of arrival, the edges of a different tree, built by a breadth-first correspondence (Section 2.4); for the present forest, (d) was not found in [GL], [Sh], [KY], [CM], [WY], [FPV], [Pet], [J01], [J05], [Kn98], [CL], [MV], which were read in full; not verified against [Kn3] |
| Lemma 2.5 | hook length formula for forests | proved; the last assertion is the hook length formula for forests, known: [GS, Lemma 2.1], credited there to [Kn3, Exercise 20, p. 70] (not read), and [BW, p. 167], which quotes it from the same page of [Kn3]; the same lemma of [GS] gives the mutual independence of the events $\iota_v=0$; the law of the sum of the numbers $\iota_v$, which follows from the joint law, is known: [BW, Theorem 1.3]; the joint law: not found in [Sh], [GL], [WY], [KY], [CM], [J05], [Kn98], [MV], [MM98], [BW], which were read in full (in [BW] the proof of that theorem for this sum is an induction with the two steps of the proof of this lemma [BW, pp. 180–181], and the article states the result for the sum only); not verified against [GS] outside its second and tenth sections, which were read and do not contain it |
| Theorem 2.6 | the law of the history forest | proved; (2.2) and (a): differs: [Sh, Theorem 4] gives the left side of (2.3) as the sum over the labeled forests of the product of $w$ at the vertex inversion numbers, and (2.3) follows from it by Lemma 2.5; the bijections of [Sh, Lemma 2 and Theorem 3], [GL, Theorem 3.4 and Remark 3.5] and [KY, Section 2.4] carry the displacement of a car to the number of inversions at a vertex; (b) and (c): differs: [MV, Section 4.1 with Note 1, p. 38] states that, given the sizes of the components that receive the cars, the displacements are independent and uniform; the identification of these sizes with the fringe subtree sizes of a uniform forest was not found in [MV], [Sh], [GL], [KY], [CM], [WY], [FPV], [Kn98], [J01], [J05], [Pet], [CEHHMPU], [CL], which were read in full (in [CL] the displacement of a car does not occur); not verified against [Kn3] |
| Corollary 2.7 | the maximum displacement | proved; special case known: for $k=1$ the right side of (2.4), $\mathbb E\prod_v1/t_v=n!/p_n$, is in [MM98, Section 5] in the form $n!/n^n$ for the tree with its root (Section 3.1), and the left side is the proportion of permutations; for $k\ge2$ not found in [CEHHMPU], [Pet], [MV], [Sh], [GL], [KY], [CM], [WY], [FPV], [Kn98], [J01], [J05], [CL], which were read in full: none of them states (2.4) or describes the law of $M$ as that of $\max_vD_v$; the nearest statement is the fragmentation of an almost full table in [Pet, p. 458]; the value $b_{n,n-1}=n^{n-2}$ is known: [CEHHMPU, Example 2.8] |
| Theorem 2.8 | Poisson Galton–Watson trees | proved; known: [J12, Example 10.2, p. 131, with (10.10)]; for mean $1$ a remark of Aldous [Ald, p. 242]; for every mean in $(0,1]$, [P99, Section 4.2], where it is called an observation of Aldous; also [CM, pp. 5–6] |
| Theorem 2.9 | the blocks at an intermediate time | proved; known: for the blocks of linear probing the law is [J01, Lemma 4.1], quoted in [J05, Lemma 3.1] and in [J12, Example 12.6, p. 146] and stated without proof in [Pet, p. 459]; the count of the lists that leave a given set occupied is [KY, Proposition 2.5 in the numbering of the arXiv version] and the case of unit masses of [MV, Theorem 2.6]; the identification with the tree sizes of a uniform forest is [CL, Section 5.1]; Pavlov's representation of those tree sizes is [P99, Proposition 7 and Section 4.1] |
| Theorem 2.10 | additive coalescent | proved; known: the merging rule is [CL, Section 1.4, (1.5)] and the identification with the discrete additive coalescent is [CL, Section 8], both in the running text there; for the model of aggregating servers on a cycle, of which the present process is the case of equal rates, the additive rule for the sizes is [Ber, Proposition 2(ii)]; the coalescent of forests is [AP, Lemma 1] and [P99, Theorem 5 with (10)]; the statement of [CL] is recalled in [MV, Remark 1.13], where the law of the process of the multiset of sizes is shown to be the same for a class of rules of displacement [MV, Theorem 2.7] |
| Proposition 2.11 | last-car recursion and exponential formula | proved; known: the recursion (2.7) for the threshold weight is [AF, Theorem 3.7], and for the weight $[t]_q/t$ of the total displacement [FPV, p. 495] and [Kn98, (2.1)]; the equation (2.8) for an arbitrary weight is [CGG, Theorems 4.1 and 4.4] |
| Proposition 2.12 |  | proved; the closed formulas quoted in (b) and in the paragraph after the proof are cited |
| Proposition 2.13 | spare spots | proved; known: the count is [KY, Theorem 2.2 in the numbering of the arXiv version, where it is said to be adapted from Pitman and Stanley], [Kn98, p. 562], [J01, (1.3)] and [AF, (1)]; special case known: for the weight $w(d)=x^d$ the identity is [Kn98, (1.3) and (1.5)], [FPV, p. 504] and [KY, Theorem 2.7 with (2.14)]; differs: for the threshold weight the left side is the number of $\ell$-interval rational parking functions, for which [AF, Theorem 3.8] gives a recursion and not this identity; for a general weight the identity was not found in these articles, which were read in full |
| Remark 2.14 | the forest of the family with spare spots | proved; the model is known: it is that of Moon as presented in [P99, Section 4.2, (18)], in its rooted form; the identification of the forest of the family with spare spots with it is not stated in [P99], which was read in full, and was not looked for elsewhere |
| Proposition 2.15 |  | proved; differs: [MV, (4.38), in the proof of Lemma 4.10, p. 49] gives the exact law of the size of the component that receives the car arriving at a given time, from which the formula follows by one summation with Lemma 2.2, and does not write the law of the displacement; [J05] prints the three statements from which the formula follows at once, (34) on p. 195, (4) on p. 181 and Lemma 3.1, and states as theorems the limit of this law for a load below one [J05, Theorem 4.5] and the exact law of the displacement of a uniformly chosen key [J05, Theorem 7.2]; the formula for the car that arrives at a given time is not printed there; for the last car its two pieces are in [Pet, p. 458], not combined; [CL, p. 12, with (2.6)] gives the law of the size of the block that contains a given place after a given number of cars, from which the formula follows by one step that the article does not take, and the displacement of a car does not occur in that article, which was read in full |
| Theorem 2.16 | Petersson | proved; cited: [Pet, Theorem 5.1], with the limit variable described by [Pet, Theorem 4.7], which is [JP, Corollary 2.4]; transferred to parking functions by Theorem 2.1 |
| Lemma 3.1 | cancellation of the exponentials | proved; elementary (a multinomial count); it is a step of the proof of Theorem 3.2 and was not looked for in the literature as a separate statement |
| Theorem 3.2 | pattern formula | proved; special case known: for the patterns, with all nodes of type descendant, that have one node besides the root, or two such nodes of which the second is a proper descendant of the first or has a subtree disjoint from that of the first, (3.1) is the Poisson case of the exact formulas of [J16, Lemma 5.1, and Lemma 6.1 with (6.7) and (6.8)], which hold for every critical offspring law with finite variance; related exact formulas, for several fringe subtrees of prescribed shapes, or of one common size, in a plane tree with prescribed degrees, are [BHJ, Lemma 3.2] and [BHJ2, (5.4)]; for one node besides the root, the proof of [Ald, Lemma 9, pp. 241–242] has an exact product formula for the joint law of the fringe subtree at a uniform vertex and of the rest of the tree, valid for every critical offspring law under the hypotheses of that lemma, by which the two pieces are independent given their sizes; that formula prescribes the fringe subtree as a tree and not by its size, has the number of leaves of the rest as a factor, and is not evaluated there for Poisson offspring; the general statement, and part (b) for patterns with more than one node besides the root, were not found in [J16], [HJ], [J12], [P99], [Pa], [J06], [ABH], [AP], [BHJ], [BHJ2], [KV], [Wa12], [Wa], [Ald], which were read in full, nor in the chapters of [CSP] read; see the paragraph after Proposition 3.4 |
| Corollary 3.3 |  | proved; (a) the first count is known: [J16, Lemma 5.1], [KV, Corollary 2.3], and for every simply generated family [Pa, (13)–(14)] and [KuP, (16)]; the second count, that of the trees of the forest, is the case of a node of type child and was not looked for as a separate statement; (b) special case known: two vertices, [J16, Lemma 6.1 with (6.7)]; (c) known: [MM78, (5.2)], [AP2, (107)], and [CSP, Exercise 10.1.4 with (9.5)]; (d) known: [P01, Section 4.1] |
| Proposition 3.4 | depth of a vertex in a forest with $q$ trees | proved; special case known: the case $q=1$ is Corollary 3.3(c), which is known; for $q\ge2$ not found in [J12], [P99], [ABH], [J06], [Pa], [AP], [Ald], which were read in full, nor in [CSP] in the chapters read |
| Theorem 3.5 | skeleton expansion | proved; not found in [J16], [HJ], [FK], [FFK], [MM98], [ABH], [Wa12], [Wa], which were read in full with this statement; the sources [J12], [P99], [Pa], [J06] and the chapters of [CSP] read are not counted among these: they were read in full with other statements of this paper and not with this one, and the record of those readings shows only that no weight on fringe subtree sizes occurs in them; not verified against the literature on hook length formulas [CGG], [KP], [JY], read in part |
| Lemma 3.6 | Stirling's formula with a remainder | proved, from Stirling's formula $m!\sim\sqrt{2\pi m}\,(m/e)^m$, for which see [FS, (32), p. 37, with a proof in Appendix B.6] |
| Proposition 3.7 | the number of cars near the top | proved; differs: [Pet, Corollary 4.10] gives the mean $\frac{4}{3\sqrt{2\pi}}z^{3/2}$ of the limit variable in the window $q=zn^{2/3}$, which is the constant of the asymptotic formula here, without a statement on the convergence of the means; an exact formula or a bound for finite $n$ was not found in [Pet], [J01], [J05], [FPV], [Kn98], [KY], [CM], [MV], which were read in full |
| Proposition 3.8 | the chain of the largest fringe subtrees | proved; differs: the sources have the continuum counterpart, a stable subordinator of index $\frac12$, for the largest block [Pet, Theorem 3.2], for the block of the first car [CL, Theorem 1.8] and for the fragment containing a uniform point [Ber2, Proposition 11], and for a finite table only the law of the first split [Pet, p. 458] and the law, at a fixed time, of the size of the block of a given car [CL, (2.6)]; the finite statement was not found in [Pet], [J01], [Ber], [Ber2], [AP], [Mie], [ABH], [J16], [J12], [MV], [BHJ2], [CL], which were read in full, nor in the chapters of [CSP] read; not verified against [CD], on large fringe subtrees of conditioned Galton–Watson trees, which was not read |
| Remark 3.9 | the published proof of the limit law and its finite counterparts | the statements attributed to [Pet] are cited; the displayed identity is (2.4), proved; the comparison of the two conditional probabilities is proved in the remark |
| Proposition 3.10 |  | proved; the two moments themselves are known: [FPV, Theorem 4 with $m=n+1$] and [Kn98, (5.4), (5.5)]; a decomposition of the mean and of the variance given the sizes of the receiving components is in [MV, Section 4, pp. 41 and 47 to 51]; their expression through the path length of the forest was not found in [FPV], [Kn98], [J01], [KY], [CM], [MV], which were read in full; the exact values for $n\le7$ were also checked by enumeration, Appendix B, item C10 |
| Proposition 4.1 | attaching a subtree | proved; elementary; not looked for as a separate statement; not found in [Pa], [P99], [ABH], [J06], [J12] and the chapters of [CSP] read, in which no weight on fringe subtree sizes occurs; not verified against [CGG], [KP], [JY], which treat products over hook lengths and were read in part |
| Theorem 4.2 | weights on fringe subtree sizes compatible with the deletion of uniform edges | proved; differs: the theorems of the literature characterise weights on the numbers of children, [Pa, Lemma 1] and [BP, Corollary 3]; for the constant weight conditions (ii) and (iii) are known, in a stronger form, [P99, Theorem 4] with [CSP, p. 220]; for the weight $c/s$ condition (ii) is known in the form in which the pieces are described given their sizes: it is the splitting property of random recursive trees, [BB, Proposition 1], attributed there to [MM], which could not be obtained; the equivalence for weights on fringe subtree sizes was not found in [Pa], [FKP], [BB], [PP], [KuP], [J06], [P99], [ABH], [AP], [HJ], [J12], [BP], which were read in full, nor in the chapters of [CSP] read; the last assertion, on the threshold weight, is proved in the paragraph that follows the two proofs and was checked by direct computation for $k\le5$, Appendix B, item C4; see the paragraph after the proofs |
| Theorem 4.3 | refinement of conditioned compositions one cut at a time | proved; differs: [BP, Theorem 2] is the corresponding equivalence for partitions of a set, with a larger family of weights; [Mie], [Ber] and [ABH, Theorem 1.3] treat ordered or cyclic versions with other rules; the rule on a segment and its converse were not found in [BP], [Mie], [Ber], [Ber2], [P99], [ABH], [AP], [J12], [MV], [CL], which were read in full, nor in the chapters of [CSP] read; see the paragraph after Lemma 4.5 |
| Lemma 4.4 | the number of parts under $\mathbb P_1$ | proved; the law of $Q$ is known: it is the law of the number of cyclic points of a uniform random mapping, [P99, (21)], attributed there to Harris, and [CSP, Example 1.9 with (9.5)], and of one plus the depth of a uniform vertex of a uniform rooted tree (Corollary 3.3(c)); the moment identities and the bounds for $D_m$ are computed here |
| Lemma 4.5 | comparison | proved; a consequence of Theorem 4.3 and Lemma 4.4; not looked for in the literature as a separate statement |
| Proposition 5.1 | curvature and loss | proved; not looked for as a separate statement; not found in [CEHHMPU], [Pet] and [AF], which were read in full; none of them builds a forest from the parking process ([Pet] recalls that hash tables correspond to random rooted forests and uses a theorem of Pavlov on the largest tree of such a forest, without constructing the correspondence) |
| Proposition 5.2 | loss formula | proved; not looked for as a separate statement; not found in [CEHHMPU], [Pet] and [AF], which were read in full; none of them builds a forest from the parking process ([Pet] recalls that hash tables correspond to random rooted forests and uses a theorem of Pavlov on the largest tree of such a forest, without constructing the correspondence) |
| Theorem 5.3 | increase far on the left | proved; Appendix A.1; not found in [CEHHMPU], [AF], [Pet], [J01], [J05], [Kn98], [KY], [CM], [FPV], [MV], [BEHLMMO], [CTT], [FHMS], [ABHJM], which were read in full; the search of Section 1.2, which is a search by titles and abstracts, returned no such result |
| Theorem 5.4 | decrease far on the right | proved; Appendix A.2; not found in the sources named in the bracket of Theorem 5.3, nor returned by the search of Section 1.2 |
| Remark 5.5 | the first two steps and the last step | proved, using the exact computation of the rows with $n\le300$, script `c13_rows.py`; the inequalities are those stated in [CEHHMPU, Section 6, before Conjecture 6.5] |
| Lemma 5.6 |  | proved; known in the weak form $a_ja_{j+2}\ge a_{j+1}^2$: [So, Section 6.1, p. 44] states as a known fact that $\bigl((n+1)^{n-1}/n!\bigr)_{n\ge0}$, which is the sequence $(a_j)_{j\ge1}$, is a Stieltjes moment sequence, and the Hankel minors of order $2$ of such a sequence are non-negative; the monotone ratio and the strict inequality are proved here |
| Proposition 5.7 | lattice condition | proved; the implication from log-convexity to the lattice condition is known, for these weights: [Lac, Proposition 3.2 and its proof, (3.13) and (3.14), pp. 8 and 9]; the converse was not found in [St], [Br94], [So], which were read in full; [CCP, after Lemma 4.2] asserts a converse for a related property without proof; not verified against the parts of [Lac] and [CCP] that were not read, nor against [Kar], [BuW], [Lig], which were not read |
| Proposition 5.8 | the first layer | proved; not looked for as a separate statement; not found in [CEHHMPU], [Pet] and [AF], which were read in full; none of them builds a forest from the parking process ([Pet] recalls that hash tables correspond to random rooted forests and uses a theorem of Pavlov on the largest tree of such a forest, without constructing the correspondence) |
| Theorem 5.9 | propagation of a decrease | proved; not found in the sources named in the bracket of Theorem 5.3, nor returned by the search of Section 1.2; the proof uses two finite exact computations, script `c08_propagation.py`: the rational numbers $\mathcal M(r,k)$ of Appendix A.3 for $2\le r\le255$, from $k=\lceil r^{3/2}\rceil$ up to the first positive value for each $r$, and the cross product at the $122$ positions with $\mathcal M(r,k)\le0$, all of which have $n\le102$; Appendix A.3 |
| Corollary 5.10 | reduction | proved; it uses Theorem 5.9, whose proof uses the finite computations of the script `c08_propagation.py`; the label is that of Theorem 5.9 |
| Conjecture 5.11 | Target T | conjecture; the grounds are stated after the corollary that follows this statement |
| Conjecture 5.12 |  | conjecture; the grounds follow |
| Corollary 5.13 |  | proved; the label is that of Theorem 5.9 |
| Counterexample 5.14 | a fixed outcome | counterexample; Appendix B, item C9 |
| Counterexample 5.15 | another offspring law | counterexample; Appendix B, item C9 |
| Proposition 5.16 | the threshold weight: Riccati equation and integrating factor | proved; differs: for the weight $x^d$ of the total displacement the corresponding equations are [FPV, Lemma 2, (9)] and [Kn98, (2.14)]; the equation for the threshold weight was not found in [FPV], [Kn98], [KY], [CEHHMPU], [AF], which were read in full |
| Proposition 5.17 | street decomposition | proved; known: [GS, Section 10] for the decomposition; the count is the case $u=1$ of [GS, Corollary 10.3] |
| Proposition 5.18 |  | proved; not found in the two parts of [GS] that were read, its second and its tenth section, which contain the decomposition of Proposition 5.17 and not the forest of the present paper; the statement was not looked for in the other sources, and is not verified against the rest of [GS] |
| Proposition 5.19 | the dominant pole | proved; (b) known: it is the conclusion of the theorem on supercritical sequence schemas [FS, Theorem V.1] for $G=C_k$, whose hypotheses are verified in (a) and at the beginning of the proof of (b), and the proof given here is the proof of that theorem, from Pringsheim's theorem [FS, Theorem IV.6], the lemma on series with non-negative coefficients [FS, Lemma IV.1] and the expansion of the coefficients of a meromorphic function [FS, Theorem IV.10]; (a) and (c), with the value $C_k'(\rho_k)=k$: not found in [CEHHMPU], [AF], [FPV], [Kn98], [KY], [HKMNY], which were read in full; not verified against [FS] outside the pages read, which do not contain them |
| Proposition 5.20 | expansion over minimal violators | proved; checked against the recursion for $n\le14$, Appendix B, item C7; not found in [CEHHMPU], [AF], [CM], [KY], [J05], [Kn98], [FPV], which were read in full |
| Problem 6.1 | Target T | conjecture; this is Conjecture 5.11, and the grounds are those given in Section 5.6 |
| Problem 6.2 |  | conjecture; this is Conjecture 5.12, with the grounds given there |
| Problem 6.3 | the separating property | open; the facts on which the question rests are exact computations: Appendix B, item C14 |
| Problem 6.4 | a local form of the limit law | open; the implication stated at the end is proved |
| Problem 6.5 | the process conditioned to stay below the threshold | open |
| Problem 6.6 | a continuum limit of the refinement of Theorem 4.3 | open |
| Problem 6.7 | other threshold statistics | open; the case $j=1$ of the first question is proved below |

The claims of the running text on which later sections rely, with the status given at the place where each is made:

| place | claim | status |
|---|---|---|
| Section 1.3 | the probability that the maximum displacement is below a threshold is the expectation of a functional of the fringe subtree sizes of a conditioned Poisson Galton–Watson forest; the cumulative counts depend on the fringe subtrees below the threshold only through Cayley's coefficients | proved: Corollary 2.7 with Theorem 2.8; Theorem 3.5. That these facts account for the recurrence of the classical objects is an observation and is not claimed as a theorem |
| Section 1.2 | a sequence of distributions can converge to a unimodal limit without being unimodal | proved: by an example |
| Section 2.2, after Corollary 2.7 | every sum over the parking functions of a product of one function $w$ of the displacements equals $Z_n[g]$ with $g(t)=\frac1t\sum_{d<t}w(d)$ | proved: (2.3) |
| Section 2.3, after Theorem 2.8 | a Galton–Watson tree with Poisson offspring of any mean in $(0,1]$, conditioned to have $n+1$ vertices and labeled uniformly by $\{0,\dots,n\}$ with the label $0$ at its root, is, after the removal of its root, the uniform forest on $[n]$ | proved: (2.6) |
| Section 2.4 | the tree of [CL, Sections 5.1 and 8] is not the history forest | proved: by an example |
| Section 2.4 | $n-\tau$ has the law of the number of uniformly deleted edges of a uniform tree until all components have at most $k$ vertices | proved, with the identification of the two chains cited in Theorem 2.10 |
| Section 2.5, after Proposition 2.12 | the labelings of a fixed forest, counted by $q$ to the number of inversions, give $n!\prod_v[t_v]_q/t_v$ | proved: Lemma 2.5 |
| Section 2.5, after Proposition 2.13 | with spare spots the displacements are independent uniform marks on the fringe subtree sizes of a forest with weight $r$ for each tree; the family is the state of the original process before the last $r-1$ cars | proved: Proposition 2.13 |
| Section 3.1 | $\Pr(M<k)=\mathbb E\exp(\sum_iF_k(T_i))$ with the toll $\log\min(1,k/\lvert T\rvert)$, which violates the hypotheses of [J16, Theorems 1.5 and 1.13] | proved: (2.4); inspection of the hypotheses |
| Section 3.1 | the tilted law for an integer threshold is the law of the shape of the history forest, with uniform labels, of a parking function conditioned on $M<k$ | proved: Theorem 2.6(c) |
| Section 3.2, after Corollary 3.3 | $\mathrm{Ren}$ is the renewal sequence of the random walk with Borel steps | proved: Corollary 3.3(d) |
| Section 3.3, after Theorem 3.5 | $Z_n(k')$ is given by (3.2) for every $k'\ge k$; a step is obtained from (3.2) with $k-1$; for $n\le2k+1$ the sum runs over compositions | proved: Theorem 3.5 |
| Section 3.4 | $\sum_{j\le q}\mathrm{Ren}(j)$ is of order $\sqrt q$ | proved: Lemma 3.6 |
| Section 3.5, after Proposition 3.10 | the first term of the variance is at most of order $n^{5/2}$; $\operatorname{Var}\mathsf T\sim\frac{10-3\pi}{24}n^3$ | proved; proved, cited from [FPV] |
| Section 4.1 | the Boltzmann model of rooted labeled trees at the parameter $\lambda e^{-\lambda}$ is the Poisson($\lambda$) Galton–Watson tree; at $1/e$ its size has the law $\mathrm{Bor}$ | proved: Lemma 1.1 and Theorem 2.8 |
| Section 4.1 | the block sizes after $t$ cars, in uniformly random order, have the law of the block sizes of the microcanonical Gibbs partition with the weights $j^{j-1}$, and in time they form the additive Marcus–Lushnikov coalescent | proved: Theorems 2.9 and 2.10, restated |
| Section 4.3 | for $n\le2k+1$ the threshold weight induces on the cut sets of a segment a law with a Cayley coefficient for each gap and a factor for each cut point | proved: Theorem 3.5 |
| Section 4.3 | on the circle, the composition read from the spot that stays empty to the end does not have the product law | counterexample: Appendix B, item C15 |
| Section 4.3 | the rule of the parking process on the circle is the case of equal rates of the model of [Ber] | proved: comparison of the two definitions |
| Section 5.3 | raising the factors of the cut points makes the cut set stochastically larger | proved: Lemma 5.6, Proposition 5.7 and the FKG inequality |
| Section 5.3 | a kernel $(n,t)\mapsto a_{n-t}$ is totally positive of order $2$ exactly when $(a_j)$ is log-concave; the kernels built from Cayley's coefficients or from the Borel masses are not | proved |
| Section 5.6 | the rows with $n\le300$ are unimodal and have no zero step; the same for $n\le600$ | computational evidence: Appendix B, items C5 and C14 |
| Section 5.6 | Target T implies Conjecture P | proved, using the rows with $n\le300$ and, through Theorem 5.9, the computations of `c08_propagation.py` |
| Section 5.6 | the inequality of Target T holds in every row with $95\le n\le600$ and fails in $81$ rows with $n\le94$ | computational evidence: items C5 and C14 |
| Section 5.6 | the cross product is positive at all $15{,}252$ first-layer positions with $n\le250$ | computational evidence: item C5 |
| Section 5.6 | Conjecture 5.12 together with the non-decrease of the rows $n\ge15$ on their left halves implies Conjecture P | proved, using the rows with $n\le14$ |
| Section 5.7, items 1 to 4 | the rows are not log-concave for $80\le n\le300$; the ratio of curvature to boundary is not log-concave at $n=172$; two models with the same representation have rows that are not unimodal; no violation in the two proved regions for $n\le300$ | counterexample; counterexample; counterexample (items C9, C14); computational evidence |
| Section 5.8, after Proposition 5.19 | $Z_n(k)\sim n!\,\rho_k^{-n-1}/k$ for fixed $k$; the prime pieces form a renewal process after the change of variable $x\mapsto\rho_kx$ | proved: Proposition 5.19(b) |
| Section 6.1 | given Theorem 5.3, Target T is equivalent to the conjunction of its three parts | proved |
| Section 6.3 | the table of the rows of the family with spare spots that are not unimodal, $1\le r\le12$, $1\le m\le600$ | computational evidence and counterexamples: item C14 |

## Bibliography

Theorem, equation and page numbers in the citations are those of the version named in each entry. Each entry says how far the source was read for this paper: "read in full" means every page of an article, or, for a book, every page of the chapters named; "read in part" is followed by the pages or sections read; "not read" is followed by what the citation rests on. The file `attributions-paper.md` that accompanies the paper records for every citation the passage on which it rests, and `literature-adjacent.md` the readings on which the literature labels rest.

[ABHJM] B. A. Ager-Hart, M. Beerbower, P. E. Harris, J. Jakovleski and M. McClinton, Counting $\ell$-interval Fubini rankings through their parking outcome, arXiv:2603.00693, version 1 (2026), 21 pages. Read in full.
